Stabilization distance bounds from link Floer homology
András Juhász, Ian Zemke

Abstract
. We consider the set of connected surfaces in the 4-ball with boundary a fixed knot in the 3-sphere. We define the stabilization distance between two surfaces as the minimal g g such that we can get from one to the other using stabilizations and destabilizations through surfaces of genus at most g g . Similarly, we consider a double point distance between two surfaces of the same genus, which is the minimum over all regular homotopies connecting the two surfaces of the maximal number of double points appearing in the homotopy. To many of the concordance invariants defined using Heegaard Floer homology, we construct an analogous invariant for a pair of surfaces. We show that these give lower bounds on the stabilization distance and the double point distance. We compute our invariants for some pairs of deform-spun slice disks by proving a trace formula on the full infinity knot Floer complex, and by determining the action on knot Floer homology of an automorphism of the connected sum of a knot with itself that swaps the two summands. We use our invariants to find pairs of slice disks with arbitrarily large distance with respect to many of the metrics we consider in this paper. We also answer a slice disk analogue of Problem 1.105 (B) from Kirby’s problem list by showing the existence of non-0-cobordant slice disks.



\DefineSimpleKey

bibmyurl

2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
57R58; 57R40; 57R42; 57Q45

1.
Introduction

There is a natural stabilization operation on smooth, oriented surfaces in 4-manifolds
where one attaches an embedded 1-handle to the surface. This operation was recently considered by Baykur and Sunukjian
[
2
]
.
When the 1-handle is unknotted, this does not change the fundamental group of the surface complement.
They asked the following question: If two surfaces are topologically isotopic,
then do they become smoothly isotopic after a single unknotted 1-handle stabilization?
They verified this
for most known constructions of pairs of exotic surfaces, such as rim surgery. A related question is how many stabilizations are required to make a given pair of surfaces isotopic.

There is a parallel notion of stabilization for 4-manifolds. A classical result of Wall
[
51
]
states that if two smooth, simply-connected 4-manifolds
are homeomorphic, then they become diffeomorphic after taking connected sums with
some number of copies of
\mathbb{S}^{2}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}
. It is an open conjecture
whether a single copy of
\mathbb{S}^{2}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}
always suffices. Recently, Lin and Mukherjee
[
24
]
constructed a pair of surfaces-with-boundary in a punctured K3 that are topologically isotopic but not smoothly isotopic, and remain so after stabilizing their complements once with
\mathbb{S}^{2}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}
. They showed this using family Bauer–Furuta invariants.

In this paper, we construct invariants that provide lower bounds on the number of
1-handle stabilizations required to make two surfaces isotopic.
Using a generalization of 1-handle stabilization, we endow the set of smooth, connected, oriented,
and properly embedded surfaces in
B^{4}
with boundary a knot
K
with a type of metric
that we call the
stabilization distance
. The stabilization distance between two surfaces
bounds from below the number of 1-handle stabilizations required to make them isotopic.
Using the link Floer TQFT, we define several invariants of pairs of surfaces bounding
K
that
give lower bounds on the stabilization distance. We compute these invariants for certain pairs
of slice disks arising from deform-spinning, and observe they
often give non-trivial lower bounds. Furthermore, we give examples in Section
10
where these lower bounds can be arbitrarily large.

Throughout this paper, we work in the smooth category, and all manifolds
are assumed to be oriented, unless otherwise stated.

1.1.
Metric filtrations on the set of surfaces bounding a knot

In Definition
2.9
, we introduce a very general type of stabilization operation for surfaces in 4-manifolds
that extends the 1-handle stabilization considered by Baykur and Sunukjian.
Let
S
be a properly embedded surface in a 4-manifold
W
.
To obtain the stabilization of
S
, we choose a 4-ball
B\subseteq\Int(W)
such that
B\cap S

is a collection of disks that can be isotoped into
\partial B^{4}
relative to their boundaries.
In particular,
\partial B\cap S
is an unlink. We then replace
S\cap B

with a properly embedded surface
S_{0}\subseteq B
such that
\partial S_{0}=\partial B\cap S
.
Any two surfaces in the same relative homology class can be related by a finite sequence of
such stabilizations and destabilizations (in fact, 1-handle stabilizations suffice).

Let
K
be a knot in
\mathbb{S}^{3}
. We denote by
\Surf(K)
the set of isotopy classes of connected, properly embedded surfaces
in
B^{4}
with boundary
K
. In Definition
2.14
, we introduce the
stabilization distance

\mu_{\st}(S,S^{\prime})
of a pair of surfaces
S
,
S^{\prime}\in\Surf(K)

to be the minimum of

\max\{g(S_{1}),\dots,g(S_{k})\}

over sequences of connected, properly embedded surfaces
S_{1}
, …,
S_{k}
in
\Surf(K)

connecting
S
and
S^{\prime}
such that consecutive surfaces are related by a stabilization or a destabilization.
Furthermore, if
K
is slice and
S\in\Surf(K)
, then
we define the
destabilizing genus

g_{\dest}(S)
to be the stabilization distance of
S
from
the subset of slice disks.

We also define the
M-distance
function
M_{(S,S^{\prime})}\colon[0,2]\to\mathbb{R}^{\geq 0}

of a pair of surfaces
S
,
S^{\prime}\in\Surf(K)
, which is similar to the stabilization distance,
but where the stabilization operation is allowed to change
the ambient 4-manifold. Instead of changing the surface in a 4-ball,
one can glue in a pair
(X_{0},S_{0})
, where
\partial X_{0}=\mathbb{S}^{3}
and
\partial S_{0}
is an unlink,
and
b_{2}^{+}(X_{0})=b_{1}(X_{0})=0
.
The
M
-degree
of a pair
(W,S)
, where
W
is a compact 4-manifold and
S
is a
properly embedded surface, is defined in Section
7
. It is a function
on
[0,2]
that measures not only the genus of
S
,
but also a homological quantity depending on
[S]\in H_{2}(W)

and the intersection form
Q_{W}
of
W
.
The
M
-distance of a pair of surfaces
S
,
S^{\prime}\in\Surf(K)

minimizes the maximal
M
-degree along sequences
(W_{1},S_{1})\dots,(W_{n},S_{n})

connecting
(B^{4},S)
and
(B^{4},S^{\prime})
such that
(W_{i},S_{i})

and
(W_{i+1},S_{i+1})
are related by the above stabilization operation.

Another notion of distance we consider in this paper is the
cobordism distance
, which we denote
\mu_{\Cob}(S,S^{\prime})
. If
S
,
S^{\prime}\in\Surf(K)
, we set
\mu_{\Cob}(S,S^{\prime})
to be the minimal
g
such that there is a 5-dimensional cobordism
(I\times B^{4},Y)
, where
Y
is a smoothly and properly embedded, oriented 3-manifold-with-corners such that

\partial Y=-(\{0\}\times S)\cup(\{1\}\times S^{\prime})\cup(I\times K),

projection of
Y
to
I
is Morse, and such that each regular level set of
Y
is a surface such that the sum of the genera of its components is at most
g
. We say that
S
and
S^{\prime}
are
strictly
g
-cobordant
if
\mu_{\Cob}(S,S^{\prime})=g
. Compare this to the notion of
g
-cobordism
of 2-knots defined by Melvin
[
29
]
, where one requires every component of each level set to have genus at most
g
, and
g
-concordance
, where, in addition,
Y\approx I\times S^{2}
. Two surfaces are strictly
0
-cobordant if and only if they are
0
-cobordant.
In particular,
\mu_{\Cob}
defines an ultrametric on the set of
0
-cobordism classes of surfaces.
It is straightforward to see that

\mu_{\Cob}(S,S^{\prime})\leq\mu_{\st}(S,S^{\prime}).

Note that Sunukjian showed that there are infinitely many distinct
0
-concordance classes of 2-knots in
S^{4}

[
50
]
. Dai and Miller
[
5
]
improved this result to show that the 0-concordance monoid of 2-knots was infinitely generated.

For
g\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\Surf_{g}(K)
denote the subset of
\Surf(K)
consisting
of genus
g
surfaces. If
S
,
S^{\prime}\in\Surf_{g}(K)
, then they are regularly homotopic relative to
K
.
We define the
double point distance

\mu_{\Sing}(S,S^{\prime})
as
\widetilde{\mu}_{\Sing}(S,S^{\prime})+g
, where

\widetilde{\mu}_{\Sing}(S,S^{\prime})
is obtained by minimizing half the maximal number of double points
that appear during a regular homotopy from
S
to
S^{\prime}
; see Definition
6.2
.
When
g(S)\neq g(S^{\prime})
, we set
\mu_{\Sing}(S,S^{\prime})=\infty
.
Motivated by an earlier version of this paper, Singh
[
48
]
showed that

\mu_{\st}(S,S^{\prime})\leq\mu_{\Sing}(S,S^{\prime})+1

using techniques from Gabai’s proof
[
9
]
of the 4-dimensional light bulb theorem. It is an open problem whether the
+1
is necessary in the above formula.

Both
\mu_{\st}
and
\mu_{\Sing}
are
metric filtrations
on
\Surf(K)
;
i.e., they are nonnegative, symmetric, and satisfy the ultrametric inequality

\mu(S,S^{\prime\prime})\leq\max\{\,\mu(S,S^{\prime}),\mu(S^{\prime},S^{\prime\prime})\,\}

for any
S
,
S^{\prime}
,
S^{\prime\prime}\in\Surf(K)
; see Section
2.2
.
Furthermore,
M_{(S,S^{\prime})}(t)
is also a metric filtration for every
t\in[0,2]
.
If
S
,
S^{\prime}\in\Surf_{0}(K)
, then
\mu_{\st}(S,S^{\prime})=0
if and only if
S
and
S^{\prime}
are related
by genus zero stabilizations, hence
\Surf_{0}(K)/\{\text{2-knots}\}
is an ultrametric space.
However, in general,
\mu_{\st}(S,S)=g(S)
, and if
\mu_{\st}(S,S^{\prime})=g(S)=g(S^{\prime})
,
it is possible that
S
and
S^{\prime}
are not related by genus zero stabilizations.

1.2.
Lower bounds from Heegaard Floer homology

Our aim is to provide computable lower bounds on the stabilization distance, the double point distance, the destabilizing genus, and the cobordism distance using the link Floer TQFT of the second author
[
58
]
, which extends the TQFT of the
first author
[
23
]
from
\widehat{\mathit{HFL}}
to the full infinity complex
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{\infty}
.
If
\mathbb{K}=(K,w,z)
is a doubly-based knot in
\mathbb{S}^{3}
, then
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{K})
is a
\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}

filtered chain complex over the two-variable Laurent polynomial ring

\mathcal{R}^{\infty}:=\mathbb{F}_{2}[U,V,U^{-1},V^{-1}].

We give an overview of
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{K})
in Section
3.1
.
There is a variation, denoted
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(\mathbb{K})
, corresponding to the subspace
of
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{K})
in nonnegative bi-filtration, which is a module over the ring

\mathcal{R}^{-}:=\mathbb{F}_{2}[U,V].

Knot Floer homology has been used by many authors to provide numerical concordance invariants that provide deep geometric information. Important examples are the knot invariants
\tau
,
\nu
,
V_{k}
for
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
and
\Upsilon
, introduced by Ozsváth–Szabó
[
36
,
42
]
, Hom–Wu
[
15
]
, Rasmussen
[
44
]
,
and Ozsváth–Stipsicz–Szabó
[
33
]
, respectively.
Since
\tau
,
\nu
,
V_{k}
, and
\Upsilon
are concordance invariants, they
all vanish when the knot is slice.

It is well known that the knot invariants
\tau
,
\nu
, and
V_{k}
give lower bounds on the 4-ball genus
g_{4}(K)
:

\tau(K)\leq\nu(K)\leq g_{4}(K)\text{ and }V_{k}(K)\leq\left\lceil\frac{g_{4}(K)-k}{2}\right\rceil

whenever
k\leq g_{4}(K)
; see
[
36
]
*Theorem 1.1,

[
15
]
*Section 2, and
[
45
]
*Theorem 2.3.
The invariant
\Upsilon
also gives lower bounds on the 4-ball genus; see

[
33
]
*Theorem 1.11. The invariants
\tau
and
\Upsilon

satisfy more general versions of the genus bound involving surfaces
in negative definite 4-manifolds
W
with
b_{1}(W)=b_{2}^{+}(W)=0
; see

[
36
]
*Theorem 1.1 and

[
57
]
*Theorem 1.1.

In Section
4
, we show that, by mirroring their constructions,
we can define secondary versions of all of the above knot invariants for a pair of surfaces

S
,
S^{\prime}\in\Surf(K)
in
B^{4}
with boundary a knot
K
in
\mathbb{S}^{3}
.
We show that these give formally analogous lower bounds on the metric filtrations

\mu_{\st}
,
\mu_{\Sing}
, and
M
:

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
S
,
S^{\prime}\in\Surf(K)
. Then

\tau(S,S^{\prime})\leq\nu(S,S^{\prime})\leq\min\{\,\mu_{\st}(S,S^{\prime})\text{, }\mu_{\Sing}(S,S^{\prime})\,\}.

Furthermore,

\Upsilon_{(S,S^{\prime})}(t)\leq M_{(S,S^{\prime})}(t)

for every
t\in[0,2]
.
Finally, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

(1.1)

V_{k}(S,S^{\prime})\leq\left\lceil\frac{\mu_{\Sing}(S,S^{\prime})-k}{2}\right\rceil.

If
S
and
S^{\prime}
are disks, then Equation (
1.1
) also holds with
\mu_{\st}
in place of
\mu_{\Sing}
.

The bounds stated in Theorem
1.1
are proven separately throughout the paper;
see Theorems
5.13
,
5.14
,
6.7
,

6.14
, and
7.5
,
as well as Proposition
6.8
. In Section
4.7
, we introduce a novel
invariant
\kappa(S,S^{\prime})
that does not have an analogue for knots,
and which only gives a lower bound on
\mu_{\Sing}(S,S^{\prime})
; see Theorem
6.9
.

Using the link Floer TQFT, we also introduce another integer invariant

\mathcal{I}(S)
of a surface
S
which has
g(S)>0
and has boundary a slice knot
K
;
see Definition
5.6
. In Theorem
5.10
, we prove that
\mathcal{I}(S)
bounds the stabilization distance
between
S
and the subset of slice disks:

\mathcal{I}(S)\leq g_{\dest}(S).

However,
\mathcal{I}(S)
is in general difficult to compute,
as it involves determining the link cobordism maps for infinitely many
decorations on the surface
S
. Giving a lower bound on
\mathcal{I}(S)
is theoretically feasible,
since it only involves finding two decorations on
S
satisfying a
simple condition, whose induced cobordism maps disagree.
We define an analogue of
\kappa(S,S^{\prime})
for a single surface,
\kappa_{0}(S)
, which gives a computable
lower bound on
g_{\dest}(S)
; see Theorem
5.11
.
1
1

1

In subsequent work
[
19
]
, we show that
\mathcal{I}(S),\kappa_{0}(S)\in\{g(S),g(S)+1\}
, and hence
\mathcal{I}(S)
and
\kappa_{0}(S)
give potential obstructions to surfaces being stabilized (cf. Proposition 5.5 therein). By analyzing the proof of
[
19
]
*Theorem 1.7 and by considering cases where the bound is sharp, one may find surfaces where
\mathcal{I}(S)=g(S)+1
. For example, any genus 2 slice surface for
T_{2,3}\#T_{2,3}
has
\mathcal{I}(S)=3
.

The invariants
\tau
,
\kappa
, and
\kappa_{0}
can all be derived from
\Upsilon
.
As opposed to the case of knots,
\Upsilon_{(S,S^{\prime})}(t)
is not a symmetric function.
We will see in Theorem
4.20
that, for all
t\in[0,2]
sufficiently close to 0, we have

\Upsilon_{(S,S^{\prime})}(t)=\tau(S,S^{\prime})\cdot t.

However, for
t
sufficiently close to
2
, we have

\Upsilon_{(S,S^{\prime})}(t)=\begin{cases}(\kappa_{0}(S)-g(S))\cdot(2-t)+g(S)\cdot t&\text{ if }g(S)>g(S^{\prime}),\\
(\kappa(S,S^{\prime})-g(S))\cdot(2-t)+g(S)\cdot t&\text{ if }g(S)=g(S^{\prime}).\end{cases}

We now review the construction of the invariants.
If
S\in\Surf(K)
, then it can be viewed as a
link cobordism from
\emptyset
to
(\mathbb{S}^{3},K)
.
If we decorate it such that the type-
\bm{\mathbf{w}}
region is a bigon, then it induces a filtered chain map

\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{\infty}\colon\mathcal{R}^{\infty}\to\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{K}),

well-defined up to filtered chain homotopy; see Section
4.2
.
If instead the type-
\bm{\mathbf{z}}
region is a bigon, we obtain a map
\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{w}}}^{\infty}
.
We call
\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{\infty}
and
\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{w}}}^{\infty}
the
extremal principal invariants

of the surface
S
.

Given surfaces
S
,
S^{\prime}\in\Surf(K)
, the invariants

\tau(S,S^{\prime})\text{, }\nu(S,S^{\prime})\text{, }V_{k}(S,S^{\prime})\text{, and }\Upsilon_{S,S^{\prime}}(t)

are all extracted from the pair of maps
(\bm{\mathbf{t}}^{\infty}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}},\bm{\mathbf{t}}^{\infty}_{S^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}})

by algebraically mirroring the construction of their knot invariant counterparts.
Hence, we think of our invariants as
secondary
versions of the knot invariants.
The invariant
\kappa_{0}(S)
is derived from
\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{w}}}^{\infty}
, and we obtain

\kappa(S,S^{\prime})
from the pair
(\bm{\mathbf{t}}^{\infty}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{w}}},\bm{\mathbf{t}}^{\infty}_{S^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{w}}})
.

For example, to obtain
\tau
, we set
U=0
and
V=1
in the
\mathcal{R}
-module

\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(\mathbb{K})
, and obtain the
\mathbb{Z}
-filtered complex
\widehat{\mathit{CFK}}{}^{\mathit{fil},z}(\mathbb{K})
whose homology is
\widehat{\mathit{HF}}(\mathbb{S}^{3})\cong\mathbb{F}_{2}
.
Given a pair of surfaces
S
,
S^{\prime}\in\Surf(K)
,
the elements
\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)
and
\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)
of
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(\mathbb{K})
give rise to
elements
\widehat{t}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(1)
and
\widehat{t}_{S^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(1)
of
\widehat{\mathit{CFK}}{}^{\mathit{fil},z}(\mathbb{K})
. We define the invariant

\tau(S,S^{\prime}):=\min\{n\geq\max\{g,g^{\prime}\}:\left[\widehat{t}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(1)\right]=\left[\widehat{t}_{S^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(1)\right]\text{ as elements of }H_{*}(\widehat{\mathit{CFK}}{}^{\mathit{fil},z}_{n}(\mathbb{K}))\},

where
\widehat{\mathit{CFK}}{}^{\mathit{fil},z}_{n}(\mathbb{K})
is the part of
\widehat{\mathit{CFK}}{}^{\mathit{fil},z}(\mathbb{K})
in filtration at most
n
.

1.3.
Computing the invariants for deform-spun disks using the trace formula

In Section
10
, we compute the secondary invariants for
several pairs of deform-spun slice disks, using the computer algebra software
SageMath
[
46
]
. We exhibit several
examples where the lower bound on the stabilization distance is 2 or 3, and a family with arbitrarily large distance.
We note that, in our examples, the pairs of slice disks are
not

topologically isotopic relative to their boundaries.

Let
K
be a knot in
\mathbb{S}^{3}
, and suppose that the 3-ball
B
intersects
K
in an unknotted arc.
Then
(\mathbb{S}^{3}\setminus\Int(B),K\setminus\Int(B))
is a ball-arc pair
(B^{3},a)
.
Suppose that we are given an isotopy
\phi\colon I\times\mathbb{S}^{3}\to\mathbb{S}^{3}
of
\mathbb{S}^{3}
that is the identity on
B
, and such
that
\phi_{0}=\Id_{\mathbb{S}^{3}}
and
\phi_{1}(K)=K
, where
\phi_{t}(x):=\phi(t,x)
for every
t\in I
and
x\in\mathbb{S}^{3}
.
Then the
deform-spun
slice disk
D_{K,\phi}\subseteq B^{4}
is defined by taking

\bigcup_{t\in I}\{t\}\times\phi_{t}(a)\subseteq I\times B^{3},

and rounding the corners along
\{0,1\}\times\partial B^{3}
.
This is a slice disk of
-K\#K
. The isotopy class of
D_{K,\phi}
, relative to
-K\#K
,
only depends on the diffeomorphism
\phi_{1}
, so we will write
D_{K,\phi_{1}}
instead of
D_{K,\phi}
.

In this paper, we consider three automorphisms of a ball-arc pair.
The first is the
roll-spinning
automorphism
r
, which
corresponds to a positive Dehn twist in the longitudinal direction of the knot
K
.
The automorphism
r
is supported in a neighborhood of
K
.
The second is the
twist-spinning
automorphism
t
, which is similar to roll-spinning,
but instead twists in the meridional direction.
The third automorphism
\bm{\mathbf{R}}^{\pi}
that we consider is specific to
knots of the form
K\#K
. The
summand-swapping
automorphism
\bm{\mathbf{R}}^{\pi}
of
K\#K
is the
composition of an isometry of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
that swaps the two copies of
K
, and a
half Dehn twist that ensures
\bm{\mathbf{R}}^{\pi}
fixes
K\#K
pointwise.

We show that, if
K
is a knot in
\mathbb{S}^{3}
, then the roll-spun and twist-spun slice disks
D_{K,r}
,

D_{K,t}\in\Surf_{0}(-K\#K)
satisfy

\mu_{st}(D_{K,r},D_{K,t})\leq 2;

see Proposition
2.21
. We conjecture that the upper bound can be improved to
1
.

We obtain lower bounds on the stabilization distance and the double point distance using our secondary invariants.
For this end, we first compute the extremal principal invariant

\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{D_{K,{\phi_{1}}}}^{\infty}(1)\in\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{\infty}(-K\#K)
of a deform-spun slice disk.
We define the canonical cotrace map

\cotr\colon\mathcal{R}^{\infty}\to\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{\infty}(Y,\mathbb{L},\mathfrak{s})\otimes_{\mathcal{R}^{\infty}}\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{\infty}(-Y,-\mathbb{L},\mathfrak{s})

as the dual of the trace pairing.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
D_{K,\varphi}
be a slice disk of the doubly-based knot
-\mathbb{K}\#\mathbb{K}=(-K\#K,w,z)
, obtained by deform-spinning
a doubly-based knot
\mathbb{K}=(K,w,z)
in
\mathbb{S}^{3}
using an automorphism
\varphi
of
(\mathbb{S}^{3},K)
.
If we write
C:=\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{K})
, then

E\circ\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{D_{K,\varphi}}^{\infty}\simeq(\id\otimes\varphi_{*})\circ\cotr\in\Hom_{\mathcal{R}^{\infty}}(\mathcal{R}^{\infty},C^{\vee}\otimes C),

where
E\colon\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{\infty}(-\mathbb{K}\#\mathbb{K})\to C^{\vee}\otimes C
is the chain homotopy equivalence
induced by a pair-of-pants link cobordism.

Our proof of Theorem
1.2
uses a much more general
trace formula for the full link Floer TQFT, Theorem
9.3
,
extending a result from our earlier work
[
20
, Theorem 1.1]
for sutured Floer
homology, as well as a result of the second author for the graph TQFT

[
55
]
*Theorem 1.6.

Hence, to compute the secondary invariants of deform-spun slice disks, it
remains to determine the induced diffeomorphism map
\varphi_{*}
in specific examples.
The map
r_{*}
induced by the basepoint moving diffeomorphism is well-known,
and is given by the formula

r_{*}\simeq\id+\Phi\circ\Psi,

which was proven on
\mathit{HFK}^{-}(\mathbb{K})
by Sarkar
[
47
]
, and
extended to
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{K})
by the second author
[
54
]
. The maps

\Phi
and
\Psi
are two special endomorphisms of knot Floer homology, which
we describe in Section
3.2
. It turns out that
\tau(D_{K,\id},D_{K,r})\leq 1

for the canonical deform-spun slice disks
D_{K,\id}
and the roll-spun slice disks
D_{K,r}
;
see Proposition
10.1
.

To provide more interesting examples beyond roll-spinning, we compute a
formula for the map induced by the summand-swapping automorphism
\bm{\mathbf{R}}^{\pi}
on

\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{K}\#\mathbb{K})
in Theorem
8.2
. We prove
that there is a chain homotopy equivalence identifying

\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{K}\#\mathbb{K})
and
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{K})\otimes\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{K})
under which

\bm{\mathbf{R}}^{\pi}_{*}\simeq\Sw\circ\left(\id\otimes\id+(\Phi\circ\Psi)\otimes\id+\Phi\otimes\Psi\right),

where
\Sw
is the map that swaps the two tensor factors.
To our knowledge, after Sarkar’s formula, this is the only other known formula
for a mapping class group action on the knot Floer complexes of a family of knots in
\mathbb{S}^{3}
.

1.4.
Families with large distance

Using the trace formula and our invariants, we prove the following:

Theorem 1.3
.

Given
n\geq 0
, there is a knot
K_{n}
and a pair of slice disks
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
for
K_{n}
, such that
\tau(D_{1},D_{2})\geq n
. In particular
\omega(D_{1},D_{2})\geq n
for
\omega\in\{\mu_{\st},\mu_{\Sing},\mu_{\Cob}\}
.

The slice disks appearing in our proof of Theorem
1.3
are deform spun slice disks of

T_{p,q}\#T_{p,q}\#\bar{T}_{p,q}\#\bar{T}_{p,q}

for various
p
and
q
.

We note that the above results appeared after the work of Miller and Powell
[
30
]
, who constructed for each
n
a pair of slice disks such that any stabilization sequence between
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
required at least
n
stabilizations. Their result does not imply ours, since it focuses on the total number of stabilizations, as opposed to the maximal genus. Hence it does not give a lower bound on the cobordism distance. See also work of Miyazaki
[
32
]
.

Theorem
1.3
gives an answer to a slice disk analogue of Problem 1.105 (B) of Kirby’s Problem List
[
43
]
that asks whether every 2-knot is
0
-null-cobordant:

Corollary 1.4
.

For every
g
, there is a knot
K_{g}
and a pair of slice disks that are not strictly
g
-cobordant. In particular, there are slice disks that are not 0-cobordant in the sense of Melvin
[
29
]
.

Remark 1.5
.

We note that Dai, Mallick and Stoffregen have independently found examples of slice disks with large stabilization distance
[
4
]
. Additionally, they use some of the techniques of this paper in their work to study equivariant knots.

2.
The stabilization distance of a pair of surfaces

In this section, we first review deform-spun slice disks.
We then introduce the notion of a metric filtration,
which is a generalization of an ultrametric,
but where the distance of a point from itself can be nonzero.
We proceed to define the stabilization distance of a pair of surfaces with boundary a given knot, which
is an instance of a metric filtration. Finally, we show that the stabilization distance
of a 1-roll-spun and a 1-twist-spun slice disk is always at most two.

2.1.
Deform-spun slice disks

We review the definitions of deform-spun slice disks
[
21
, Section 3]
.
Originally, Litherland
[
26
]
introduced deform-spinning to construct
2-knots in
\mathbb{R}^{4}
, generalizing twist-spinning, due to Zeeman
[
52
]
,
and roll-spinning, due to Fox
[
6
]
. An analogous construction can be used to obtain
slice disks in
B^{4}
. The following is
[
21
, Definition 3.1]
:

Definition 2.1
.

Let
K
be a knot in
\mathbb{S}^{3}=-B^{3}\cup B^{3}
such that
K
intersects
-B^{3}
in an unknotted arc,
and write
a=K\cap B^{3}
.
Furthermore, let
\phi\colon I\times\mathbb{S}^{3}\to\mathbb{S}^{3}
be an isotopy of
\mathbb{S}^{3}
such that

\phi_{0}=\Id_{\mathbb{S}^{3}}
,
\phi_{t}|_{-B^{3}}=\Id_{-B^{3}}
for every
t\in I
, and
\phi_{1}(K)=K
.
The
deform-spun
slice disk
D_{K,\phi}\subseteq B^{4}
is defined by taking

\bigcup_{t\in I}\{t\}\times\phi_{t}(a)\subseteq I\times B^{3},

and rounding the corners along
\{0,1\}\times\partial B^{3}
.
The surface
D_{K,\phi}
is a slice disk for
-K\#K
, where
-K
stands for
(-\mathbb{S}^{3},-K)
.

Note that
\partial\mathbb{R}^{4}_{+}=\mathbb{R}^{3}=\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}\cup\mathbb{R}^{3}_{-}
.
Intuitively, we consider the arc
a
in
\mathbb{R}^{3}_{-}
, which we
rotate about the plane
\mathbb{R}^{2}=\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}\cap\mathbb{R}^{3}_{-}
in
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{+}
,
while applying the isotopy
\phi
, until we reach
\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}
.
The following result is
[
21
, Lemma 3.3]
, which immediately follows from
the work of Hatcher
[
11
]
.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
\varphi
be an automorphism of
(\mathbb{S}^{3},K)
such that
\varphi|_{-B^{3}}=\Id_{-B^{3}}
.
Then there is an isotopy
\phi\colon I\times\mathbb{S}^{3}\to\mathbb{S}^{3}

as in Definition
2.1
, such that
\phi_{1}=\varphi
. Furthermore,
the isotopy class of the deform-spun disk
D_{K,\phi}
only depends on
\varphi
,
which we denote by
D_{K,\varphi}
.

We now recall the definition of roll-spinning, based on the description of
Litherland
[
26
]
*Example 2.2. The following is
[
21
, Definition 3.5]
:

Definition 2.3
.

Let
K
be a knot in
\mathbb{S}^{3}
. Choose a tubular neighborhood
N(K)
of
K
as well as an identification

N(K)\approx K\times B^{2}
which induces the Seifert framing of
K
.
Let
X=\mathbb{S}^{3}\setminus\Int(N(K))
be the knot exterior.
Furthermore, let
\partial X\times I
be a collar of
\partial X
in
X
, with
\partial X\times\{0\}
be identified with
\partial X\subseteq X
.
We identify
K
with
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
. Choose a smooth monotonic function
\varphi\colon\mathbb{R}\to I

such that
\varphi(s)=0
for
s\leq 0
and
\varphi(s)=1
for
s\geq 1
.
We define the
rolling diffeomorphism

r\colon(\mathbb{S}^{3},K)\to(\mathbb{S}^{3},K)
by the formula

r(\bar{x},\bar{\theta},s)=\left(\overline{x+\varphi(s)},\bar{\theta},s\right)\text{ for }(\bar{x},\bar{\theta},s)\in K\times\partial B^{2}\times I\approx\partial X\times I,

and let
r(p)=p
for
p\in\mathbb{S}^{3}\setminus(\partial X\times I)
.

Similarly, we define the
twisting diffeomorphism

t\colon(\mathbb{S}^{3},K)\to(\mathbb{S}^{3},K)
by the formula

t(\bar{x},\bar{\theta},s)=\left(\overline{x},\overline{\theta+\varphi(s)},s\right)\text{ for }(\bar{x},\bar{\theta},s)\in K\times\partial B^{2}\times I\approx\partial X\times I,

and let
t(p)=p
for
p\in\mathbb{S}^{3}\setminus(\partial X\times I)
.

Let
B\subseteq N(K)
be an open ball that intersects
K
in an arc.
Then
(\mathbb{S}^{3}\setminus B,K\setminus B)
is diffeomorphic to a ball-arc pair

(B^{3},a)
, and
r|_{B}=\id_{B}
. We define the
(k,l)
-twist-roll-spin
of
K
to be
D_{K,t^{k}r^{l}}
.

Note that
D_{K,\id}
is simply the spun slice disk of
K
, obtained using the identity deformation.
We will call this the
canonical
slice disk of
-K\#K
.

2.2.
Metric filtrations

Definition 2.4
.

Let
X
be a set. We say that a function
\mu\colon X\times X\to\mathbb{R}^{\geq 0}

is a
metric filtration
on
X
if it is symmetric, and satisfies the
ultrametric inequality

\mu(x,x^{\prime\prime})\leq\max\{\mu(x,x^{\prime}),\mu(x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime})\}

for every
x
,
x^{\prime}
,
x^{\prime\prime}\in X
.

The metric filtrations appearing in the paper will all be instances of the following construction.

Example 2.5
.

Let
X
be a path-connected topological space, and
f\colon X\to\mathbb{R}^{\geq 0}
a continuous function.
Given points
x
,
x^{\prime}\in X
, we define

\mu(x,x^{\prime}):=\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\colon I\to X\\
\gamma(0)=x\text{, }\gamma(1)=x^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\max_{t\in I}(f\circ\gamma)(t),

where the infimum is taken over continuous paths
\gamma
. This is clearly a metric filtration.
Note that
\mu(x,x)=f(x)
.

Similarly, let
G=(V,E)
be a connected graph, and
f\colon V\to\mathbb{R}^{\geq 0}
a function.
For
x
,
x^{\prime}\in V
, we define
\mu(x,x^{\prime})
to be the infimum of

\max\{f(x_{1}),\dots,f(x_{n})\}

over paths
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
in
V
such that
x_{1}=x
and
x_{n}=x^{\prime}
.
This is a special case of the above construction: Set
X
to be the 1-complex
associated to
G
, and extend
f
over the 1-cells of
X
linearly. Typically we will be interested in graphs where
f
is integrally valued on
V
. For these graphs, we can replace the infimum with a minimum.

Definition 2.6
.

Let
\mu
be a metric filtration on the set
X
. Then we define its
normalization
as

\widetilde{\mu}(x,x^{\prime}):=\mu(x,x^{\prime})-\min\{\mu(x,x),\mu(x^{\prime},x^{\prime})\}.

Recall that
g\colon X\times X\to\mathbb{R}^{\geq 0}
is a pseudometric on
X
if it is symmetric, satisfies the triangle
inequality, and
g(x,x)=0
for every
x\in X
(but
g(x,y)=0
does not necessarily
imply that
x=y
).

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
\mu
be a metric filtration on the set
X
. Then its normalization
\widetilde{\mu}

is a pseudometric.

Proof.

Choose points
x
,
x^{\prime}
,
x^{\prime\prime}\in X
, and
write
a=\mu(x,x)
,
a^{\prime}=\mu(x^{\prime},x^{\prime})
, and
a^{\prime\prime}=\mu(x^{\prime\prime},x^{\prime\prime})
.
It is clear that
\widetilde{\mu}(x,x)=0
.
If we apply the ultrametric inequality to the triple
x
,
x^{\prime}
,
x

we obtain that
\mu(x,x^{\prime})\geq a
. Similarly, by considering the
triple
x^{\prime}
,
x
,
x^{\prime}
, we get that
\mu(x,x^{\prime})\geq a^{\prime}
, and hence

\mu(x,x^{\prime})\geq\max\{a,a^{\prime}\}
. In particular,
\widetilde{\mu}(x,x^{\prime})\geq 0
.

It remains to prove the triangle inequality

\widetilde{\mu}(x,x^{\prime\prime})\leq\widetilde{\mu}(x,x^{\prime})+\widetilde{\mu}(x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime}).

By definition,

\widetilde{\mu}(x,x^{\prime})=\mu(x,x^{\prime})-\min\{a,a^{\prime}\}\quad{and}\quad\widetilde{\mu}(x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime})=\mu(x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime})-\min\{a^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime}\}.

Without loss of generality, we can assume that

\max\{\mu(x,x^{\prime}),\mu(x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime})\}=\mu(x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime}).

Hence, by the ultrametric inequality,
\mu(x,x^{\prime\prime})\leq\mu(x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime})
.
So it suffices to show that

-\min\{a,a^{\prime\prime}\}\leq\mu(x,x^{\prime})-\min\{a,a^{\prime}\}-\min\{a^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime}\}.

We saw that
\max\{a,a^{\prime}\}\leq\mu(x,x^{\prime})
, hence we only need to prove that

\min\{a,a^{\prime}\}+\min\{a^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime}\}\leq\max\{a,a^{\prime}\}+\min\{a,a^{\prime\prime}\}.

This holds because
\max\{a,a^{\prime}\}
bounds both terms on the left-hand side from above,
while
\min\{a,a^{\prime\prime}\}
bounds at least one of them from above.
∎

2.3.
The stabilization distance

In this section, we describe a collection of topological numerical invariants associated
to pairs of surfaces bounding a knot.
Given a properly embedded surface
S
in a 4-manifold
W
, we describe
several ways of increasing the genus of
S
within
W
. Our description
is inspired by Baykur and Sunukjian
[
2
]
.
The most general notion, and the one we will focus on in this paper, is the following:

Definition 2.8
.

Let
S
be a connected and properly embedded surface in a 4-manifold
W
.
Suppose that
B^{4}\subseteq\Int(W)
is an embedded 4-ball such that
\partial B^{4}\cap S

is an unlink of
m
components, written as
U_{1}\cup\cdots\cup U_{m}
. Furthermore, suppose
that
S\cap B^{4}
is a collection of
m
pairwise disjoint and properly embedded disks

D_{1}\cup\cdots\cup D_{m}
that can simultaneously be smoothly isotoped into
\partial B^{4}
relative to their boundaries.
Let
S_{0}
be an oriented, connected, properly embedded genus
n
surface in
B^{4}
such
that
\partial S_{0}=U_{1}\cup\dots\cup U_{m}
. The surface

S^{\prime}:=(S\setminus\Int(B^{4}))\cup S_{0}

is the
(m,n)
-stabilization
of
S
along
(B^{4},S_{0})
.
We say the
genus
of an
(m,n)
-stabilization is
m+n-1
,
which we note is
g(S^{\prime})-g(S)
.

If
S^{\prime}
is the
(m,n)
-stabilization of
S
, then we say that
S
is the
(m,n)
-destabilization
of
S^{\prime}
. In this paper, a
stabilization
refers to an
(m,n)
-stabilization and a
destabilization
to an
(m,n)
-destabilization for some
m
and
n
.

A schematic of an
(m,n)
-stabilization is shown in Figure
2.1
.

Figure 2.1.
A
(3,2)
-stabilization.

Note that performing a
(1,0)
-stabilization is the same as taking the connected sum of a surface
S
with a 2-knot contained in a small 4-ball, disjoint from
S
. We additionally define the following special cases of stabilization:

Definition 2.9
.

Let
S
be a properly embedded surface in the 4-manifold
W
,
and suppose that
S^{\prime}
is obtained from
S
by an
(m,n)
-stabilization along
(B^{4},S_{0})
.

(1)

We say
S^{\prime}
is an
unknotted surface stabilization
of
S

if
m=1
and
S_{0}
is smoothly isotopic into
\partial B^{4}
relative to
\partial S_{0}
. If, furthermore,
n=1
, then we call this a
trivial stabilization
.

(2)

We say
S^{\prime}
is a
1-handle stabilization
of
S
if
(m,n)=(2,0)

and
S_{0}\cup D_{1}\cup D_{2}
is the boundary of a 3-dimensional 1-handle that is embedded in
W
.

Unknotted surface stabilization and 1-handle stabilization
have been introduced by Boyle
[
3
]
,
and studied further by Baykur and Sunukjian
[
2
]
.

Lemma 2.10
.

The surface
S^{\prime}
can be obtained from
S
by a genus
g
unknotted surface stabilization
if and only if it can be obtained by
g
disjoint trivial stabilizations.

Proof.

Suppose that
S^{\prime}
is obtained from
S
by an unknotted surface stabilization along
(B^{4},S_{0})
.
After isotoping
S_{0}
into
\mathbb{S}^{3}
, it becomes a Seifert surface of the unknot
\partial S_{0}
.
If
g>0
, the map

\pi_{1}(S_{0}\setminus N(\partial S_{0}))\to\pi_{1}(\mathbb{S}^{3}\setminus N(\partial S_{0}))\cong\mathbb{Z}

is not injective, hence
S_{0}
is compressible in
\mathbb{S}^{3}
by the loop theorem.
Compressing corresponds to reversing a 1-handle stabilization in
\mathbb{S}^{3}
.
If we push the interior of the 1-handle into
B^{4}
, it becomes unknotted.
By induction on the genus of
S_{0}
, we see that
S^{\prime}
can be obtained from
S
by
g
consecutive
trivial stabilizations. However, in dimension
4
, we can always isotope consecutive trivial stabilizations
to be disjoint from each other. The opposite implication is straightforward.
∎

As in
[
21
]
*Definition 3.8, we can define the
peripheral map

h_{S}\colon\pi_{1}(\partial W\setminus\partial S)\to\pi_{1}(W\setminus S)

for a properly embedded surface
S
in
W
. Given surfaces
S
and
S^{\prime}
in
W

with
\partial S=\partial S^{\prime}
,
we say that their peripheral maps are
equivalent
if there is an
isomorphism

g\colon\pi_{1}(W\setminus S)\to\pi_{1}(W\setminus S^{\prime})

such that
h_{S^{\prime}}=g\circ h_{S}
. The equivalence class of the peripheral map is clearly
an invariant of
S
up to ambient isotopy in
W
fixing
\partial W
pointwise.

Lemma 2.11
.

Suppose that
S^{\prime}
is obtained from
S
by a trivial stabilization.
Then their peripheral maps are equivalent.

Proof.

Boyle
[
3
, Lemma 11]
showed that
\pi_{1}(W\setminus S)\cong\pi_{1}(W\setminus S^{\prime})
.
Indeed, a trivial stabilization corresponds to taking the connected sum of
(W,S)
and
(\mathbb{S}^{4},T^{2})
,
where
T^{2}
is an unknotted torus. Since
\mathbb{S}^{4}\setminus T^{2}
is homotopy equivalent to
the suspension of
\mathbb{S}^{3}\setminus T^{2}
,
we have
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{S}^{4}\setminus T^{2})\cong\mathbb{Z}
, generated by the meridian of
T^{2}
.
Hence, the claim follows from the Seifert–van Kampen theorem.
As the connected sum is taken in the interior of
W
, it follows that the peripheral maps are equivalent.
∎

As shown by Boyle
[
3
, Lemma 9]
, a nontrivial 1-handle stabilization might change
the fundamental group of the surface complement, though it is always a quotient of
the original group. Based on his work,
Baykur and Sunukjian
[
2
, Lemma 3]
determined when two
1-handle stabilizations give isotopic surfaces. If the 1-handle
h
is attached along the points
a
,
b\in S
,
then we can act on the homotopy class of the core of
h
by either adding the class of the
meridian of
S
, or pre- or post-composing with the push-off of a loop in
\pi_{1}(S,a)

or
\pi_{1}(S,b)
. The equivalence class of the homotopy class of the core of
h

determines the resulting surface up to isotopy, also in the case when
\Sigma
has boundary.

Corollary 2.12
.

Let
D_{\varphi}
and
D_{\varphi^{\prime}}
be deform-spun slice disks of a knot
-K\#K
,
where
\varphi
and
\varphi^{\prime}
are non-isotopic automorphisms of
(\mathbb{S}^{3},K)
that are
fixed in a neighborhood of a point of
K
. Then one cannot obtain
D_{\varphi^{\prime}}

from
D_{\varphi}
by a sequence of trivial stabilizations and destabilizations (or, equivalently, by
unknotted surface stabilizations and destabilizations).

Proof.

According to the proof of
[
21
, Proposition 3.9]
, the peripheral maps
of
D_{\varphi}
and
D_{\varphi^{\prime}}
are inequivalent. Hence, the result follows from Lemma
2.11
.
∎

Proposition 2.13
.

Let
S
and
S^{\prime}
be compact, properly embedded surfaces in the compact 4-manifold
W

such that
\partial S=\partial S^{\prime}
and
[S]=[S^{\prime}]\in H_{2}(W,\partial S)
. Then
\Sigma
and
\Sigma^{\prime}
become ambient isotopic
relative to
\partial W
after finitely many 1-handle stabilizations.

Proof.

This is a relative version of
[
2
, Theorem 5]
,
and the proof is analogous. The idea is that one removes a neighborhood
of
S\cap S^{\prime}
, and chooses a relative Seifert manifold
M
for

(S\cup{-}S^{\prime})\setminus N(S\cap S^{\prime}).

Then a self-indexing Morse function on
M
with only index 1 and 2 critical points
that is minimal along
S
and maximal along
S^{\prime}
gives the required handles.
∎

Definition 2.14
.

Suppose that
S
and
S^{\prime}
are connected, properly embedded surfaces in the
compact 4-manifold
W
such that
\partial S=\partial S^{\prime}
is a knot
K
in
\partial W
.
We define the
stabilization distance
of the pair
(S,S^{\prime})
, for which we write

\mu_{\st}(S,S^{\prime})
, to be the minimum of

\max\{g(S_{1}),\dots,g(S_{k})\}

over sequences of connected, properly embedded surfaces
S_{1},\dots,S_{k}
in
W

such that

(1)

S_{1}=S
and
S_{k}=S^{\prime}
,

(2)

\partial S_{i}=K
for
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
, and

(3)

S_{i}
and
S_{i+1}
are related by a stabilization or destabilization, up to proper isotopy,
for
i\in\{1,\dots,k-1\}
.

We define
\mu_{\st}(S,S^{\prime})
to be
\infty
if no such sequence exists. Analogously, we define
\overline{\mu}_{\st}(S,S^{\prime})
by minimizing

\max\{g(S_{1}),\dots,g(S_{k})\}-\min\{g(S_{1}),\dots,g(S_{k})\}

over the same set of sequences. Finally, we let

\widetilde{\mu}_{\st}(S,S^{\prime})=\mu_{\st}(S,S^{\prime})-\min\{g(S),g(S^{\prime})\},

which we call the
normalized stabilization distance
.

The
(trivial) 1-handle distance
of
S
and
S^{\prime}
is defined similarly to
\mu_{\st}
,
except that
S_{i}
and
S_{i+1}
are related by adding or removing a (trivial) 1-handle.
We denote the 1-handle distance by
\mu_{1}(S,S^{\prime})
, and the trivial 1-handle distance by
\mu_{1}^{0}(S,S^{\prime})
.

We observe that

\widetilde{\mu}_{\st}(S,S^{\prime})\leq\overline{\mu}_{\st}(S,S^{\prime})\leq\mu_{\st}(S,S^{\prime})\leq\mu_{1}(S,S^{\prime})\leq\mu_{1}^{0}(S,S^{\prime}).

Furthermore, if
[S]=[S^{\prime}]
in
H_{2}(W,K)
,
then
\mu_{1}(S,S^{\prime})
is finite by Proposition
2.13
,
and hence so are
\mu_{\st}(S,S^{\prime})
and
\overline{\mu}_{\st}(S,S^{\prime})
.
On the other hand,
\mu_{1}^{0}(S,S^{\prime})
might be infinite by Corollary
2.12
.
Note that
\overline{\mu}_{\st}(S,S^{\prime})=0
if and only if
S
and
S^{\prime}
become
isotopic after taking connected sums with 2-knots. Since
\mu_{\st}(S,S)=g(S)
,
the normalized distance satisfies
\widetilde{\mu}_{\st}(S,S)=0
.

Consider the graph whose vertices are isotopy classes (rel. boundary) of connected surfaces in
W
with boundary the knot
K

in a fixed relative homology class in
H_{2}(W,K)
, and whose edges correspond to
(m,n)
-stabilization
for some
m
and
n
. If we apply the procedure outlined in Example
2.5

to the genus function, we obtain
\mu_{\st}
, which is hence a metric filtration in the sense
of Definition
2.4
.
Its normalization in the sense of Definition
2.6
is
\widetilde{\mu}_{\st}
.
So, as a special case of Lemma
2.7
, we obtain the following:

Lemma 2.15
.

Let
W
be a compact 4-manifold, and
K
a knot in
\partial W
.
The normalized stabilization distance
\widetilde{\mu}_{\st}
is a pseudometric
when restricted to surfaces in a given class in
H_{2}(W,K)
.

For a knot
K
in
\mathbb{S}^{3}
, let us write
\Surf(K)
for the set of isotopy classes of
connected, oriented, properly embedded surfaces in
B^{4}
with boundary
K
,
and
\Surf(K)/\{\text{2-knots}\}
for
\Surf(K)
modulo genus 0 stabilizations.
We denote by
\Surf_{g}(K)
the subset of genus
g
surfaces in
\Surf(K)
.
If
K
is slice, we will write

\mathcal{D}(K):=\Surf_{0}(K)

for the set of isotopy classes of slice disks of
K
in
B^{4}
,
and
\mathcal{D}(K)/\{\text{2-knots}\}
for
\mathcal{D}(K)
modulo genus 0 stabilizations.

Remark 2.16
.

Note that
(\Surf(K)/\{\text{2-knots}\},\widetilde{\mu}_{\st})
is a
pseudometric space

(i.e.,
\widetilde{\mu}_{\st}(S,S^{\prime})=0
can hold for
S\neq S^{\prime}
in
\Surf(K)/\{\text{2-knots}\}
),
while
(\Surf(K)/\{\text{2-knots}\},\overline{\mu}_{\st})
is a
metric space
.
Furthermore,
\mu_{\st}
is a metric filtration, so it satisfies the ultrametric inequality

\mu_{\st}(S,S^{\prime\prime})\leq\max\{\mu_{\st}(S,S^{\prime}),\mu_{\st}(S^{\prime},S^{\prime\prime})\}

for any
S
,
S^{\prime}
,
S^{\prime\prime}\in\Surf(K)
, and so does
\widetilde{\mu}_{\st}
when restricted
to
\Surf_{g}(K)
.

If one of
S
,
S^{\prime}\in\Surf(K)
is a disk,
and
S_{1},\dots,S_{k}
is a sequence of surfaces connecting
S
and
S^{\prime}
,
as in Definition
2.14
, then

\min\{g(S_{1}),\dots,g(S_{k})\}=0,

so
\mu_{\st}(S,S^{\prime})
and
\overline{\mu}_{\st}(S,S^{\prime})

are both obtained by minimizing
\max\{g(S_{1}),\dots,g(S_{k})\}
.
Hence
\mu_{\st}
and
\overline{\mu}_{\st}
agree on
\mathcal{D}(K)/\{\text{2-knots}\}
, and

(\mathcal{D}(K)/\{\text{2-knots}\},\mu_{\st})
is an
ultrametric space
;
i.e., a metric space that satisfies the ultrametric inequality.
Our invariants from Heegaard Floer homology naturally give bounds on
\mu_{\st}
,
hence we will not study
\overline{\mu}_{\st}
in the rest of this paper.

Definition 2.17
.

If
K
is a slice knot and
S\in\Surf(K)
,
we define the
destabilizing genus

g_{\dest}(S)
of
S

to be the minimum of

\max\{g(S_{1}),\dots,g(S_{n})\}

over sequences of properly embedded surfaces
S_{1},\dots,S_{n}
in
B^{4}
such that

(1)

S_{i+1}
is obtained from
S_{i}
via stabilization or destabilization for
i\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}
,

(2)

S_{1}=S
, and
S_{n}
is a slice disk of
K
.

By definition,
g_{\dest}(S)\geq g(S)
. Furthermore, if
D
is a slice disk of
K
,
then
g_{\dest}(S)\leq\widetilde{\mu}_{\st}(S,D)
, and hence
g_{\dest}(S)
is finite.
In fact,
g_{\dest}(S)
is the distance of
S
from
\mathcal{D}(K)/\{\text{2-knots}\}
in the pseudometric space

(\Surf(K)/\{\text{2-knots}\},\widetilde{\mu}_{\st})
.

Proposition 2.18
.

Let
S_{1}
,
S_{2}\in\Surf(K)
. Then

\mu_{\st}(S_{1},S_{2})\leq 2g(K)+\max\{g(S_{1}),g(S_{2})\},

where
g(K)
is the Seifert genus of
K
.

Proof.

Let
S
be a minimal genus Seifert surface for
K
, and choose an open ball
B\subseteq\Int(S)
.
Consider the product
S\times I\subseteq\mathbb{S}^{3}
, where we identify

S
with
S\times\{0\}
. For
i\in\{1,2\}
, isotope
S_{i}
near
\partial S_{i}
such that
it becomes a surface
S_{i}^{\prime}
with boundary
K\times\{1\}
. We let

\Sigma_{i}:=((S\setminus B)\times\{0\})\cup(\partial B\times I)\cup((S\setminus B)\times\{1\})\cup S_{i}^{\prime}.

Then
\Sigma_{i}
is a surface of genus
2g(K)+g(S_{i})
that can be obtained from
S_{i}

by
2g(K)
1-handle stabilizations in
\mathbb{S}^{3}
. Indeed, let
a_{1},\dots,a_{2g}
be pairwise disjoint arcs in
S\setminus B
with boundary on
\partial B
that span
H_{1}(S,B)
. If we compress
\Sigma_{i}
along the curves

(a_{i}\times\{0,1\})\cup(\partial a_{i}\times I)

using the compressing disks
a_{i}\times I\subseteq S\times I
, we obtain
S_{i}
, up to proper isotopy.

If we push
\Sigma_{i}\setminus(S\times\{0\})
into
\Int(B^{4})
relative to
\partial B\times\{0\}
,
we obtain a surface
\Sigma_{i}^{\prime}
that is a stabilization of
S
with
m=1
.
The sequence of surfaces
S_{1}
,
\Sigma_{1}^{\prime}
,
S
,
\Sigma_{2}^{\prime}
,
S_{2}
satisfies the requirements of
Definition
2.14
, and has maximal genus
2g(K)+\max\{g(S_{1}),g(S_{2})\}
.
∎

Corollary 2.19
.

If
K
is a slice knot and
S\in\Surf_{g}(K)
, then

g_{\dest}(S)\leq 2g(K)+g.

Proposition 2.20
.

Let
K
be a slice knot, and
S
,
S^{\prime}\in\Surf(K)
. Then

\widetilde{\mu}_{\st}(S,S^{\prime})\geq|g_{\dest}(S)-g_{\dest}(S^{\prime})|.

Proof.

Since the claim is symmetric in
S
and
S^{\prime}
, we can assume that
g_{\dest}(S)\leq g_{\dest}(S^{\prime})
.
Let
D
be a slice disk for
K
such that
\widetilde{\mu}_{\st}(D,S)=g_{\dest}(S)
.
Then

g_{\dest}(S)+\widetilde{\mu}_{\st}(S,S^{\prime})=\widetilde{\mu}_{\st}(D,S)+\widetilde{\mu}_{\st}(S,S^{\prime})\geq\widetilde{\mu}_{\st}(D,S^{\prime})\geq g_{\dest}(S^{\prime})

by the triangle inequality, and the result follows.
∎

2.4.
An upper bound on the distance between 1-roll-spun and 1-twist-spun slice disks

Let
t^{n}r^{m}
denote the
n
-twist-
m
-roll-spinning diffeomorphism of
K
.
We will show the following:

Proposition 2.21
.

If
K
is a knot in
\mathbb{S}^{3}
, then the deform-spun slice disks
D_{K,r}
,

D_{K,t}\in\Surf_{0}(-K\#K)
satisfy

\mu_{st}(D_{K,r},D_{K,t})\leq 2.

Proof.

Let
B_{0}\subseteq\mathbb{S}^{3}
denote a 3-ball that intersects
K
in an unknotted arc.
We consider the knotted ball-arc pair
(B,a)
, where
B:=\mathbb{S}^{3}\setminus\Int(B_{0})
and
a=K\cap B
.
We present both slice disks
D_{K,r}
and
D_{K,t}
as movies
of ball-arc pairs which start and end at
(B,a)
.

Figure 2.2.
The slice disks
D_{K,t^{\operatorname{wr}(\mathcal{D})}r}
and
D_{K,t}
of
-K\#K
. In the top row, we rotate the diagram counterclockwise a full turn in the plane, and consecutive frames differ by a small rotation.

We begin with describing a movie for
D_{K,t^{n}r}
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
We pick a diagram
\mathcal{D}
for
K
with
\operatorname{wr}(\mathcal{D})=n
. We view the diagram
\mathcal{D}
as nearly being
embedded in a plane
P
. The movie for
D_{K,t^{n}r}
consists of
rotating the diagram
\mathcal{D}
about an axis perpendicular to
P
(and shifting
along the axis perpendicular to
P
slightly), while translating
\mathcal{D}

in the plane so that the image of
K
intersects
B
in an unknotted arc.
This is a movie for
D_{K,t^{n}r}
for some
n
. The exponent
n

is equal to the difference between the blackboard framing and the Seifert
framing. Since the difference between the blackboard framing and the Seifert
framing is
\operatorname{wr}(\mathcal{D})
, it follows that this movie represents
D_{K,t^{\operatorname{wr}(\mathcal{D})}r}
.

Next, we describe a movie for the slice disk
D_{K,t}
. We pick a line

\ell
in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
which coincides with
K
inside
B_{0}
, and is disjoint
from
K
outside a small neighborhood of
B_{0}
. The movie for

D_{K,t}
is obtained by rotating
a
in a full twist about
\ell
.
Schematics of the movies for
D_{K,t^{\operatorname{wr}(\mathcal{D})}r}
and
D_{K,t}

are shown in Figure
2.2
.

We now present a stabilization sequence from
D_{K,t^{\operatorname{wr}(\mathcal{D})}r}
to

D_{K,t}
that has maximal genus two. Let us write
\{a_{s}:s\in I\}
for the movie
of arcs corresponding to
D_{K,t^{\operatorname{wr}(\mathcal{D})}r}
.
Suppose that
a_{s}=\phi_{s}(K)\cap B
for a 1-parameter family of rigid motions

\phi_{s}\colon\mathbb{S}^{3}\to\mathbb{S}^{3}
for
s\in I
that nearly preserve the plane
P
.
We give
K
a parametrization
\gamma(s)
such that
\phi_{s}(\gamma(s))
is the
center point of the ball
B_{0}=\mathbb{S}^{3}\setminus\Int(B)
(note that
B_{0}
is the
region inside the red ball in Figure
2.2
).

Figure 2.3.
Attaching a 1-handle to the disk
D_{K,t}
by adding a pair
of bands to the beginning of the movie for
D_{K,t}
.

Let
0<s_{1}<\dots<s_{n}<1
be the times such that
\gamma(s_{i})

is the lower point of a crossing of
\mathcal{D}
, which we denote
c_{i}
.
Let
\Cr(\mathcal{D})
be the set
\{c_{1},\dots,c_{n}\}
of crossings of
\mathcal{D}
.
As
s
passes
s_{i}
for some
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
,
the overstrand of the crossing
c_{i}
passes over
\partial B
in the movie
a_{s}
.

As a first step, we attach a 1-handle to
D_{K,t^{\operatorname{wr}(\mathcal{D})}r}
, by adding
two bands to the beginning of the movie
a_{s}
, as in Figure
2.3
.
We can move the second band to the end of the movie. This breaks each arc
a_{s}
into a knot
K_{s}

disjoint from
\partial B
, which we can view as a copy of our original knot
K
,
as well as a small, boundary-parallel arc attached to
\partial B
.
We now wish to continuously pull the family of knots
K_{s}
downward,
such that they do not pass over
\partial B
for any
s
(or, phrased another
way, such that there is a path from
\partial B
to
\infty\in\mathbb{S}^{3}

disjoint from
K_{s}
for all
s\in I
).

Given
\bm{\mathbf{c}}\subseteq\Cr(\mathcal{D})
, we let
S_{\bm{\mathbf{c}}}\in\Surf_{1}(-K\#K)

denote the genus one surface obtained by modifying the movie
\{a_{s}:s\in I\}

such that the upper strand of
c_{i}
passes over
\partial B
if
c_{i}\in\bm{\mathbf{c}}
, and the upper
strand of
c_{i}
passes under
\partial B
if
c_{i}\not\in\bm{\mathbf{c}}
; see Figure
2.4
.

Figure 2.4.
On the top row, we show a portion of the surface
S_{\bm{\mathbf{c}}}

when
c_{i}\in\bm{\mathbf{c}}
. The upper strand of a crossing
c_{i}

passes over
\partial B
. On the bottom row, we show a portion of the
movie for
S_{\bm{\mathbf{c}}}
when
c_{i}\not\in\bm{\mathbf{c}}
. The upper strand of
the crossing
c_{i}
passes underneath
\partial B
.

Let
c_{i}
and
c_{j}
be consecutive crossings in
\bm{\mathbf{c}}
of opposite sign.
We claim that
S_{\bm{\mathbf{c}}}
and
S_{\bm{\mathbf{c}}\setminus\{c_{i},c_{j}\}}

become isotopic after a single stabilization. The stabilization is
obtained by attaching a band connecting the upper strands of the crossings
c_{i}
and
c_{j}
,
followed by attaching the dual band; see Figure
2.5
.

Figure 2.5.
A movie for a common stabilization of
S_{\bm{\mathbf{c}}}
and

S_{\bm{\mathbf{c}}\setminus\{c_{i},c_{j}\}}
, when
c_{i}
and
c_{j}
are
consecutive crossings in
\bm{\mathbf{c}}
of opposite sign.
In the movie, a band is added between the
first and the second frames. An isotopy connects the second and
third frames. The third and fourth frames are related by attaching the dual band.

In the case that
\operatorname{wr}(\mathcal{D})=0
, the number of positive crossings is
equal to the number of negative crossings, so we can eliminate all
crossings from
\bm{\mathbf{c}}
pairwise via the stabilization sequence described above. Hence

\mu_{st}(S_{\Cr(\mathcal{D})},S_{\emptyset})\leq 2.

We note that
S_{\Cr(\mathcal{D})}
is a genus one stabilization of
D_{K,r}
,
while the surface
S_{\emptyset}
is described by a movie that
starts at
(B,a)
, then has a copy of
K
break off and move away
from
\partial B
. This copy of
K
rotates
\tw(\mathcal{D})
-many times near a plane far
away from
\partial B
, where
\tw(\mathcal{D})
denotes
the
twisting number
of
\mathcal{D}
, and then
K
is reattached
to the arc on
\partial B
via a band.

It is a general fact that
\tw(\mathcal{D})+\operatorname{wr}(\mathcal{D})
is always odd for
any diagram of a knot (as can be verified by noting that Reidemeister
moves and crossing changes do not change the quantity
\tw(\mathcal{D})+\operatorname{wr}(\mathcal{D})

modulo 2, and that
\tw+\operatorname{wr}=1
for a trivial diagram of an unknot).
Since we picked
\mathcal{D}
to satisfy
\operatorname{wr}(\mathcal{D})=0
, we conclude that

\tw(\mathcal{D})\equiv 1\pmod{2}.

Since a path of rotations about a line in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
induces the
generator of
\pi_{1}(\SO(3))\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, we can assume that the
copy of
K
which breaks off rotates in a plane exactly once.

Hence, our movie for
S_{\emptyset}
becomes a copy of
K
which
breaks off of
(B,a)
as in Figure
2.3
, then makes one
full rotation in a plane (away from
\partial B
), and is finally
reconnected to the arc attached to
\partial B
.
A continuous deformation transforms this final movie into a
stabilization of the movie for
D_{K,t}
shown in Figure
2.2
.
∎

We conclude this section with the following conjecture (compare Proposition
10.1
, below).

Conjecture 2.22
.

If
K
is a knot in
\mathbb{S}^{3}
, then the deform-spun slice disks
D_{K,r}
,

D_{K,t}\in\Surf_{0}(-K\#K)
satisfy

\mu_{st}(D_{K,r},D_{K,t})\leq 1.

3.
Background on the link Floer TQFT

In this section, we recall some previous results about the link Floer TQFT which we will need
to compute the effect of stabilization on the link cobordism maps in Section
5
,
to determine the map induced by the summand-swapping diffeomorphism in Section
8
,
and to prove the trace formula in Section
9
.

3.1.
The full link Floer TQFT

We first recall the category whose objects are multi-based links, and
whose morphisms are decorated link cobordisms, following the notation
of the second author
[
58
]
; see also the equivalent construction of the first author
[
23
]
.

Definition 3.1
.

A
multi-based link

\mathbb{L}=(L,\bm{\mathbf{w}},\bm{\mathbf{z}})
in a closed, oriented (not necessarily connected) 3-manifold
Y
is an oriented link

L\subseteq Y
, together with two disjoint collections of basepoints
\bm{\mathbf{w}}
,
\bm{\mathbf{z}}\subseteq L
such that

(1)

each component of
L
has at least two basepoints;

(2)

the basepoints along a link component of
L
alternate between
\bm{\mathbf{w}}
and
\bm{\mathbf{z}}
, as one traverses the link;

(3)

each component of
Y
has at least one component of
L
, and each component of
L
has at least two basepoints.

Definition 3.2
.

Let
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
be 3-manifolds containing multi-based links
\mathbb{L}_{1}=(L_{1},\bm{\mathbf{w}}_{1},\bm{\mathbf{z}}_{1})

and
\mathbb{L}_{2}=(L_{2},\bm{\mathbf{w}}_{2},\bm{\mathbf{z}}_{2})
, respectively. A
decorated link cobordism
from

(Y_{1},\mathbb{L}_{1})
to
(Y_{2},\mathbb{L}_{2})
is a pair
(W,\mathcal{F})=(W,(S,\mathcal{A}))
, where

(1)

W
is an oriented cobordism from
Y_{1}
to
Y_{2}
,

(2)

S
is a properly embedded, oriented surface in
W
with
\partial S=-L_{1}\cup L_{2}
, and

(3)

\mathcal{A}
is a properly embedded 1-manifold in
S

that divides
S
into two subsurfaces,
S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
and
S_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}
,
that meet along
\mathcal{A}
, such that
\bm{\mathbf{w}}_{1}
,
\bm{\mathbf{w}}_{2}\subseteq S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}

and
\bm{\mathbf{z}}_{1}
,
\bm{\mathbf{z}}_{2}\subseteq S_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}
.

Multi-based links and equivalence classes of decorated link cobordisms form a category.

The first author
[
23
]
showed that decorated link cobordisms induce functorial maps
on the hat version of link Floer homology.
The second author
[
58
]
extended this to the
full infinity
complex
, denoted
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{\infty}
, which is a minor variation of the
infinity complexes of Ozsváth–Szabó
[
37
]
and
Rasmussen
[
44
]
. We now review the construction of
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{\infty}
.

Let
\mathcal{R}^{-}
denote the ring
\mathbb{F}_{2}[U,V]
, and let
\mathcal{R}^{\infty}
denote the
ring
\mathbb{F}_{2}[U,V,U^{-1},V^{-1}]
, obtained by inverting
U
and
V
in
\mathcal{R}^{-}
.
Suppose that
\mathbb{L}=(L,\bm{\mathbf{w}},\bm{\mathbf{z}})
is a multi-based link in
Y
. Given a
multi-pointed diagram
(\Sigma,\bm{\mathbf{\alpha}},\bm{\mathbf{\beta}},\bm{\mathbf{w}},\bm{\mathbf{z}})
for
(Y,\mathbb{L})
(see

[
41
]
*Definition 3.1), the complex
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{\infty}(Y,\mathbb{L},\mathfrak{s})
is
the free module over
\mathcal{R}^{\infty}
generated by intersection points
\bm{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\beta}
with
\mathfrak{s}_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}(\bm{\mathbf{x}})=\mathfrak{s}
. Over
\mathbb{F}_{2}
, the generators are the monomials

U^{i}V^{j}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{x}},

where
i
,
j\in\mathbb{Z}
.

The module
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{\infty}(Y,\mathbb{L},\mathfrak{s})
has a filtration
\mathcal{G}
over

\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}
, where the subset
\mathcal{G}_{(n,m)}\subseteq\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{\infty}(Y,\mathbb{L},\mathfrak{s})

is generated over
\mathbb{F}_{2}
by monomials
U^{i}V^{j}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{x}}
with
i\geq n
and
j\geq m
.
We denote the
\mathcal{R}^{-}
-submodule
\mathcal{G}_{(0,0)}
by
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(Y,\mathbb{L},\mathfrak{s})
, and
call it the
full minus complex
. It is generated over
\mathbb{F}_{2}
by monomials
U^{i}V^{j}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{x}}
with
i
,
j\geq 0
.

There is a filtered,
\mathcal{R}^{\infty}
-equivariant endomorphism
\partial
of
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{\infty}(Y,\mathbb{L},\mathfrak{s})
,
defined by the formula

\partial\bm{\mathbf{x}}=\sum_{\bm{\mathbf{y}}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\beta}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\phi\in\pi_{2}(\bm{\mathbf{x}},\bm{\mathbf{y}})\\
\mu(\phi)=1\end{subarray}}\#\widehat{\mathcal{M}}(\phi)\cdot U^{n_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}(\phi)}V^{n_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(\phi)}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{y}},

which satisfies
\partial\circ\partial=0
.

When
[L]=0
in
H_{1}(Y)
and
\mathfrak{s}
is torsion,
the chain complex
(\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{\infty}(Y,\mathbb{L},\mathfrak{s}),\partial)
has several gradings.
Ozsváth and Szabó defined a homological grading and an Alexander
grading. It is convenient for our purposes to repackage their two gradings
into three gradings that satisfy a linear dependency. Namely, there are two
homological gradings,
\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
and
\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}
, and an Alexander grading

A
, which together satisfy

A=\frac{1}{2}(\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}-\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}).

Note that
V
is
+1
graded with respect to
A
, and
U
is
-1
graded.

When
\mathbb{K}=(K,w,z)
is a doubly-based knot in
\mathbb{S}^{3}
,
we will write
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{K})
for
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{3},\mathbb{K},\mathfrak{s}_{0})
, where
\mathfrak{s}_{0}
is the unique
\Spin^{c}
structure on
\mathbb{S}^{3}
.

The second author
[
58
]
constructed cobordism maps for the full knot
and link Floer complexes. Given a decorated link cobordism

(W,\mathcal{F})
from
(Y_{1},\mathbb{L}_{1})
to
(Y_{2},\mathbb{L}_{2})
and a
\Spin^{c}
structure
\mathfrak{s}\in\Spin^{c}(W)
,
there is an induced
\mathcal{R}^{\infty}
-equivariant, filtered chain map

F_{W,\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{s}}\colon\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{\infty}(Y_{1},\mathbb{L}_{1},\mathfrak{s}|_{Y_{1}})\to\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{\infty}(Y_{2},\mathbb{L}_{2},\mathfrak{s}|_{Y_{2}}),

well-defined up to filtered,
\mathcal{R}^{\infty}
-equivariant chain homotopy.

3.2.
Basepoint actions on link Floer homology

Let
\mathbb{L}=(L,\bm{\mathbf{w}},\bm{\mathbf{z}})
be a multi-based link in the 3-manifold
Y
, and fix
\mathfrak{s}\in\Spin^{c}(Y)
.
We recall that, for each
w\in\bm{\mathbf{w}}
and
z\in\bm{\mathbf{z}}
, there are distinguished endomorphisms
\Phi_{w}
and
\Psi_{z}
of
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{\infty}(Y,\mathbb{L},\mathfrak{s})
. If
\mathcal{H}=(\Sigma,\bm{\mathbf{\alpha}},\bm{\mathbf{\beta}},\bm{\mathbf{w}},\bm{\mathbf{z}})
is a
diagram for
(Y,\mathbb{L})
, then
\Phi_{w}
and
\Psi_{z}
can be defined via the formulas

(3.1)

\Phi_{w}(\bm{\mathbf{x}})=\sum_{\bm{\mathbf{y}}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\beta}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\phi\in\pi_{2}(\bm{\mathbf{x}},\bm{\mathbf{y}})\\
\mu(\phi)=1\end{subarray}}n_{w}(\phi)\#\widehat{\mathcal{M}}(\phi)\cdot U^{n_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}(\phi)-1}V^{n_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(\phi)}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{y}},

and

(3.2)

\Psi_{z}(\bm{\mathbf{x}})=\sum_{\bm{\mathbf{y}}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\beta}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\phi\in\pi_{2}(\bm{\mathbf{x}},\bm{\mathbf{y}})\\
\mu(\phi)=1\end{subarray}}n_{z}(\phi)\#\widehat{\mathcal{M}}(\phi)\cdot U^{n_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}(\phi)}V^{n_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(\phi)-1}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{y}}

for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\beta}
with
\mathfrak{s}_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}(\mathbf{x})=\mathfrak{s}
.
According to
[
56
]
*Lemma 4.1,
the endomorphisms
\Phi_{w}

and
\Psi_{z}
are the link cobordism maps induced by the two decorations of
(I\times Y,I\times L)
shown in Figure
3.1
.
When we are working with doubly-based knots, we will often write
\Phi
and
\Psi
for the maps
\Phi_{w}

and
\Psi_{z}
, respectively.

Figure 3.1.
The two decorated link cobordisms for
\Phi_{w}
and
\Psi_{z}
. The diagrams indicate the decorations on the surface
I\times L
. Here we denote
\bm{\mathbf{w}}
by solid basepoints, and
\bm{\mathbf{z}}
by open basepoints. The shaded regions denote
\Sigma_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
and the unshaded regions denote
\Sigma_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}
.

According to
[
58
]
*Lemma 4.9, the basepoint actions satisfy

(3.3)

\Phi_{w}^{2}\simeq\Psi_{z}^{2}\simeq 0.

Note that the dividing sets on the decorated link cobordisms corresponding to
\Phi_{w}^{2}
and
\Psi_{z}^{2}

both contain a closed curve that bounds a disk in either
S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
or
S_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}
.

3.3.
Quasi-stabilizations and basepoint moving maps

We now review the quasi-stabilization maps. Suppose
\mathbb{L}=(L,\bm{\mathbf{w}},\bm{\mathbf{z}})
is a
multi-based link in
Y
, and suppose that
w
and
z
are two new
basepoints contained in a single component of
L\setminus(\bm{\mathbf{w}}\cup\bm{\mathbf{z}})
. Let us assume
that
w
immediately follows
z
with respect to the orientation of
L
,
and write

\mathbb{L}_{w,z}^{+}:=(L,\bm{\mathbf{w}}\cup\{w\},\bm{\mathbf{z}}\cup\{z\}).

There are two
quasi-stabilization
maps

S_{w,z}^{+}\text{, }T_{w,z}^{+}\colon\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{\infty}(Y,\mathbb{L},\mathfrak{s})\to\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{\infty}(Y,\mathbb{L}^{+}_{w,z},\mathfrak{s}),

as well as two
quasi-destabilization
maps
S_{w,z}^{-}
and
T_{w,z}^{-}
, defined in the opposite direction.
If instead
z
follows
w
with respect to the orientation of
L
, then we
obtain similar maps
S_{z,w}^{\pm}
and
T_{z,w}^{\pm}
.

We briefly summarize the construction of the quasi-stabilization maps. See
[
28
]
*Section 6 and
[
58
]
*Section 4 for further details. Suppose

\mathcal{H}=(\Sigma,\bm{\mathbf{\alpha}},\bm{\mathbf{\beta}},\bm{\mathbf{w}},\bm{\mathbf{z}})
is a diagram for
(Y,\mathbb{L})
. Let
w^{\prime}
and
z^{\prime}

denote the basepoints adjacent to
w
and
z
on
\mathbb{L}
. There is a component

A
of
\Sigma\setminus\bm{\mathbf{\alpha}}
which contains
w^{\prime}
and
z^{\prime}
. We pick a simple
closed curve
\alpha_{s}\subseteq A
that cuts
A
into two components, one of
which contains
w^{\prime}
and the other
z^{\prime}
. We add another curve,
\beta_{0}
,
that bounds a small disk on
\Sigma
, which is cut into two bigons by

\alpha_{s}
, and is disjoint from the other
\bm{\mathbf{\alpha}}
curves. Inside one bigon, we
place
w
. In the other bigon, we place
z
. See Figure
3.2
.

Figure 3.2.
A quasi-stabilization of Heegaard diagrams. On the left, a local picture of the quasi-stabilization near
w
and
z
is shown. On the right, we have a complete example of a quasi-stabilization. The shaded region denotes
A
. Also shown are the points
w^{\prime}
and
z^{\prime}
.

We write
\theta^{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
and
\theta^{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}
for the higher
\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
- and

\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}
-graded intersection points of
\alpha_{s}\cap\beta_{0}
,
respectively. The maps
S_{w,z}^{+}
and
T_{w,z}^{+}
are defined via the
formulas

(3.4)

S_{w,z}^{+}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}):=\bm{\mathbf{x}}\times\theta^{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}\quad\text{and}\quad T_{w,z}^{+}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}):=\bm{\mathbf{x}}\times\theta^{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}

for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\beta}
, extended
\mathcal{R}^{\infty}
-equivariantly.
The quasi-destabilization maps are defined dually, via the equations

(3.5)

S_{w,z}^{-}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}\times\theta^{\bm{\mathbf{w}}})=0,\quad S_{w,z}^{-}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}\times\theta^{\bm{\mathbf{z}}})=\bm{\mathbf{x}},\quad T_{w,z}^{-}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}\times\theta^{\bm{\mathbf{w}}})=\bm{\mathbf{x}},\quad\text{and}\quad T_{w,z}^{-}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}\times\theta^{\bm{\mathbf{z}}})=0,

extended
\mathcal{R}^{\infty}
-equivariantly.
The decorations on
(I\times Y,I\times L)
inducing the
quasi-stabilization maps
S_{w,z}^{+}
,
S_{w,z}^{-}
,
T_{w,z}^{+}
, and

T_{w,z}^{-}
are shown in Figure
3.3
.

Figure 3.3.
The decorated link cobordisms for the quasi-stabilization maps.

We will need the following relations between the quasi-stabilization maps and the basepoint actions:

(3.6)

\displaystyle T_{w,z}^{+}

\displaystyle\simeq\Psi_{z}S_{w,z}^{+},

(3.7)

\displaystyle T_{w,z}^{-}

\displaystyle\simeq S_{w,z}^{-}\Psi_{z},

(3.8)

\displaystyle\Phi_{w}

\displaystyle\simeq S_{w,z}^{+}S_{w,z}^{-},

(3.9)

\displaystyle\Psi_{z}

\displaystyle\simeq T_{w,z}^{+}T_{w,z}^{-}.

Proofs of equations (
3.6
)–(
3.9
) can be found in
[
58
]
*Lemmas 4.10 and 4.11.
Examples of the corresponding dividing sets for the relations in equations (
3.6
)–(
3.9
)
appear in Figure
3.4
.

Figure 3.4.
Dividing set manipulations corresponding to the relations

T_{w,z}^{+}\simeq\Psi_{z}S_{w,z}^{+}
and
\Phi_{w}\simeq S_{w,z}^{+}S_{w,z}^{-}
.

Next, we review the connection between the basepoint moving maps
and the quasi-stabilization maps.
We first focus on using the quasi-stabilization maps to move a single
basepoint, while fixing all other basepoints. Suppose that
(L,\bm{\mathbf{w}}_{0}\cup\{w^{\prime}\},\bm{\mathbf{z}})

is a multi-based link in
Y
. Suppose that
(w,z)
are a new
pair of basepoints in a single component of
L\setminus(\bm{\mathbf{w}}_{0}\cup\{w^{\prime}\}\cup\bm{\mathbf{z}})
,
such that
z
is adjacent to
w^{\prime}
. Suppose further that, according to
the orientation of
L
, the three basepoints appear in the order
w^{\prime}
,
z
,
w
.
We can construct a diffeomorphism

\tau^{w\leftarrow w^{\prime}}\colon(Y,L,\bm{\mathbf{w}}_{0}\cup\{w^{\prime}\},\bm{\mathbf{z}})\to(Y,L,\bm{\mathbf{w}}_{0}\cup\{w\},\bm{\mathbf{z}}),

by moving
w^{\prime}
to
w
along the arc connecting them, but fixing all of
Y
outside a neighborhood of this arc.
According to
[
58
]
*Lemma 4.24,

(3.10)

T^{+}_{w,z}\simeq T_{z,w^{\prime}}^{+}\tau^{w\leftarrow w^{\prime}}_{*}.

Equation (
3.10
) has a simple description in terms of dividing sets and link cobordisms,
shown in Figure
3.5
.

Figure 3.5.
The interpretation of equation (
3.10
)
in terms of decorated link cobordisms.

Using the quasi-stabilization maps, we can also move a pair of adjacent
basepoints at the same time. Suppose that
\mathbb{L}=(L,\bm{\mathbf{w}}_{0},\bm{\mathbf{z}}_{0})
is a
multi-based link (though we allow the case where
\bm{\mathbf{w}}_{0}
and
\bm{\mathbf{z}}_{0}

intersect a single component of
L
trivially). Suppose that
(w^{\prime},z^{\prime},w,z)

are four new consecutive basepoints on a single
component of
L\setminus(\bm{\mathbf{w}}_{0}\cup\bm{\mathbf{z}}_{0})
. We assume that these four basepoints appear in the order
w^{\prime}
,
z^{\prime}
,
w
and
z
on the link, with
z
being the first and
w^{\prime}
being the last with respect to the orientation of
L
. There is a diffeomorphism

\rho^{(w^{\prime},z^{\prime})\leftarrow(w,z)}\colon(Y,L,\bm{\mathbf{w}}_{0}\cup\{w\},\bm{\mathbf{z}}_{0}\cup\{z\})\to(Y,L,\bm{\mathbf{w}}_{0}\cup\{w^{\prime}\},\bm{\mathbf{z}}_{0}\cup\{z^{\prime}\}),

obtained by moving the pair
(w,z)
to
(w^{\prime},z^{\prime})
, but fixing everything outside a neighborhood
of an interval containing the four basepoints
(w^{\prime},z^{\prime},w,z)
.
According to
[
58
]
*Lemma 4.27, there is a chain homotopy

(3.11)

\rho^{(w^{\prime},z^{\prime})\leftarrow(w,z)}_{*}\simeq S_{w,z}^{-}T_{w^{\prime},z^{\prime}}^{+}.

Equation (
3.11
) can be interpreted in terms of the manipulation of
dividing sets shown in Figure
3.6
.

Figure 3.6.
The interpretation of the relation
\rho^{(w^{\prime},z^{\prime})\leftarrow(w,z)}_{*}\simeq S_{w,z}^{-}T_{w^{\prime},z^{\prime}}^{+}

in terms of dividing sets.

Another useful relation is the following:

(3.12)

T_{w,z}^{+}S_{w,z}^{-}+S_{w,z}^{+}T_{w,z}^{-}+\id\simeq 0.

See
[
58
]
*Lemma 4.13. Note that Equation (
3.12
) follows immediately from the formulas in Equations (
3.4
) and (
3.5
).
For a pictorial description, see Figure
3.7
.
Equation (
3.12
) is an example of the
bypass relation
,
which is often helpful when doing computations in the link Floer TQFT.

Figure 3.7.
A pictorial description of the bypass relation, equation (
3.12
).

3.4.
Cobordism maps for saddles

Next, we discuss the maps for saddle cobordisms.
Suppose that
\mathbb{L}=(L,\bm{\mathbf{w}},\bm{\mathbf{z}})
is a multi-based link in
Y
,
and that
B
is an embedded band for
L
that has both ends in subarcs of
L\setminus(\bm{\mathbf{w}}\cup\bm{\mathbf{z}})

that run from
\bm{\mathbf{w}}
to
\bm{\mathbf{z}}
, or has both ends in subarcs that run from
\bm{\mathbf{z}}
to
\bm{\mathbf{w}}
.
Assuming that
\mathbb{L}(B)
, the result of band surgery, is also a valid multi-based link, there is a map

F_{B}^{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}\colon\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{\infty}(Y,\mathbb{L},\mathfrak{s})\to\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{\infty}(Y,\mathbb{L}(B),\mathfrak{s}),

described in
[
58
]
*Section 6.
The map
F_{B}^{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}
corresponds to a saddle link cobordism,
with an index 1 critical point occurring in the type-
\bm{\mathbf{z}}
subregion.
There is another map
F_{B}^{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
, with the same domain and codomain as
F_{B}^{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}
,
that corresponds to adding a band to the type-
\bm{\mathbf{w}}
subregion.

The relation between the band maps and the basepoint maps
is studied in
[
58
]
*Section 9.1. According to
[
58
]
*Lemma 9.1,

(3.13)

F_{B}^{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}\Phi_{w}+\Phi_{w}F_{B}^{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}\simeq 0.

In contrast, the map
F_{B}^{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}
does not always commute with
\Psi_{z}
.
Instead, if
z
is either of the two
\bm{\mathbf{z}}
-basepoints adjacent to the ends of
B
, then

(3.14)

F_{B}^{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}\Psi_{z}+\Psi_{z}F_{B}^{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}\simeq F_{B}^{\bm{\mathbf{w}}},

according to
[
58
]
*Proposition 9.3.
In fact, the three dividing sets corresponding to the maps in equation (
3.14
)
can be interpreted as a bypass relation on a saddle cobordism; see
[
58
]
*Figure 9.1.

Note that, if
z
and
z^{\prime}
are the two
\bm{\mathbf{z}}
-basepoints adjacent to the ends of
B
,
then equation (
3.14
) holds for both
z
and
z^{\prime}
.
As a consequence, if we sum both relations, we obtain

(3.15)

F_{B}^{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(\Psi_{z}+\Psi_{z^{\prime}})+(\Psi_{z}+\Psi_{z^{\prime}})F_{B}^{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}\simeq 0.

3.5.
Birth cobordisms and quasi-stabilizations

We recall from
[
58
]
*Section 7.1 the birth cobordism map.
Suppose
\mathbb{U}=(U,w,z)
is a doubly-based unknot, which is unlinked from the
multi-based link in
Y
, and is given a distinguished Seifert disk
D
. In
this situation, there is a well defined birth map

\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{U},D}^{+}\colon\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{\infty}(Y,\mathbb{L},\mathfrak{s})\to\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{\infty}(Y,\mathbb{L}\cup\mathbb{U},\mathfrak{s}).

The map
\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{U},D}^{+}
corresponds to a birth cobordism in
I\times Y
,
where the disk portion of the link cobordism surface is decorated with a
single dividing arc.

The map
\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{U},D}^{+}
can be computed as follows. We pick a Heegaard
diagram
(\Sigma,\bm{\mathbf{\alpha}},\bm{\mathbf{\beta}},\bm{\mathbf{w}},\bm{\mathbf{z}})
for
(Y,\mathbb{L})
such that
w,z\in\Sigma
,
and
D\cap\Sigma
consists of an embedded arc in
\Sigma\setminus(\bm{\mathbf{\alpha}}\cup\bm{\mathbf{\beta}})
that connects
w
and
z
. We add two new curves,
\alpha_{0}
and

\beta_{0}
, that bound a small disk containing
D\cap\Sigma
, and intersect
in a pair of points. Let
\theta_{\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}^{+}\in\alpha_{0}\cap\beta_{0}
denote the higher Maslov graded intersection point (the designation
is the same for both
\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
and
\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}
). The map
\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{U},D}^{+}
is
defined via the formula

\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{U},D}^{+}(\bm{\mathbf{x}})=\bm{\mathbf{x}}\times\theta^{+}_{\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}

for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\beta}
, and extended
\mathcal{R}^{\infty}
-equivariantly.
There is also a death map
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{U},D}^{-}
, defined in the opposite direction,
though it will not make an appearance in this paper.

Suppose
L
is a link in
Y
, and
U
is an unknot that bounds a Seifert
disk
D
, disjoint from
L
. Suppose further that
B
is a band connecting

U
and
L
, which is disjoint from the interior of
D
. Let

\phi\colon(Y,L)\to(Y,(L\cup U)(B))

denote a diffeomorphism which is the identity outside a neighborhood of
B\cup D
.

Since a birth cobordism adds two basepoints, the composition of a birth
cobordism map and a band map is not simply the diffeomorphism map
\phi_{*}
.
Instead the composition is a quasi-stabilization. More precisely, if

\mathbb{U}=(U,w,z)
, and
B
is an
\alpha
-band (i.e., has both ends on strands of

L
that lie in the
\alpha
-handlebody), then

(3.16)

F_{B}^{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}\mathcal{B}^{+}_{\mathbb{U},D}\simeq T_{w,z}^{+}\phi_{*}.

A proof of equation (
3.16
) can be found in

[
58
]
*Proposition 8.5. Note that there are other variations of
equation (
3.16
). For example, if
B
is instead a
\beta
-band, then

(3.17)

F_{B}^{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}\mathcal{B}^{+}_{\mathbb{U},D}\simeq T_{z,w}^{+}\phi_{*}.

A schematic illustrating equation (
3.16
) is shown in Figure
3.8
.

Figure 3.8.
A manipulation demonstrating equation (
3.16
).
The composition of a birth cobordism followed by a band is (up to diffeomorphism) a quasi-stabilization.

3.6.
4-dimensional connected sums of link cobordisms

Suppose
(W_{1},\mathcal{F}_{1})
and
(W_{2},\mathcal{F}_{2})
are two link cobordisms, and pick
two embedded 4-balls
D_{1}\subseteq W_{1}
and
D_{2}\subseteq W_{2}
such that
D_{i}\cap\mathcal{F}_{i}
consists of a 2-dimensional disk which intersects the dividing set of
\mathcal{F}_{i}
in a single arc. We glue
W_{1}\setminus\Int(D_{1})
to
W_{2}\setminus\Int(D_{2})
using an orientation-reversing diffeomorphism which restricts to an orientation-reversing diffeomorphism of
\mathcal{F}_{1}\cap\partial D_{1}
and
\mathcal{F}_{2}\cap\partial D_{2}
, and is compatible with the dividing sets. We write
(W_{1}\#W_{2},\mathcal{F}_{1}\#\mathcal{F}_{2})
for the resulting link cobordism. Using a handle cancellation argument
in the connected sum region, one can prove that

(3.18)

F_{W_{1}\#W_{2},\mathcal{F}_{1}\#\mathcal{F}_{2},\mathfrak{s}_{1}\#\mathfrak{s}_{2}}\simeq F_{W_{1},\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathfrak{s}_{1}}\otimes F_{W_{2},\mathcal{F}_{2},\mathfrak{s}_{2}}.

See
[
56
]
*Proposition 5.2 for a detailed proof. To make
use of equation (
3.18
), it will be convenient to
have a more explicit description of the cobordism map for
(W_{1}\#W_{2},\mathcal{F}_{1}\#\mathcal{F}_{2})
. To this end, suppose the following:

(1)

(Y_{1},\mathbb{L}_{1})
and
(Y_{2},\mathbb{L}_{2})
are two 3-manifolds with multi-based links.

(2)

\mathbb{S}_{0}\subseteq Y_{1}\sqcup Y_{2}
is a framed 0-sphere with one foot in
Y_{1}
and the other in
Y_{2}
.

(3)

\mathbb{S}_{2}\subseteq Y_{1}\#Y_{2}
is the dual framed 2-sphere.

(4)

B
is a band in
Y_{1}\#Y_{2}
that connects
\mathbb{L}_{1}
and
\mathbb{L}_{2}
, and intersects
\mathbb{S}_{2}
in a single arc.

(5)

B
is adjacent to the basepoints
w_{1}
and
z_{1}
on
\mathbb{L}_{1}
, as well as
w_{2}
and
z_{2}
on
\mathbb{L}_{2}
.

(6)

B^{\prime}
is the band in
Y_{1}\#Y_{2}
attached to
\mathbb{L}_{1}\#\mathbb{L}_{2}
dual to
B
.

When
(W_{i},\mathcal{F}_{i})
is the identity cobordism of
(Y_{i},\mathbb{L}_{i})
for
i\in\{1,2\}
,
equation (
3.18
) can be rewritten as

(3.19)

F_{\mathbb{S}_{2}}F_{B^{\prime}}^{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}\Phi_{w}F_{B}^{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}F_{\mathbb{S}_{0}}\simeq\id,

where
w\in\{w_{1},w_{2}\}
. Figure
3.9
shows equation (
3.19
)
in terms of dividing sets.

Figure 3.9.
A schematic of equation (
3.19
), the
effect of taking the connected sum of two link cobordisms. There is
additionally a 4-dimensional 1-handle and 3-handle, which are not shown.

4.
Heegaard Floer invariants of surfaces

4.1.
Variations on the knot Floer complex

In this section, we describe several variations on the full infinity complex
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{\infty}(Y,\mathbb{L},\mathfrak{s})
, which we will use to define our invariants. We focus on the case that
Y=\mathbb{S}^{3}
and
\mathbb{L}=\mathbb{K}=(K,w,z)
is a doubly-based knot.

The first variation we consider is the
standard infinity complex
, denoted
\mathit{CFK}^{\infty}(\mathbb{K})
. It is defined as the homogeneous subset of
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{K})
in Alexander grading zero; i.e., the
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-vector space generated by monomials

U^{i}V^{j}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{x}}

with
A(\bm{\mathbf{x}})+j-i=0
. Since the actions of
U
and
V
are
-1
and
+1
graded with respect to the Alexander grading, the chain complex
\mathit{CFK}^{\infty}(\mathbb{K})
is not an
\mathbb{F}_{2}[U,V]
-module. However the product
\widehat{U}:=UV
is 0-graded with respect to
A
, and we view
\mathit{CFK}^{\infty}(\mathbb{K})
as an
\mathbb{F}_{2}[\widehat{U},\widehat{U}^{-1}]
-module. The complex
\mathit{CFK}^{\infty}(\mathbb{K})
contains essentially the same information as
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{K})
. We will use
\mathit{CFK}^{\infty}(\mathbb{K})
to define our invariants
V_{k}(S,S^{\prime})
and
\mathcal{I}(S)
.

There are two
small minus complexes
,

\mathit{CFK}^{-}_{U=0}(\mathbb{K}):=\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(\mathbb{K})\otimes_{\mathcal{R}^{-}}\mathcal{R}^{-}/(U)\,\text{ and }\,\mathit{CFK}^{-}_{V=0}(\mathbb{K}):=\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(\mathbb{K})\otimes_{\mathcal{R}^{-}}\mathcal{R}^{-}/(V),

which are modules over
\mathbb{F}_{2}[V]
and
\mathbb{F}_{2}[U]
, respectively.
By inverting
V
or
U
, respectively, we obtain the
small infinity complexes

\mathit{CFK}^{\infty}_{U=0}(\mathbb{K})\quad\text{and}\quad\mathit{CFK}^{\infty}_{V=0}(\mathbb{K}),

whose homologies are canonically isomorphic to
\mathbb{F}_{2}[V,V^{-1}]
and
\mathbb{F}_{2}[U,U^{-1}]
, respectively.

By setting
V=1
in the complex
\mathit{CFK}^{-}_{U=0}(\mathbb{K})
, we obtain the
Alexander filtered complex

\widehat{\mathit{CFK}}{}^{\mathit{fil},z}(\mathbb{K}),

described by Ozsváth and Szabó
[
36
]
.
It has an increasing filtration over
\mathbb{Z}
; i.e., we have an increasing sequence of subcomplexes

\widehat{\mathit{CFK}}{}^{\mathit{fil},z}_{i}(\mathbb{K})\subseteq\widehat{\mathit{CFK}}{}^{\mathit{fil},z}_{i+1}(\mathbb{K})

for
i\in\mathbb{Z}
. The subspace
\widehat{\mathit{CFK}}{}^{\mathit{fil},z}_{i}(\mathbb{K})
is generated by the set of
intersection points
\bm{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\beta}
with
A(\bm{\mathbf{x}})\leq i
.
The differential counts holomorphic disks which are allowed to pass over
z
, but not
w
.
Note that

\widehat{\mathit{CFK}}{}^{\mathit{fil},z}_{i}(\mathbb{K})=\widehat{\mathit{CFK}}{}^{\mathit{fil},z}(\mathbb{K})\cong\widehat{\mathit{CF}}(\mathbb{S}^{3})

for sufficiently large
i
, while
