\widehat{\mathit{CFK}}{}^{\mathit{fil},z}_{i}(\mathbb{K})=\{0\}
for sufficiently negative
i
. In particular, the total homology of
\widehat{\mathit{CFK}}{}^{\mathit{fil},z}(\mathbb{K})
is isomorphic to
\widehat{\mathit{HF}}(\mathbb{S}^{3})\cong\mathbb{F}_{2}
.

Symmetrically, one can filter using the
w
basepoint to get a
\mathbb{Z}
-filtered chain complex
\widehat{\mathit{CFK}}{}^{\mathit{fil},w}(\mathbb{K})
. The filtration
\widehat{\mathit{CFK}}{}^{\mathit{fil},w}(\mathbb{K})
is
decreasing
; i.e.,

\widehat{\mathit{CFK}}{}^{\mathit{fil},w}_{i+1}(\mathbb{K})\subseteq\widehat{\mathit{CFK}}{}^{\mathit{fil},w}_{i}(\mathbb{K}),

where
\widehat{\mathit{CFK}}{}^{\mathit{fil},w}_{i}(\mathbb{K})
is generated over
\mathbb{F}_{2}
by intersection points
\bm{\mathbf{x}}
with
A(\bm{\mathbf{x}})\geq i
.

Remark 4.1
.

The chain complex
\mathit{CFK}^{-}_{U=0}(\mathbb{K})
and the filtered chain complex
\widehat{\mathit{CFK}}{}^{\mathit{fil},z}(\mathbb{K})
contain equivalent information. To see this, note that
\widehat{\mathit{CFK}}{}^{\mathit{fil},z}(\mathbb{K})
is obtained from
\mathit{CFK}^{-}_{U=0}(\mathbb{K})
by setting
V=1
, and using the filtration induced by the Alexander grading. In the other direction,
\mathit{CFK}^{-}_{U=0}(\mathbb{K})
is obtained by taking a basis of intersection points of
\widehat{\mathit{CFK}}{}^{\mathit{fil},z}(\mathbb{K})
, and weighting an intersection point
\bm{\mathbf{y}}
appearing in
\partial\bm{\mathbf{x}}
by
V^{A(\bm{\mathbf{x}})-A(\bm{\mathbf{y}})}
.

Furthermore, there is a conjugation symmetry of knot Floer homology that allows one to recover
\mathit{CFK}^{-}_{V=0}(\mathbb{K})
from
\mathit{CFK}^{-}_{U=0}(\mathbb{K})
, and vice versa, and similarly recover
\widehat{\mathit{CFK}}{}^{\mathit{fil},w}(\mathbb{K})
from
\widehat{\mathit{CFK}}{}^{\mathit{fil},z}(\mathbb{K})
.
Our invariant
\tau(S,S^{\prime})
will be defined in Section
4.3
using
\widehat{\mathit{CFK}}{}^{\mathit{fil},z}(\mathbb{K})
,
while
\kappa(S,S^{\prime})
and
\kappa_{0}(S)
in Section
4.7

in terms of
\mathit{CFK}^{-}_{U=0}(\mathbb{K})
.

Another variation we use to construct our invariants is the
t
-modified complex
, denoted
\mathit{tCFK}^{-}(\mathbb{K})
, due to Ozsváth–Stipsicz–Szabó
[
33
]
. If
t=\frac{m}{n}\in[0,2]
is a rational number with
m
and
n
relatively prime integers and
n\neq 0
, then we consider the ring
\mathbb{F}_{2}[v^{1/n}]
, where
v
has grading
-1
. The ring
\mathbb{F}_{2}[v^{1/n}]
can be given an action of
\mathbb{F}_{2}[U,V]
by having
U
act by
v^{2-t}
and
V
act by
v^{t}
. The chain complex
\mathit{tCFK}^{-}(\mathbb{K})
is defined as the tensor product

\mathit{tCFK}^{-}(\mathbb{K}):=\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(\mathbb{K})\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{2}[U,V]}\mathbb{F}_{2}[v^{1/n}].

The invariant
\Upsilon_{(S,S^{\prime})}(t)
will be defined in Section
4.6
using
\mathit{tCFK}^{-}(\mathbb{K})
.

A final variation is the
hat complex

\widehat{\mathit{CFK}}(\mathbb{K}):=\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(\mathbb{K})\otimes_{\mathcal{R}^{-}}\mathcal{R}^{-}/(U,V).

We will not discuss
\widehat{\mathit{CFK}}(\mathbb{K})
extensively in this paper,
since it does not contain enough information to compute most of our invariants.

4.2.
The principal invariants of a surface bounding a knot

We now describe two generalizations of the slice disk invariant

t_{D}\in\widehat{\mathit{HFK}}(\mathbb{K})
, defined by Marengon and the first author
[
17
]
,
to higher genus surfaces in the full infinity complex.

Definition 4.2
.

Let
\mathbb{K}=(K,w,z)
be a multi-based knot in
\mathbb{S}^{3}
, and let
S\in\Surf_{g}(K)
be a surface in
B^{4}
bounding
K
.
If
\mathcal{A}
is a decoration on
S
consisting of a single arc which divides
S
into two connected subsurfaces, then we say that the map
F_{B^{4},(S,\mathcal{A})}
is a
principal invariant
of the surface
S
.

Let
\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}
denote the decoration on
S
consisting of a single arc
such that
g(S_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}})=g(S)
and
g(S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}})=0
; see Figure
4.1
. We define

\bm{\mathbf{t}}^{\infty}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}:=F_{B^{4},(S,\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}})}\colon\mathcal{R}^{\infty}\to\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{K}).

Similarly, if
\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
denotes the decoration on
S
with
g(S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}})=g(S)
and
g(S_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}})=0
, we define

\bm{\mathbf{t}}^{\infty}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{w}}}:=F_{B^{4},(S,\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}})}\colon\mathcal{R}^{\infty}\to\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{K}).

We call
\bm{\mathbf{t}}^{\infty}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
and
\bm{\mathbf{t}}^{\infty}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}
the
extremal principal invariants

of the surface
S
.

Both
\bm{\mathbf{t}}^{\infty}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
and
\bm{\mathbf{t}}^{\infty}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}
are filtered,
\mathcal{R}^{\infty}
-equivariant
chain map that are well-defined up to filtered,
\mathcal{R}^{\infty}
-equivariant chain homotopy.
Furthermore, both of them induce isomorphisms on homology by
[
57
, Theorem 9.9]
.
By
[
57
, Theorem 1.4]
, the map
\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{w}}}^{\infty}

decreases the Alexander grading by
g(S)
, while
\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{\infty}
increases it by
g(S)
.
When
D\in\mathcal{D}(K)
is a slice disk for
K
, then
\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}=\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
,
and we denote
\bm{\mathbf{t}}^{\infty}_{D,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}=\bm{\mathbf{t}}^{\infty}_{D,\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
by
\bm{\mathbf{t}}^{\infty}_{D}
.

Figure 4.1.
The dividing set
\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}
used to define the map
\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{\Sigma,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{\infty}
.

Although the chain complexes
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{K})
and
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(\mathbb{K})
contain equivalent information,
it is convenient to also define maps

\bm{\mathbf{t}}^{-}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{w}}}\text{, }\bm{\mathbf{t}}^{-}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}\colon\mathcal{R}^{-}\to\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(\mathbb{K})

as the cobordism maps on the full minus complexes. For a slice disk
D
, we again have

\bm{\mathbf{t}}^{-}_{D,\bm{\mathbf{w}}}=\bm{\mathbf{t}}^{-}_{D,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}
, which we denote by
\bm{\mathbf{t}}^{-}_{D}
.
By
[
20
]
*Theorem 1.4, when we set
U=V=0
, the map
\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{D}^{-}
,
defined using the maps from
[
58
]
, becomes
t_{D}
, defined in
[
17
]
.

We note that the elements
[\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{w}}}^{-}(1)]
and
[\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)]

in the homology group
\mathcal{H\!F\!L}^{-}(\mathbb{K}):=H_{*}(\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(\mathbb{K}))
contain exactly the
same information as the
\mathcal{R}^{-}
-equivariant,
\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}
-filtered chain
homotopy types of the maps

\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{w}}}^{-},\,\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}\colon\mathcal{R}^{-}\to\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(\mathbb{K}),

since two filtered, equivariant maps from
\mathcal{R}^{-}
to
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(\mathbb{K})
are
filtered, equivariantly chain homotopic if and only if their values on
1\in\mathcal{R}^{-}
differ by
\partial\eta
for some
\eta\in\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(\mathbb{K})
. Nonetheless,
we will usually not view
\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{w}}}^{-}
and
\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}
as elements of

\mathcal{H\!F\!L}^{-}(\mathbb{K})
because, on its own, the group
\mathcal{H\!F\!L}^{-}(\mathbb{K})
is not sufficient to define our invariants.

We now describe some basic properties of the invariants
\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{w}}}^{\infty}
and
\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{\infty}
.
Let
r
denote the rolling automorphism of
(\mathbb{S}^{3},\mathbb{K})
that consists of a Dehn twist about
K
;
see Definition
2.3
. Then

(4.1)

r_{*}\circ\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{\infty}\simeq\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{\infty},

and similarly for
\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{w}}}^{\infty}
,
as the corresponding dividing sets differ by a Dehn twist about
\partial S
, and are hence isotopic.

Definition 4.3
.

Let
C
and
C^{\prime}
be free,
\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}
-filtered chain complexes over
\mathcal{R}^{\infty}
.
We say that a map
f\colon C\to C^{\prime}
is
skew-equivariant
and
skew-filtered
if

f\circ U=V\circ f,\quad f\circ V=U\circ f,\quad\text{and}\quad f(\mathcal{G}_{i,j}(C))\subseteq\mathcal{G}_{j,i}(C^{\prime}).

Given skew-equivariant and skew-filtered chain maps
f\text{, }g\colon C\to C^{\prime}
,
we say that they are
skew-equivariant and skew-filtered chain homotopic
, and write
f\eqsim g
,
if they are chain homotopic through a skew-equivariant and skew-filtered chain homotopy.

There is a skew-equivariant and skew-filtered homotopy automorphism

\iota_{K}\colon\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{K})\to\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{K}),

defined as the composition of a tautological conjugation automorphism of

\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{K})
, and the map induced by a half Dehn twist about
K

that switches
w
and
z
; see
[
13
]
*Section 6.1.
The map
\iota_{K}
satisfies
\iota_{K}^{2}\simeq r_{*}
.

Using the conjugation formula for the link cobordism maps
[
56
]
*Theorem 1.3,
as well as the same manipulation of dividing sets as in equation (
4.1
), one obtains

(4.2)

\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{w}}}^{\infty}\circ\iota_{\mathcal{R}^{\infty}}\eqsim\iota_{K}\circ\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{\infty},

where
\iota_{\mathcal{R}^{\infty}}\colon\mathcal{R}^{\infty}\to\mathcal{R}^{\infty}
denotes the unique
involution that switches
U
and
V
.
Using equation (
4.1
), we can rewrite equation (
4.2
) as

(4.3)

\iota_{K}\circ\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{w}}}^{\infty}\circ\iota_{\mathcal{R}^{\infty}}\simeq\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{\infty}.

Hence, together with the conjugation automorphism,
\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{w}}}^{\infty}
and
\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{\infty}

provide essentially equivalent information.

In the following subsections, we introduce several invariants of a pair of surfaces
S
,
S^{\prime}\in\Surf(K)
,
in order to give lower bounds on their stabilization distance. These are all derived from the principal invariants
for
S
and
S^{\prime}
. Furthermore, our invariants
\tau
,
\nu
,
V_{k}
, and
\Upsilon

are constructed as algebraic analogues of the homonymous knot invariants from knot Floer homology.
Hence, we shall sometimes call these
secondary versions
of their knot invariant counterparts.

4.3.
The tau invariant

Let
\mathbb{K}=(K,w,z)
be a doubly-based knot in
\mathbb{S}^{3}
.
We now describe a map

\tau\colon\Surf(K)\times\Surf(K)\to\mathbb{Z}^{\geq 0}.

It is a secondary version of the concordance invariant defined by Ozsváth and Szabó
[
36
]
.
Let
(\Sigma,\bm{\mathbf{\alpha}},\bm{\mathbf{\beta}},w,z)
be a doubly-pointed diagram representing
\mathbb{K}
. We will define
the invariant
\tau(S,S^{\prime})
for
S
,
S^{\prime}\in\Surf(K)
in terms of the Alexander filtered
complex
\widehat{\mathit{CFK}}{}^{\mathit{fil},z}(\mathbb{K})
described in Section
4.1
.

Following Section
4.2
, a surface
S\in\Surf_{g}(K)
induces a chain map

\widehat{t}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}\colon\mathbb{F}_{2}\to\widehat{\mathit{CFK}}{}^{\mathit{fil},z}(\mathbb{K})
whose image is contained in
\widehat{\mathit{CFK}}{}^{\mathit{fil},z}_{g}(\mathbb{K})
. We recall that for sufficiently large
n
, we have

(4.4)

H_{*}(\widehat{\mathit{CFK}}{}^{\mathit{fil},z}_{n}(\mathbb{K}))\cong\widehat{\mathit{HF}}(S^{3})=\mathbb{F}.

Additionally, it follows from the reduction theorem
[
58
]
*Theorem C that with respect to the isomorphism in equation (
4.4
), the element
\widehat{t}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(1)
represents
1\in\mathbb{F}
. We make the following definition:

Definition 4.4
.

Let
\mathbb{K}=(K,w,z)
be a doubly-based knot in
\mathbb{S}^{3}
.
Given surfaces
S\in\Surf_{g}(K)
and
S^{\prime}\in\Surf_{g^{\prime}}(K)
, we define the invariant

\tau(S,S^{\prime})=\min\left\{n\geq\max\{g,g^{\prime}\}:\left[\widehat{t}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(1)\right]=\left[\widehat{t}_{S^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(1)\right]\text{ in }H_{*}\left(\widehat{\mathit{CFK}}{}^{\mathit{fil},z}_{n}(\mathbb{K})\right)\right\}.

It is straightforward to see that
\tau(S,S^{\prime})
is independent of the choice of basepoints on
K
, and is furthermore a finite integer.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
S_{1}
,
S_{1}^{\prime}
,
S_{2}
,
S_{2}^{\prime}\in\Surf(K)
be surfaces such that

[S_{1}]=[S_{2}]
and
[S_{1}^{\prime}]=[S_{2}^{\prime}]
in
\Surf(K)/\{\text{2-knots}\}
.
Then

\tau(S_{1},S_{1}^{\prime})=\tau(S_{2},S_{2}^{\prime}).

Proof.

This follows from the observation that
\widehat{t}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}

is unchanged, up to filtered chain homotopy,
if we take the connected sum of
S
with a 2-knot,
which can be shown by adapting the proof of
[
21
, Lemma 4.2]
.
∎

We recall that the concordance invariant
\tau(K)
may be
computed from the
\mathbb{F}_{2}[V]
-module

\mathit{CFK}^{-}_{U=0}(\mathbb{K}):=\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(\mathbb{K})\otimes_{\mathcal{R}^{-}}\mathcal{R}^{-}/(U),

obtained from
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(\mathbb{K})
by setting
U=0
; see Ozsváth–Szabó–Thurston
[
34
]
*Lemma A.2. (Note that Ozsváth, Szabó, and Thurston use the
V=0
version of knot Floer homology. Using the conjugation symmetry, these are equivalent perspectives).

Analogously, we can reformulate
\tau(S,S^{\prime})
in terms of
\mathit{CFK}^{-}_{U=0}(\mathbb{K})
. Let us write
t_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}
and
t_{S^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}
for the maps from
\mathbb{F}_{2}[V]
to
\mathit{CFK}_{U=0}^{-}(\mathbb{K})

induced by
\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}
and
\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}
, respectively.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
\mathbb{K}=(K,w,z)
be a doubly-based knot in
\mathbb{S}^{3}
.
If
S\in\Surf_{g}(K)
and
S^{\prime}\in\Surf_{g^{\prime}}(K)
, then

\tau(S,S^{\prime})=\min\{\,n\geq\max\{g,g^{\prime}\}:V^{n-g}\cdot[t_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)]=V^{n-g^{\prime}}\cdot[t_{S^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)]\text{ in }\mathit{HFK}^{-}_{U=0}(\mathbb{K})\,\}.

Proof.

Let us write
\zeta(S,S^{\prime})
for the right-hand side. If
n=\zeta(S,S^{\prime})
, then

V^{n-g}\cdot t_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)+V^{n-g^{\prime}}\cdot t_{S^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)=\partial x

for some
x\in\mathit{CFK}_{U=0}^{-}(\mathbb{K})
. Note that
\partial
preserves the Alexander
grading,
V
increases it by one,
A(t_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1))=g
,
and
A(t_{S^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1))=g^{\prime}
. Hence, we can assume that
A(x)=n
.
Consequently, using the identification

\widehat{\mathit{CFK}}{}^{\mathit{fil},z}(\mathbb{K})\cong\mathit{CFK}_{U=0}^{-}(\mathbb{K})\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{2}[V]}\mathbb{F}_{2}[V]/(V-1),

we have
x\otimes 1\in\widehat{\mathit{CFK}}{}^{\mathit{fil},z}_{n}(\mathbb{K})
, and

\widehat{t}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(1)+\widehat{t}_{S^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(1)=\partial(x\otimes 1)\in\widehat{\mathit{CFK}}{}^{\mathit{fil},z}_{n}(\mathbb{K}).

It follows that

\tau(S,S^{\prime})\leq\zeta(S,S^{\prime}).

Conversely, suppose
n=\tau(S,S^{\prime})
. Then

\widehat{t}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(1)+\widehat{t}_{S^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(1)=\partial x
for some
x\in\widehat{\mathit{CFK}}{}^{\mathit{fil},z}_{n}(\mathbb{K})
. We write
x
as a sum of intersection points
x=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{i}
. We define an element
\widetilde{x}\in\mathit{CFK}_{U=0}^{-}(\mathbb{K})
in Alexander grading
n
via the formula
\widetilde{x}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}V^{n-A(\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{i})}\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{i}
. The element
\widetilde{x}
satisfies

V^{n-g}\cdot t_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)+V^{n-g^{\prime}}\cdot t_{S^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)=\partial\widetilde{x}.

Compare Remark
4.1
.
It follows that

\zeta(S,S^{\prime})\leq\tau(S,S^{\prime}),

which concludes the proof.
∎

Let
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
be knots in
\mathbb{S}^{3}
.
Given surfaces
S_{1}\in\Surf(K_{1})
and
S_{2}\in\Surf(K_{2})
, their boundary
connected sum
S_{1}\natural S_{2}
is an element of
\Surf(K_{1}\#K_{2})
.

Proposition 4.7
.

Let
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
be knots in
\mathbb{S}^{3}
.
If
S_{1}
,
S_{1}^{\prime}\in\Surf(K_{1})
and
S_{2}
,
S_{2}^{\prime}\in\Surf(K_{2})

are surfaces of genera
g_{1}
,
g_{1}^{\prime}
,
g_{2}
, and
g_{2}^{\prime}
, respectively, then

\tau(S_{1}\natural S_{2},S_{1}^{\prime}\natural S_{2}^{\prime})\leq\max\left\{\tau(S_{1},S_{1}^{\prime})+\max\{g_{2},g_{2}^{\prime}\},\tau(S_{2},S_{2}^{\prime})+\max\{g_{1},g_{1}^{\prime}\}\right\}.

Furthermore, when
g_{1}=g_{1}^{\prime}=g_{2}=g_{2}^{\prime}=0
, then equality holds.

Proof.

Using the connected sum formula
[
56
, Proposition 5.1]
, there is chain homotopy equivalence

F\colon\mathit{CFK}_{U=0}^{-}(\mathbb{K}_{1})\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{2}[V]}\mathit{CFK}_{U=0}^{-}(\mathbb{K}_{2})\to\mathit{CFK}_{U=0}^{-}(\mathbb{K}_{1}\#\mathbb{K}_{2}),

where the decoration on
\mathbb{K}_{1}\#\mathbb{K}_{2}
is the decoration of
\mathbb{K}_{1}
.
Furthermore, the map
F
can be taken to be the
link cobordism map for a 1-handle cobordism containing a band. By the functoriality
of the link cobordism maps,

F\circ(t_{S_{1},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}\otimes t_{S_{2},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-})\simeq t_{S_{1}\natural S_{2},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-},

and similarly for
S_{1}^{\prime}
and
S_{2}^{\prime}
.

By the Künneth theorem for tensor products over a PID, there is a short exact sequence

\begin{split}0\to\mathit{HFK}^{-}_{U=0}(\mathbb{K}_{1})\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{2}[V]}\mathit{HFK}^{-}_{U=0}(\mathbb{K}_{2})\xrightarrow{G}\mathit{HFK}^{-}_{U=0}(\mathbb{K}_{1}\#\mathbb{K}_{2})\to\\
\Tor_{\mathbb{F}_{2}[V]}^{1}(\mathit{HFK}^{-}(\mathbb{K}_{1}),\mathit{HFK}^{-}_{U=0}(\mathbb{K}_{2}))\to 0,\end{split}

where
G
is the composition of the natural map

\mathit{HFK}^{-}_{U=0}(\mathbb{K}_{1})\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{2}[V]}\mathit{HFK}^{-}_{U=0}(\mathbb{K}_{2})\to H_{*}(\mathit{CFK}_{U=0}^{-}(\mathbb{K}_{1})\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{2}[V]}\mathit{CFK}_{U=0}^{-}(\mathbb{K}_{2}))

and
F_{*}
. The map
G
sends
[t_{S_{1},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)]\otimes[t_{S_{2},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)]
to
[t_{S_{1}\natural S_{2},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)]
.

For
i\in\{1,2\}
, the
\mathbb{F}_{2}[V]
-module
\mathit{HFK}^{-}_{U=0}(\mathbb{K}_{i})

splits (non-canonically) as
\mathbb{F}_{2}[V]\oplus T_{i}
, where
T_{i}
is
a torsion
\mathbb{F}_{2}[V]
-module, and

t_{S_{i},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)=V^{g_{i}}\oplus s_{i}
and
t_{S_{i}^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)=V^{g_{i}^{\prime}}\oplus s_{i}^{\prime}
for some
s_{i}
,
s_{i}^{\prime}\in T_{i}
.
Let

(4.5)

n=\max\left\{\tau(S_{1},S_{1}^{\prime})+\max\{g_{2},g_{2}^{\prime}\},\tau(S_{2},S_{2}^{\prime})+\max\{g_{1},g_{1}^{\prime}\}\right\}.

Then we claim that

(4.6)

V^{n-g_{1}-g_{2}}\cdot([t_{S_{1},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)]\otimes[t_{S_{2},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)])=V^{n-g_{1}^{\prime}-g_{2}^{\prime}}\cdot([t_{S_{1}^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)]\otimes[t_{S_{2}^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)])

as elements of
\mathit{HFK}^{-}(\mathbb{K}_{1})\otimes\mathit{HFK}^{-}(\mathbb{K}_{2})
. This is equivalent to

\begin{split}&V^{n-g_{1}-g_{2}}((V^{g_{1}}\otimes V^{g_{2}})\oplus(s_{1}\otimes V^{g_{2}})\oplus(V^{g_{1}}\otimes s_{2})\oplus(s_{1}\otimes s_{2}))=\\
&V^{n-g_{1}^{\prime}-g_{2}^{\prime}}((V^{g_{1}^{\prime}}\otimes V^{g_{2}^{\prime}})\oplus(s_{1}^{\prime}\otimes V^{g_{2}^{\prime}})\oplus(V^{g_{1}^{\prime}}\otimes s_{2}^{\prime})\oplus(s_{1}^{\prime}\otimes s_{2}^{\prime})).\end{split}

For
i\in\{1,2\}
and
k\geq\tau(S_{i},S_{i}^{\prime})
,
by Lemma
4.6
, we have

V^{k}\oplus(V^{k-g_{i}}s_{i})=V^{k-g_{i}}\cdot[t_{S_{i},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)]=V^{k-g_{i}^{\prime}}\cdot[t_{S_{i}^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)]=V^{k}\oplus(V^{k-g_{i}^{\prime}}s_{i}^{\prime}).

Together with equation (
4.5
),
this implies that
V^{n-g_{i}}s_{i}=V^{n-g_{i}^{\prime}}s_{i}^{\prime}
and

V^{n-g_{1}-g_{2}}(s_{1}\otimes s_{2})=V^{n-g_{1}^{\prime}-g_{2}^{\prime}}(s_{1}^{\prime}\otimes s_{2}^{\prime}),

so equation (
4.6
) holds.
The result follows by applying
G
to equation (
4.6
),
and invoking Lemma
4.6
.

When
g_{1}=g_{1}^{\prime}=g_{2}=g_{2}^{\prime}=0
, then we choose

n=\tau(S_{1}\natural S_{2},S_{1}^{\prime}\natural S_{2}^{\prime}).

By Lemma
4.6
,

n
satisfies equation (
4.6
), which implies
that
V^{n}s_{i}=V^{n}s_{i}^{\prime}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
, and hence

V^{n}\cdot[t^{-}_{S_{i},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}]=V^{n}\cdot[t^{-}_{S_{i}^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}].

So
n\geq\max\{\tau(S_{1},S_{1}^{\prime}),\tau(S_{2},S_{2}^{\prime})\}
, and equality holds, as claimed.
∎

4.4.
An infinitesimal refinement of tau

In this section, we describe a refinement of
\tau(S,S^{\prime})
,
inspired by work of Ozsváth–Szabó
[
42
]
,
Hom–Wu
[
15
]
, and Hom
[
16
]
,

Let
\bar{\mathbb{Z}}
denote
\mathbb{Z}\cup\{-\infty,\infty\}
,
and write
\bar{\mathbb{Z}}^{\leq 0}=[-\infty,0]\cap\bar{\mathbb{Z}}
.
Given a knot
K
in
\mathbb{S}^{3}
, we will define a symmetric map

\tau^{+}\colon\Surf(K)\times\Surf(K)\to\mathbb{N}\times\bar{\mathbb{Z}}^{\leq 0}.

The invariant
\tau^{+}(S,S^{\prime})
takes the form

\tau^{+}(S,S^{\prime}):=(\tau(S,S^{\prime}),\tau^{\prime}(S,S^{\prime})),

where
\tau(S,S^{\prime})
is the integer defined in Section
4.3
,
and
\tau^{\prime}(S,S^{\prime})
is an element of
\bar{\mathbb{Z}}^{\leq 0}
that we define shortly.
We will think of
\tau^{\prime}
as a second-order version of
\tau
, or an infinitesimal refinement.

To define
\tau^{\prime}
, we introduce some notation. If
(i,j)\in\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}
, let

R_{i,j}:=\{\,(m,n)\in\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}:m\geq i\text{, }n\geq j\,\}.

If
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}
, let
H(\mathcal{S})
denote
the
filtered hull
of
\mathcal{S}
; i.e.,

H(\mathcal{S})=\bigcup_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{S}}R_{i,j}.

We say that
\mathcal{S}
is a
filtered
shape if

\mathcal{S}=H(\mathcal{S}).

Let
w
and
z
be basepoints on
K
, and write
\mathbb{K}=(K,w,z)
.
If
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}
, let
C(\mathbb{K},\mathcal{S})
denote the subspace of
\mathit{CFK}^{\infty}(\mathbb{K})

generated over
\mathbb{F}_{2}
by monomials
U^{i}V^{j}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{x}}
with
A(\bm{\mathbf{x}})+j-i=0

and
(i,j)\in\mathcal{S}
. If
\mathcal{S}
is a filtered shape, then
C(\mathbb{K},\mathcal{S})
is a subcomplex of
\mathit{CFK}^{\infty}(\mathbb{K})
.

More generally, we say
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}
is a
sub-quotient
shape if, whenever

(i,j)
,
(m,n)\in\mathcal{S}
with
i\leq m
and
j\leq n
,
then the entire rectangle spanned by the points
(i,j)
and
(m,n)
is
contained in
\mathcal{S}
.
If
\mathcal{S}
is a sub-quotient shape, then
C(\mathbb{K},\mathcal{S})
is in fact a sub-quotient
complex of
\mathit{CFK}^{\infty}(\mathbb{K})
; i.e., there are subcomplexes

C_{\text{in}}(\mathbb{K},\mathcal{S})\subseteq C_{\text{out}}(\mathbb{K},\mathcal{S})\subseteq\mathit{CFK}^{\infty}(\mathbb{K})
such that

C_{\text{out}}(\mathbb{K},\mathcal{S})/C_{\text{in}}(\mathbb{K},\mathcal{S})

is chain isomorphic to
C(\mathbb{K},\mathcal{S})
. Indeed, the two sub-complexes of
\mathit{CFK}^{\infty}(\mathbb{K})
are

\begin{split}C_{\text{out}}(\mathbb{K},\mathcal{S})&:=C\left(\mathbb{K},H(\mathcal{S})\right)\text{, and}\\
C_{\text{in}}(\mathbb{K},\mathcal{S})&:=C\left(\mathbb{K},H(\mathcal{S})\setminus\mathcal{S}\right).\end{split}

We note that, if
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}
is an arbitrary subset, then its filtered hull

H(\mathcal{S})
is automatically a filtered shape. It is an easy exercise to show that,
if
\mathcal{S}
is a sub-quotient shape, then
H(\mathcal{S})\setminus\mathcal{S}
is filtered. Hence

C_{\text{in}}(\mathbb{K},\mathcal{S})
and
C_{\text{out}}(\mathbb{K},\mathcal{S})
are both subcomplexes of
\mathit{CFK}^{\infty}(\mathbb{K})
.

We note that the map
\bm{\mathbf{t}}^{-}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}
naturally has image in the
g(S)
Alexander graded subspace of
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(\mathbb{K})
.
Hence, there is a well-defined map

V^{-g(S)}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}\colon\mathbb{F}_{2}[\widehat{U}]\to C(\mathbb{K},R_{0,-g(S)}).

Furthermore, if
\mathcal{S}
is a sub-quotient shape of
\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}

such that
R_{0,-g(S)}\subseteq H(\mathcal{S})
, then there is a natural map

q\colon C(\mathbb{K},R_{0,-g(S)})\to C(\mathbb{K},\mathcal{S}),

which is the composition of the inclusion map
C(\mathbb{K},R_{0,-g(S)})\to C_{\text{out}}(\mathbb{K},\mathcal{S})
,
followed by the quotient map
C_{\text{out}}(\mathbb{K},\mathcal{S})\to C_{\text{out}}(\mathbb{K},\mathcal{S})/C_{\text{in}}(\mathbb{K},\mathcal{S})\cong C(\mathbb{K},\mathcal{S})
.
In particular,
[V^{-g(S)}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)]
determines a well-defined element of
H_{*}(C(\mathbb{K},\mathcal{S}))
.

Define

{\vphantom{I}}{}_{n}I:=\{0\}\times([-n,\infty)\cap\mathbb{Z}),

which is a sub-quotient shape.
Noting that
C(\mathbb{K},{\vphantom{I}}_{n}I)
is chain isomorphic to
\widehat{\mathit{CFK}}{}^{\mathit{fil},z}_{n}(\mathbb{K})
, we obtain the following:

Lemma 4.8
.

Given a doubly-based knot
\mathbb{K}=(K,w,z)
and surfaces
S
,
S^{\prime}\in\Surf(K)
, we have

\tau(S,S^{\prime})=\min\left\{n\geq\max\{g(S),g(S^{\prime})\}:[V^{-g(S)}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}^{-}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(1)]=[V^{-g(S^{\prime})}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}^{-}_{S^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(1)]\text{ in }H_{*}(C(\mathbb{K},{\vphantom{I}}_{n}I))\right\}.

We are now ready to define the refinement
\tau^{\prime}(S,S^{\prime})
.

Definition 4.9
.

For
n
,
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, let

{\vphantom{L}}{}_{n}L_{m}:={\vphantom{I}}_{n}I\cup([0,m]\cap\mathbb{Z})\times\{-n\}.

This is an L-shaped subset of
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}
, and hence a sub-quotient shape.

Let
\mathbb{K}=(K,w,z)
be a doubly-pointed knot in
\mathbb{S}^{3}
,
let
S
,
S^{\prime}\in\Surf(K)
, and write
\tau=\tau(S,S^{\prime})
.
Then we define

\tau^{\prime}(S,S^{\prime})=-\sup\{\,m\in\mathbb{Z}:[V^{-g(S)}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}^{-}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(1)-V^{-g(S^{\prime})}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}^{-}_{S^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(1)]=0\in H_{*}\left(C(\mathbb{K},{\vphantom{L}}_{\tau}L_{m}\right)\,\}.

Note that
{\vphantom{L}}{}_{\tau}L_{0}={\vphantom{I}}_{\tau}I
, and
[V^{-g(S)}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}^{-}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(1)-V^{-g(S^{\prime})}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}^{-}_{S^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(1)]=0

in
H_{*}(C(\mathbb{K},{\vphantom{I}}_{\tau}I))
by the definition of
\tau
. It follows that
\tau^{\prime}(S,S^{\prime})\leq 0
.
However, if
x\in C(\mathbb{K},{\vphantom{I}}_{\tau}I)
satisfies

\partial x=V^{-g(S)}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}^{-}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(1)-V^{-g(S^{\prime})}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}^{-}_{S^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(1)

in
C(\mathbb{K},{\vphantom{I}}_{\tau}I)
, then
\partial x
might have some nonzero terms in
([0,m]\cap\mathbb{Z})\times\{-\tau\}
for
m>0
.
Hence
[V^{-g(S)}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}^{-}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(1)-V^{-g(S^{\prime})}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}^{-}_{S^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(1)]
might not be zero
in
H_{*}\left(C(\mathbb{K},{\vphantom{L}}_{\tau}L_{m})\right)
for
m>0
,
and this is what
\tau^{\prime}(S,S^{\prime})
measures.

The invariant
\tau^{+}(S,S^{\prime})
was inspired by the concordance invariant
\nu(K)
,
due to Ozsváth and Szabó
[
42
]
*Definition 9.1,
which gives an improved 4-ball genus bound over
\tau(K)
by at most 1. We now extract
an analogue of
\nu
from
\tau^{+}
for pairs of surfaces in
\Surf(K)
,
though there is some information lost when doing this as

\tau^{\prime}
can take any value in
\bar{\mathbb{Z}}^{\leq 0}
.

Definition 4.10
.

Let
K
be a knot in
\mathbb{S}^{3}
and
S
,
S^{\prime}\in\Surf(K)
. Then let

\nu(S,S^{\prime})=\begin{cases}\tau(S,S^{\prime})&\text{if }\tau^{\prime}(S,S^{\prime})=-\infty,\\
\tau(S,S^{\prime})+1&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

We will see that
\nu
gives a lower bound on the stabilization and double point
distances in Proposition
6.8
.

4.5.
A sequence of local
h
-invariants

Let
K
be a knot in
\mathbb{S}^{3}
and
S
,
S^{\prime}\in\Surf_{g}(K)
.
Modeled on the invariants
V_{k}(K)
of large surgeries from knot Floer
homology, also referred to as Rasmussen’s
local
h
-invariants

[
44
]
, we describe a sequence of integer invariant
V_{k}(S,S^{\prime})

for
k\geq g
, such that

V_{g}(S,S^{\prime})\geq V_{g+1}(S,S^{\prime})\geq\cdots\geq 0,

and such that
V_{k}(S,S^{\prime})=0
for
k
sufficiently large.

Definition 4.11
.

Let
\mathbb{K}=(K,w,z)
be a doubly-based knot in
\mathbb{S}^{3}
, and let
S
,
S^{\prime}\in\Surf_{g}(K)
.
To define
V_{k}(S,S^{\prime})
, we consider the subcomplex

A_{k}^{-}(\mathbb{K}):=C(\mathbb{K},R_{0,-k})

of
\mathit{CFK}^{\infty}(\mathbb{K})
.
We think of the complexes
A_{k}^{-}(\mathbb{K})
as modules over the ring
\mathbb{F}_{2}[\widehat{U}]
, where
\widehat{U}=UV
.

The map
\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}
increases the Alexander grading by
g(S)
. If
k\geq g(S)
,
then
V^{-g(S)}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)
has Alexander grading
0
, and determines a
well-defined element of
H_{*}(A_{k}^{-}(\mathbb{K}))
. We define the invariant

V_{k}(S,S^{\prime}):=\min\left\{\,n\in\mathbb{N}:\widehat{U}^{n}\cdot[V^{-g}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}^{-}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(1)]=\widehat{U}^{n}\cdot[V^{-g}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}^{-}_{S^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(1)]\text{ in }H_{*}(A_{k}^{-}(\mathbb{K}))\,\right\}.

Remark 4.12
.

The above definition of
V_{k}
can be easily adapted to surfaces of different genera;
however, we specialize to the case when
g(S)=g(S^{\prime})
since our topological applications for
V_{k}

only hold when this is the case.

We now show that the invariants
V_{k}
of pairs of surfaces in
\Surf_{g}(K)
satisfy many of
the same properties as Rasmussen’s local
h
-invariants. The reader should
compare the following to
[
44
]
*Proposition 7.6:

Lemma 4.13
.

If
k\geq g
, then

V_{k}(S,S^{\prime})\geq V_{k+1}(S,S^{\prime})\geq V_{k}(S,S^{\prime})-1.

Proof.

There is a natural, grading-preserving inclusion of chain complexes

i_{k}\colon A_{k}^{-}(\mathbb{K})\hookrightarrow A_{k+1}^{-}(\mathbb{K}),

which becomes an isomorphism on homology after we invert
\widehat{U}
, and satisfies

(i_{k})_{*}([V^{-g}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)])=[V^{-g}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)].

Hence

V_{k+1}(S,S^{\prime})\leq V_{k}(S,S^{\prime}).

Multiplication by
\widehat{U}
induces a
-2
-graded inclusion
A_{k+1}^{-}(\mathbb{K})\hookrightarrow A_{k}^{-}(\mathbb{K})
of chain complexes,
which becomes an isomorphism on homology after we invert
\widehat{U}
. The map sends

[V^{-g}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(1)]\in H_{*}(A_{k+1}^{-}(\mathbb{K}))

to
\widehat{U}\cdot[V^{-g}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)]\in H_{*}(A_{k}^{-}(\mathbb{K}))
, and similarly for
S^{\prime}
. Hence, if

\begin{split}\widehat{U}^{n}\cdot[V^{-g}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)]&=\widehat{U}^{n}\cdot[V^{-g}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)]\text{ in }H_{*}(A_{k+1}^{-}(\mathbb{K}))\text{, then}\\
\widehat{U}^{n+1}\cdot[V^{-g}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)]&=\widehat{U}^{n+1}\cdot[V^{-g}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)]\text{ in }H_{*}(A_{k}^{-}(\mathbb{K})).\end{split}

We conclude that
V_{k}(S,S^{\prime})\leq V_{k+1}(S,S^{\prime})+1
.
∎

The reader should compare the following to
[
44
]
*Proposition 7.7:

Lemma 4.14
.

If
S
,
S^{\prime}\in\Surf_{g}(K)
and
g\leq k<\tau(S,S^{\prime})
, then
0<V_{k}(S,S^{\prime})
.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.6
,
we can describe
\tau(S,S^{\prime})
as the minimal
n

such that
V^{n-g}\cdot[t_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)]=V^{n-g}\cdot[t_{S^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)]
in

\mathit{HFK}^{-}_{U=0}(\mathbb{K})=H_{*}\left(\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(\mathbb{K})\otimes\mathbb{F}_{2}[U,V]/(U)\right).

Note that multiplication by
V^{k}
determines an inclusion of chain complexes

A_{k}^{-}(\mathbb{K})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(\mathbb{K}),

which we compose with the natural map
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(\mathbb{K})\to\mathit{CFK}_{U=0}^{-}(\mathbb{K})
given by

\bm{\mathbf{x}}\mapsto\bm{\mathbf{x}}\otimes 1
, corresponding to setting
U=0
.
The induced map on homology sends
[V^{-g}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)]
,
[V^{-g}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)]\in H_{*}(A_{k}^{-}(\mathbb{K}))
to

V^{k-g}\cdot[t_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)]
,
V^{k-g}\cdot[t_{S^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)]\in\mathit{HFK}^{-}_{U=0}(\mathbb{K})
, respectively.
If
g\leq k<\tau(S,S^{\prime})
, then

V^{k-g}\cdot[t_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)]\neq V^{k-g}\cdot[t_{S^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)]\text{ in }\mathit{HFK}^{-}_{U=0}(\mathbb{K}),

and consequently,

[V^{-g}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)]\neq[V^{-g}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)]\text{ in }H_{*}(A_{k}^{-}(\mathbb{K})).

Hence
V_{k}(S,S^{\prime})>0
, as claimed.
∎

4.6.
The upsilon invariant

Let
K
be a knot in
\mathbb{S}^{3}
, and let
S
,
S^{\prime}\in\Surf(K)
.
We now describe our invariant

\Upsilon_{(S,S^{\prime})}\colon[0,2]\to\mathbb{R}^{\geq 0}.

It is a secondary version of Ozsváth, Stipsicz, and Szabó’s
[
33
]
invariant
\Upsilon_{K}(t)
.

We recall the
t
-modified
version of knot Floer homology, described
by Ozsváth, Stipsicz, and Szabó. Suppose that
t=\frac{m}{n}\in[0,2]
is a
rational number with
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
relatively prime.
We define
\mathit{tCFK}^{-}(\mathbb{K})
to be the free
\mathbb{F}_{2}[v^{1/n}]
-module generated by
\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\beta}
, where
v
is a formal variable.
Similarly, let
\mathit{tCFK}^{\infty}(\mathbb{K})
be the free
\mathbb{F}_{2}[v^{1/n},v^{-1/n}]
-module generated
by
\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\beta}
. The modules
\mathit{tCFK}^{-}(\mathbb{K})
and
\mathit{tCFK}^{\infty}(\mathbb{K})
are equipped with a
differential
\partial
that satisfies

\partial\bm{\mathbf{x}}:=\sum_{\bm{\mathbf{y}}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\beta}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\phi\in\pi_{2}(\bm{\mathbf{x}},\bm{\mathbf{y}})\\
\mu(\phi)=1\end{subarray}}\#\widehat{\mathcal{M}}(\phi)\cdot v^{tn_{z}(\phi)+(2-t)n_{w}(\phi)}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{y}}

for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\beta}
.

We note that
\mathit{tCFK}^{-}(\mathbb{K})
and
\mathit{tCFK}^{\infty}(\mathbb{K})
can easily be expressed in
terms of
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(\mathbb{K})
and
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{K})
, respectively, as we now describe.
We give
\mathbb{F}_{2}[v^{1/n}]
the structure of an
\mathbb{F}_{2}[U,V]
-module, where

U
acts by
v^{2-t}
and
V
acts by
v^{t}
. With this action, we have
a canonical isomorphism

\mathit{tCFK}^{-}(\mathbb{K})\cong\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(\mathbb{K})\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{2}[U,V]}\mathbb{F}_{2}[v^{1/n}],

as well as a similar isomorphism involving
\mathit{tCFK}^{\infty}(\mathbb{K})
and

\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{K})
. Note that, in particular, if
(W,\mathcal{F})\colon(\mathbb{S}^{3},\mathbb{K}_{1})\to(\mathbb{S}^{3},\mathbb{K}_{2})
is a decorated link cobordism, then
the map
F_{W,\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{s}}
determines a
t
-modified version

\mathit{tF}_{W,\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{s}}:=F_{W,\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{s}}\otimes\id_{\mathbb{F}_{2}[v^{1/n}]}\colon\mathit{tCFK}^{-}(\mathbb{K}_{1})\to\mathit{tCFK}^{-}(\mathbb{K}_{2}).

Finally, we note that there is a
t
-grading on
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(\mathbb{K})
, defined via the formula

\gr_{t}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}):=\frac{t}{2}\cdot\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(\bm{\mathbf{x}})+\left(1-\frac{t}{2}\right)\cdot\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}(\bm{\mathbf{x}}).

This induces a well-defined grading on
\mathit{tCFK}^{-}(\mathbb{K})
,
for which we also write
\gr_{t}
. With respect to
\gr_{t}
, the variable
v
is
-1
graded.

If
S\in\Surf_{g}(K)
and
S^{\prime}\in\Surf_{g^{\prime}}(K)
, the invariants

\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}
and
\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}
admit
t
-modified versions

\mathit{t}\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}
and
\mathit{t}\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}
, respectively. Furthermore,
the elements
\mathit{t}\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)
and
\mathit{t}\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)
for
1\in\mathbb{F}_{2}[v^{1/n}]

have
\gr_{t}
-grading
-t\cdot g
and
-t\cdot g^{\prime}
, respectively.

Definition 4.15
.

For
t=\frac{m}{n}\in[0,2]
, we define

\Upsilon_{(S,S^{\prime})}(t):=\min\{\,s=k/n\geq\max\{t\cdot g,t\cdot g^{\prime}\}:v^{s-t\cdot g}\cdot[\mathit{t}\bm{\mathbf{t}}^{-}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(1)]=v^{s-t\cdot g^{\prime}}\cdot[\mathit{t}\bm{\mathbf{t}}^{-}_{S^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(1)]\in\mathit{tHFK}^{-}(\mathbb{K})\,\}.

There is an alternate definition of the invariant
\Upsilon_{(S,S^{\prime})}(t)
, which is more amenable to computations, and is based on Livingston’s description of the corresponding knot invariant
[
27
]
.
If
t\in[0,2]
, there is a filtration
\mathcal{G}_{s}^{t}(\mathbb{K})
of
\mathit{CFK}^{\infty}(\mathbb{K})
which is indexed by a parameter
s\in\mathbb{R}
. The set
\mathcal{G}_{s}^{t}(\mathbb{K})
is the
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-module generated by monomials
U^{i}V^{j}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{x}}
with
A(\bm{\mathbf{x}})+j-i=0
and

t\cdot j+(2-t)\cdot i\geq-s.

If
s\geq t\cdot g(S)
, then it is straightforward to see that
[V^{-g(S)}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)]
is a well-defined element of
H_{*}(\mathcal{G}_{s}^{t}(\mathbb{K}))
. It is not hard to adapt
[
27
]
*Section 14.1 to establish the following:

Lemma 4.16
.

If
S\in\Surf_{g}(K)
and
S^{\prime}\in\Surf_{g^{\prime}}(K)
, then

\Upsilon_{(S,S^{\prime})}(t)=\min\left\{s\geq t\cdot\max\{g,g^{\prime}\}:[V^{-g}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)]=[V^{-g^{\prime}}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)]\text{ in }H_{*}(\mathcal{G}_{s}^{t}(\mathbb{K}))\right\}.

4.7.
The kappa and kappa-nought invariants

If
\mathbb{K}=(K,w,z)
is a doubly-based knot in
\mathbb{S}^{3}
, let
\mathit{CFK}^{-}_{U=0}(\mathbb{K})

and
\mathit{CFK}^{\infty}_{U=0}(\mathbb{K})
denote the small minus and infinity knot Floer complexes
described in Section
4.1
.

Lemma 4.17
.

If
g(S)>0
, then

[t_{S,\bm{\mathbf{w}}}^{\infty}(1)]=0\in\mathit{HFK}^{\infty}_{U=0}(\mathbb{K}).

Proof.

For
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, let
\mathit{CFK}^{\infty}_{U=0}(\mathbb{K})_{n}
denote the subspace of
\mathit{CFK}^{\infty}_{U=0}(\mathbb{K})
in Alexander grading
n
.
Explicitly, the subspace
\mathit{CFK}^{\infty}_{U=0}(\mathbb{K})_{n}
is generated by monomials of the form
V^{i}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{x}}
, where

A(\bm{\mathbf{x}})+i=n.

We define the reduction map

R_{w}^{n}\colon\mathit{CFK}^{\infty}_{U=0}(\mathbb{K})_{n}\to\widehat{\mathit{CF}}(\mathbb{S}^{3},w),

by the formula
R_{w}^{n}(V^{i}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{x}})=\bm{\mathbf{x}}
. It is straightforward to see that

R_{w}^{n}
is a chain map. Furthermore, since the differential on

\mathit{CFK}^{\infty}_{U=0}(\mathbb{K})
preserves the Alexander grading, the map
R_{w}^{n}
is
a chain isomorphism. Consider the chain isomorphism

R_{w}:=\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}R_{w}^{n}\colon\mathit{CFK}^{\infty}_{U=0}(\mathbb{K})\to\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\widehat{\mathit{CF}}(\mathbb{S}^{3},w).

In particular, since
\widehat{\mathit{HF}}(\mathbb{S}^{3},w)
is supported in
\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
-grading 0, it follow that
\mathit{HFK}^{\infty}_{U=0}(\mathbb{K})
is as well. The map
\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{w}}}^{\infty}(1)
has
\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
-grading
-2g(S)
by the grading formula in
[
57
]
*Theorem 1.4. It follows that

[\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{w}}}^{\infty}(1)]=0\in\mathit{HFK}^{\infty}_{U=0}(\mathbb{K}),

completing the proof.
∎

Definition 4.18
.

Let
\mathbb{K}=(K,w,z)
be a doubly-based knot in
\mathbb{S}^{3}
, and let
S\in\Surf_{g}(K)
for
g>0
.
Then we let

\kappa_{0}(S):=\min\left\{\,n\geq g:V^{n-g}\cdot[t_{S,\bm{\mathbf{w}}}^{-}(1)]=0\text{ in }\mathit{HFK}^{-}_{U=0}(\mathbb{K})\,\right\}.

If
S\in\Surf_{0}(K)
, we set
\kappa_{0}(S)=0
.

We note that the element
\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{w}}}^{-}(1)
lives in Alexander grading
-g
. Hence

\kappa_{0}(S)=g+\min\left\{\,k\in\mathbb{N}:[\widehat{t}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{w}}}(1)]=0\text{ in }H_{*}\left(\widehat{\mathit{CFK}}{}^{\mathit{fil},z}_{k-g}(\mathbb{K})\right)\,\right\}.

Definition 4.19
.

If
g>0
and
S
,
S^{\prime}\in\Surf_{g}(K)
, we define the invariant

\kappa(S,S^{\prime}):=\min\left\{\,n\geq g:V^{n-g}\cdot[t^{-}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{w}}}(1)]=V^{n-g}\cdot[t^{-}_{S^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{w}}}(1)]\text{ in }\mathit{HFK}^{-}_{U=0}(\mathbb{K})\,\right\}.

Note that

\kappa(S,S^{\prime})\leq\max\{\kappa_{0}(S),\kappa_{0}(S^{\prime})\}.

We emphasize that the invariant
\tau(S,S^{\prime})
is defined
in terms of the maps
t_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}
and
t_{S^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}
,
while
\kappa(S,S^{\prime})
is defined in terms of the maps
t_{S,\bm{\mathbf{w}}}^{-}
and
t_{S^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{w}}}^{-}
.
Also, unlike the invariant
\tau(S,S^{\prime})
, the
definition of
\kappa(S,S^{\prime})
only makes sense if
g(S)=g(S^{\prime})>0
.

4.8.
Upsilon near
0
and
2

Ozsváth, Stipsicz, and Szabó
[
33
]
*Proposition 1.6 proved that the knot invariant

\Upsilon_{K}(t)=-\tau(K)\cdot t
near
t=0
. In this section, we prove a
similar result for
\Upsilon_{(S,S^{\prime})}(t)
.

Theorem 4.20
.

Suppose that
S
,
S^{\prime}\in\Surf(K)
. For all
t\in[0,2]
sufficiently close to 0, we have

\Upsilon_{(S,S^{\prime})}(t)=\tau(S,S^{\prime})\cdot t.

For
t
sufficiently close to
2
, we have

\Upsilon_{(S,S^{\prime})}(t)=\begin{cases}(\kappa_{0}(S)-g(S))\cdot(2-t)+g(S)\cdot t&\text{ if }g(S)>g(S^{\prime}),\\
(\kappa(S,S^{\prime})-g(S))\cdot(2-t)+g(S)\cdot t&\text{ if }g(S)=g(S^{\prime}).\end{cases}

Proof.

The argument we present is an adaptation of Livingston’s proof of the
analogous fact
[
27
]
*Theorem 13.1 for the knot invariants
\Upsilon_{K}(t)
,
and we use the reformulation of

\Upsilon_{(S,S^{\prime})}(t)
in terms of filtrations on
\mathit{CFK}^{\infty}
from
Lemma
4.16
. We focus on
\Upsilon_{(S,S^{\prime})}(t)
near
t=2
when

g(S)>g(S^{\prime})
, since the other cases are straightforward adaptations of this.
Let us write
g=g(S)
and
g^{\prime}=g(S^{\prime})
.

Define the following sub-quotient shapes of
\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}:

\displaystyle H_{-g+1}

\displaystyle:=\{\,(i,j):j\geq-g+1\,\},

\displaystyle T_{-g,k}

\displaystyle:=\{\,(i,j):i\geq k\text{, }j=-g\,\}

\displaystyle Z_{-g,k}

\displaystyle:=H_{-g+1}\cup T_{-g,k}.

Figure 4.2.
Examples of the sub-quotient shapes
H_{-g+1}
,
Z_{-g,k}
, and
T_{-g,k}
of
\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}
.

It is straightforward to see that, for
t
sufficient close to
2
, the
complex
\mathcal{G}_{s}^{t}(\mathbb{K})
is always equal to
C(\mathbb{K},Z_{i,j})
for some
i
,
j
.
Hence
[V^{-g}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)]=[V^{-g^{\prime}}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}]\in H_{*}(\mathcal{G}_{s}^{t}(\mathbb{K}))
if and only if

C(\mathbb{K},Z_{-g,m})\subseteq\mathcal{G}_{s}^{t}(\mathbb{K}),

where

m:=\max\left\{\,k\leq 0:[V^{-g}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)]=[V^{-g^{\prime}}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}^{-}_{S^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(1)]\text{ in }H_{*}(C(\mathbb{K},Z_{-g,k}))\,\right\}.

Consequently, an easy computation shows that

(4.7)

\Upsilon_{(S,S^{\prime})}(t)=-m(2-t)+gt,

for
t
near
2
.

There is a short exact sequence of chain complexes

(4.8)

0\to C(\mathbb{K},H_{-g+1})\xrightarrow{i}C(\mathbb{K},Z_{-g,k})\xrightarrow{q}C(\mathbb{K},T_{-g,k})\to 0.

Since
H_{*}(C(\mathbb{K},T_{-g,k}))
is a torsion
\mathbb{F}_{2}[\widehat{U}]
-module (in fact,
\widehat{U}
has vanishing action), while

H_{*}(C(\mathbb{K},H_{-g+1}))
is torsion-free (in fact, isomorphic to
\mathbb{F}_{2}[\widehat{U}]
),
the connecting homomorphism of the
long exact sequence associated to equation (
4.8
) vanishes.
Consequently, there is a short exact sequence

(4.9)

0\to H_{*}(C(\mathbb{K},H_{-g+1}))\xrightarrow{i}H_{*}(C(\mathbb{K},Z_{-g,k}))\xrightarrow{q}H_{*}(C(\mathbb{K},T_{-g,k}))\to 0.

Furthermore, since
H_{*}(C(\mathbb{K},H_{-g+1}))\cong\mathbb{F}_{2}[\widehat{U}]
, it follows that

q
is injective on the torsion submodule of
H_{*}(C(\mathbb{K},Z_{-g,k}))
.
Since
[V^{-g}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)]+[V^{-g^{\prime}}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)]
is a torsion element of
H_{*}(C(\mathbb{K},Z_{-g,k}))
, it
follows that
[V^{-g}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)+V^{-g^{\prime}}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)]

is zero in
H_{*}(C(\mathbb{K},Z_{-g,k}))
if and only if its image under
q
is zero
in
H_{*}(C(\mathbb{K},T_{-g,k}))
. Consequently,

(4.10)

m=\max\left\{k\leq 0:[V^{-g}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}^{-}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(1)]=[V^{-g^{\prime}}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}^{-}_{S^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(1)]\text{ in }H_{*}(C(\mathbb{K},T_{-g,k}))\right\}.

Note that if
g>g^{\prime}
, then
[V^{-g^{\prime}}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}^{-}_{S^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}]=0
as an element of
H_{*}(C(\mathbb{K},T_{-g,k}))
,
so equation (
4.10
) implies that

(4.11)

m=\max\left\{k\leq 0:[V^{-g}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}^{-}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(1)]=0\text{ in }H_{*}(C(\mathbb{K},T_{-g,k}))\right\}.

Next, we note that multiplication by
\widehat{U}^{g}
induces a chain isomorphism
between
C(\mathbb{K},T_{-g,k})
and
C(\mathbb{K},T_{0,k+g})
. The group

C(\mathbb{K},T_{0,k+g})
is the
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-module generated by intersection points

\bm{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathbb{T}_{\alpha}\cap\mathbb{T}_{\beta}
with
A(\bm{\mathbf{x}})\geq k+g
. The differential on

C(\mathbb{K},T_{0,k+g})
counts holomorphic disks which are allowed to go over
w
,
but not
z
. This is simply the subcomplex
\widehat{\mathit{CFK}}{}^{\mathit{fil},w}_{k+g}(\mathbb{K})\subseteq\widehat{\mathit{CFK}}{}^{\mathit{fil},w}(\mathbb{K})
.
Applying the conjugation symmetry of knot Floer homology to
equation (
4.11
) implies that

(4.12)

\begin{split}m&=\max\left\{k\leq 0:[U^{g}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}^{-}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(1)]=0\text{ in }H_{*}(C(\mathbb{K},T_{0,k+g}))\right\}\\
&=-\min\left\{k\geq 0:[V^{g}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{w}}}^{-}(1)]=0\text{ in }H_{*}(\widehat{\mathit{CFK}}{}^{\mathit{fil},z}_{k-g}(\mathbb{K}))\right\}\\
&=g-\kappa_{0}(S).\end{split}

Equations (
4.7
) and (
4.12
) together imply that, for
t
near 2, we have

\Upsilon_{(S,S^{\prime})}(t)=(\kappa_{0}(S)-g)\cdot(2-t)+g\cdot t.

Similar arguments apply when
g=g^{\prime}
, and for
t
close to 0.
∎

4.9.
Further properties of the secondary invariants

Our secondary invariants satisfy a monotonicity condition with respect
to stacking link cobordisms.

Proposition 4.21
.

Suppose that
(I\times\mathbb{S}^{3},S)
is a link cobordism from
(\mathbb{S}^{3},K_{0})

to
(\mathbb{S}^{3},K_{1})
, and
S_{0}
,
S_{0}^{\prime}\in\Surf(K_{0})
.
Let
S_{1}
,
S_{1}^{\prime}\in\Surf(K_{1})
be the surfaces obtained by
stacking
S_{0}
or
S_{0}^{\prime}
and
S
, respectively. Then

\tau(S_{0},S_{0}^{\prime})+g(S)\geq\tau(S_{1},S_{1}^{\prime}).

When
g(S_{0})=g(S_{0}^{\prime})
, the invariant
\kappa
satisfies an analogous inequality; furthermore,

V_{k}(S_{0},S_{0}^{\prime})\geq V_{k+g(S)}(S_{1},S_{1}^{\prime})

for
k\geq g(S_{0})
.
Finally,

\Upsilon_{(S_{0},S_{0}^{\prime})}(t)+(1-|1-t|)\cdot g(S)\geq\Upsilon_{(S_{1},S_{1}^{\prime})}(t),

for any
S_{0}
,
S_{0}^{\prime}\in\Surf(K)
and
t\in[0,2]
.

Proof.

Choose basepoints
w_{i}
and
z_{i}
on
K_{i}
for
i\in\{0,1\}
,
and a decoration
\mathcal{A}
on
S
such that
S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
is a strip
containing
w_{0}
and
w_{1}
.
Using the functoriality of the link cobordism maps,
it sends
\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S_{0},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{\infty}(1)
to
\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S_{1},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{\infty}(1)

and
\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S_{0}^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{\infty}(1)
to
\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S_{1}^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{\infty}(1)
. Furthermore, by
[
57
, Theorem 1.4]
, the map
F_{I\times\mathbb{S}^{3},(S,\mathcal{A})}

increases the Alexander grading by
g(S)
. From these two facts, all claims can be proven quickly.
∎

A concordance
\mathcal{C}
from
K_{0}
to
K_{1}
is called
invertible

if there is a concordance
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
from
K_{1}
to
K_{0}
such that
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\circ\mathcal{C}

is the identity cobordism from
K_{0}
to itself; see Sumners
[
49
]
.

Corollary 4.22
.

Suppose that
K_{0}
and
K_{1}
are knots in
\mathbb{S}^{3}
and
S_{0}
,
S_{0}^{\prime}\in\Surf(K_{0})
.
If
\mathcal{C}
is an invertible concordance from
K_{0}
to
K_{1}
,
let
S_{1}
and
S_{1}^{\prime}
denote the surfaces in
\Surf(K_{1})

obtained by stacking
S_{0}
or
S_{0}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{C}
, respectively. Then

\omega(S_{0},S_{0}^{\prime})=\omega(S_{1},S_{1}^{\prime})

for
\omega\in\{\tau,\Upsilon\}
.
If
g(S_{0})=g(S_{0}^{\prime})
, then the same equality holds for
\omega\in\{V_{k},\kappa\}
, provided
k\geq g
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
be a left inverse of
\mathcal{C}
.
We first apply Proposition
4.21

to the surfaces
S_{0}
,
S_{0}^{\prime}
, and to the concordance
\mathcal{C}
,
to obtain that
\omega(S_{0},S_{0}^{\prime})\geq\omega(S_{1},S_{1}^{\prime})
.
Note that we recover
S_{0}
if we stack
S_{1}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
,
and
S_{0}^{\prime}
if we stack
S_{1}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
.
Hence, if we apply Proposition
4.21

to the surfaces
S_{1}
,
S_{1}^{\prime}
, and to the concordance
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
,
we obtain that
\omega(S_{1},S_{1}^{\prime})\geq\omega(S_{0},S_{0}^{\prime})
.
∎

Like
\mu_{\st}
and
\mu_{\Sing}
, our secondary invariants satisfy
the following ultrametric inequality:

Proposition 4.23
.

If
K
is a knot in
\mathbb{S}^{3}
and
S_{1}
,
S_{2}
,
S_{3}\in\Surf(K)
, then

\omega(S_{1},S_{3})\leq\max\{\,\omega(S_{1},S_{2}),\omega(S_{2},S_{3})\,\}

for
\omega\in\{\tau,\Upsilon\}
, where the inequality is to be
taken pointwise when
\omega=\Upsilon
.
If
g(S_{1})=g(S_{2})=g(S_{3})
, then the
inequality holds with
\omega\in\{V_{k},\kappa\}
, as well.

Proof.

All of the invariants are described in terms of when two distinguished
elements become equal in homology, after multiplying by some power of
V
,
\widehat{U}
, or
v
.
∎

Lemma 4.24
.

Let
K
be a knot in
\mathbb{S}^{3}
, and suppose
S_{1}
,
S_{2}
,
S_{3}\in\Surf(K)
.
If we endow
\mathbb{N}\times\bar{\mathbb{Z}}^{\leq 0}
with the lexicographic ordering,
then the map
\tau^{+}
satisfies the ultrametric inequality

\tau^{+}(S_{1},S_{3})\leq\max\{\tau^{+}(S_{1},S_{2}),\tau^{+}(S_{2},S_{3})\}.

Proof.

For
i
,
j\in\{1,2,3\}
, let us write
\tau_{ij}=\tau(S_{i},S_{j})
and
\tau^{\prime}_{ij}=\tau^{\prime}(S_{i},S_{j})
,
and let

\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{i}:=V^{-g(S_{i})}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S_{i},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1).

Without loss of generality, we can suppose that
\tau_{12}\leq\tau_{23}
.
By Proposition
4.23
, we have
\tau_{13}\leq\max\{\tau_{12},\tau_{23}\}=\tau_{23}
.
As we have endowed
\mathbb{N}\times\bar{\mathbb{Z}}^{\leq 0}
with the lexicographic ordering,
it suffices to consider the case when
\tau_{13}=\tau_{23}
, which we denote by
\tau
,
as otherwise
\tau^{+}(S_{1},S_{3})<\tau^{+}(S_{2},S_{3})
.

Choose basepoints
w
and
z
on
K
, and write
\mathbb{K}=(K,w,z)
.
First, assume that
\tau_{12}<\tau
. By the definition of
\tau_{12}
, there
exists
x\in C(\mathbb{K},{\vphantom{I}}_{\tau_{12}}I)
such that
\partial x=\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{1}-\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{2}
.
If we write
m:=-\tau^{\prime}_{23}
, by definition, there is an
y\in C(\mathbb{K},{\vphantom{L}}_{\tau}L_{m})
such that

\partial y=\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{2}-\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{3}
. Since
\tau_{12}<\tau
and
\partial
respects the Alexander filtration,
there is an inclusion of complexes

\iota\colon C(\mathbb{K},{\vphantom{I}}_{\tau_{12}}I)\to C(\mathbb{K},{\vphantom{L}}_{\tau}L_{m}).

It follows that
\iota(x)+y\in C(\mathbb{K},{\vphantom{L}}_{\tau}L_{m})
satisfies

\partial(\iota(x)+y)=\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{1}-\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{3}
. As
\tau_{13}=\tau
,
it follows that
-\tau^{\prime}_{13}\geq m
, hence
\tau^{\prime}_{13}\leq\tau^{\prime}_{23}

and
\tau^{+}(S_{1},S_{3})\leq\tau^{+}(S_{2},S_{3})
.

Now suppose that
\tau_{12}=\tau
. If we write
m^{\prime}:=-\tau^{\prime}_{12}
and
m:=-\tau^{\prime}_{23}
,
then there exist
x\in C(\mathbb{K},{\vphantom{L}}_{\tau}L_{m^{\prime}})
and
y\in C(\mathbb{K},{\vphantom{L}}_{\tau}L_{m})
such that

\partial x=\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{1}-\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{2}
and
\partial y=\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{2}-\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{3}
.
Without loss of generality, we can assume that
m^{\prime}\geq m
. Then there is a natural projection

\pi\colon C(\mathbb{K},{\vphantom{L}}_{\tau}L_{m^{\prime}})\to C(\mathbb{K},{\vphantom{L}}_{\tau}L_{m})

that is a chain map, and preserves the elements
\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2,3\}
.
Since
x+\pi(y)\in C({\vphantom{L}}_{\tau}L_{m})

and
\partial(x+\pi(y))=\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{1}-\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{3}
, we have
-\tau^{\prime}_{13}\geq m
,
hence
\tau^{\prime}_{13}\leq\tau^{\prime}_{23}
and
\tau^{+}(S_{1},S_{3})\leq\tau^{+}(S_{2},S_{3})
.
∎

5.
Link Floer homology and the stabilization distance

In this section we prove our main technical results about stabilizations and the link Floer TQFT,
and show that our invariants
\tau
and
V_{k}
give lower bounds on
\mu_{\st}
,
while
\kappa_{0}
and
\mathcal{I}
give lower bounds on
g_{\dest}
.

5.1.
Algebraic reduction

In this section, we consider the relation between the Heegaard Floer homology of multi-pointed 3-manifolds and the link Floer homology of unlinks.

There are two natural ways to reduce
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}
to
\mathit{CF}^{-}
via a tensor product. Let
M_{V=1}
denote the
(\mathbb{F}[U,V],\mathbb{F}[\widehat{U}])
-bimodule with underlying vector space
\mathbb{F}[\widehat{U}]
, where
U
acts on the left by
\widehat{U}
, and
V
acts by 1. We have
\widehat{U}
act on the right by ordinary multiplication. There is also an
(\mathbb{F}[U,V],\mathbb{F}[\widehat{U}])
-bimodule
M_{U=1}
with underlying vector space
\mathbb{F}[\widehat{U}]
, defined similarly, except that we have
V
act on the left by
\widehat{U}
and we have
U
act by
1
.

There are canonical isomorphisms

\begin{split}\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(Y,\mathbb{L},\mathfrak{s})\otimes_{\mathbb{F}[U,V]}M_{V=1}&\cong\mathit{CF}^{-}(Y,\bm{\mathbf{w}},\mathfrak{s})\\
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(Y,\mathbb{L},\mathfrak{s})\otimes_{\mathbb{F}[U,V]}M_{U=1}&\cong\mathit{CF}^{-}(Y,\bm{\mathbf{z}},\mathfrak{s}-\mathit{PD}[L]).\end{split}

These isomorphisms are obtained by taking any Heegaard diagram for
(Y,\mathbb{L})
, and ignoring the
\bm{\mathbf{z}}
basepoints, or ignoring the
\bm{\mathbf{w}}
basepoints.

In particular, for any
\mathbb{F}[U,V]
-equivariant map
F
from
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(Y_{1},\mathbb{L}_{1},\mathfrak{s}_{1})
to
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(Y_{2},\mathbb{L}_{2},\mathfrak{s}_{2})
, we obtain a map
F|_{V=1}
from
\mathit{CF}^{-}(Y_{1},\bm{\mathbf{w}}_{1},\mathfrak{s}_{1})
to
\mathit{CF}^{-}(Y_{2},\bm{\mathbf{w}}_{2},\mathfrak{s}_{2})
. There is also a map
F|_{U=1}
from
\mathit{CF}^{-}(Y_{1},\bm{\mathbf{z}}_{1},\mathfrak{s}_{1}-\mathit{PD}[L_{1}])
to
\mathit{CF}^{-}(Y_{2},\bm{\mathbf{z}}_{2},\mathfrak{s}_{2}-\mathit{PD}[L_{2}])
.

An important special case is when
\mathbb{L}
is an unlink, and each link component has exactly two basepoints. We say that a diagram for
(Y,\mathbb{L})
is a
minimal unlink diagram
if each
\bm{\mathbf{w}}
basepoint occurs in the same component of
\Sigma\setminus(\bm{\mathbf{\alpha}}\cup\bm{\mathbf{\beta}})
as a
\bm{\mathbf{z}}
basepoint. In this case, a Seifert disk is canonically specified by picking a collection of arcs in
\Sigma\setminus(\bm{\mathbf{\alpha}}\cup\bm{\mathbf{\beta}})
which connects each
\bm{\mathbf{w}}
basepoint to a
\bm{\mathbf{z}}
basepoint. By pushing the interiors of these arcs off of
\Sigma
, in both directions, a collection of Seifert disks for
\mathbb{L}
is spanned. In particular, there is a canonical Seifert surface
S
of
\mathbb{L}
which is determined by the diagram, and
A_{S}(U^{i}V^{j}\bm{\mathbf{x}})=j-i
for all intersection points
\bm{\mathbf{x}}
.

Additionally, in the case of a minimal unlink diagram
\mathcal{H}=(\Sigma,\bm{\mathbf{\alpha}},\bm{\mathbf{\beta}},\bm{\mathbf{w}},\bm{\mathbf{z}})
, there is a canonical isomorphism

(5.1)

\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(\Sigma,\bm{\mathbf{\alpha}},\bm{\mathbf{\beta}},\bm{\mathbf{w}},\bm{\mathbf{z}},\mathfrak{s})\cong\mathit{CF}^{-}(\Sigma,\bm{\mathbf{\alpha}},\bm{\mathbf{\beta}},\bm{\mathbf{w}},\mathfrak{s})\otimes_{\mathbb{F}[\widehat{U}]}\mathbb{F}[U,V],

where we view
\widehat{U}
as acting on
\mathbb{F}[U,V]
via the product
UV
.

In particular, if we are given minimal unlink diagrams for
(Y_{1},\mathbb{L}_{1})
and
(Y_{2},\mathbb{L}_{2})
as well as an
\mathbb{F}[U,V]
-equivariant map
F
from
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(Y_{1},\mathbb{L}_{1},\mathfrak{s}_{1})
to
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(Y_{2},\mathbb{L}_{2},\mathfrak{s}_{2})
, we may view
F|_{U=1}\otimes\id_{\mathbb{F}[U,V]}
and
F|_{V=1}\otimes\id_{\mathbb{F}[U,V]}
as also being maps from
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(Y_{1},\mathbb{L}_{1},\mathfrak{s}_{1})
to
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(Y_{2},\mathbb{L}_{2},\mathfrak{s}_{2})
. For our purposes, it is useful to compare these maps to the original map
F
:

Lemma 5.1
.

Suppose that
\mathbb{L}_{1}\subseteq Y_{1}
and
\mathbb{L}_{2}\subseteq Y_{2}
are unlinks, and pick minimal unlink diagrams for
(Y_{1},\mathbb{L}_{1})
and
(Y_{2},\mathbb{L}_{2})
, respectively. Suppose that
F\colon\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(Y_{1},\mathbb{L}_{1},\mathfrak{s}_{1})\to\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(Y_{2},\mathbb{L}_{2},\mathfrak{s}_{2})
is an
\mathbb{F}[U,V]
-equivariant map, which is homogeneously graded with respect to the Alexander grading, and shifts the Alexander grading by
\Delta
.

(1)

If
\Delta\geq 0
, then

F=V^{\Delta}\cdot(F|_{V=1}\otimes\id_{\mathbb{F}[U,V]})\quad\text{and}\quad U^{\Delta}\cdot F=(F|_{U=1}\otimes\id_{\mathbb{F}[U,V]}).

(2)

If
\Delta\leq 0
, then

V^{-\Delta}\cdot F=(F|_{V=1}\otimes\id_{\mathbb{F}[U,V]})\quad\text{and}\quad F=U^{-\Delta}\cdot(F|_{U=1}\otimes\id_{\mathbb{F}[U,V]}).

Proof.

Consider the claim for
\Delta\geq 0
. In this case, the maps
F
and
F|_{V=1}\otimes\id_{\mathbb{F}[U,V]}
agree up to an overall power of
V
. Since
F|_{V=1}\otimes\id_{\mathbb{F}[U,V]}
preserves the Alexander grading, the overall power is
V^{\Delta}
. The same argument works for the other claims.
∎

Remark 5.2
.

Lemma
5.1
is stated using two fixed minimal unlink diagrams for
(Y_{1},\mathbb{L}_{1})
and
(Y_{2},\mathbb{L}_{2})
, so we do not claim that the map
F|_{V=1}\otimes\id_{\mathbb{F}[U,V]}
and
F|_{U=1}\otimes\id_{\mathbb{F}[U,V]}
are natural maps. We may view these maps as being natural if we fix a set of Seifert disks for
\mathbb{L}_{1}
and
\mathbb{L}_{2}
.

5.2.
Stabilizations and link Floer homology

In this section, we prove our main computational results about stabilizations
and link Floer homology.
Before we state our computational results, we recall that the link cobordism
maps admit extensions

F_{W,\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{s}}\colon\Lambda^{*}(H_{1}(W)/\Tors)\otimes\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(Y_{1},\mathbb{L}_{1},\mathfrak{s}|_{Y_{1}})\to\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(Y_{2},\mathbb{L}_{2},\mathfrak{s}|_{Y_{2}})

that incorporate the action of
\Lambda^{*}(H_{1}(W)/\Tors)
, similar to the
cobordism maps of Ozsváth and Szabó
[
40
]
; see

[
57
]
*Section 12.2 for a description.

If
F
and
G
are two maps from
\mathbb{F}_{2}[U,V]
to

\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(Y,\mathbb{L},\mathfrak{s})
, we say that
F\simeq G

modulo the action of
H_{1}(Y)
, and write

F\simeq G\mod H_{1}(Y),

if there are classes
[\gamma_{1}],\dots,[\gamma_{k}]\in H_{1}(Y)
, as well as
\mathbb{F}_{2}[U,V]
-equivariant maps
J_{1},\dots,J_{k}
from
\mathbb{F}_{2}[U,V]
to

\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(Y,\mathbb{L},\mathfrak{s})
, such that

F+G\simeq\sum_{i=1}^{k}A_{\gamma_{i}}\circ J_{i}.

(Note that if
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
are homologous 1-cycles in
Y
, then
A_{\gamma}\simeq A_{\gamma^{\prime}}
.)

Lemma 5.3
.

Suppose that
(W,\mathcal{F})\colon\emptyset\to(Y,\mathbb{U})
is a decorated link
cobordism from the empty set to a doubly-based unknot
\mathbb{U}
in
Y
, equipped
with a Seifert disk
D
, and let
\mathfrak{s}\in\Spin^{c}(W)
. Pick a Heegaard diagram for
(Y,\mathbb{U})
where the
w
and
z
basepoints are immediately adjacent.
Write
\mathcal{F}=(S,\mathcal{A})
, and suppose that
H_{1}(Y)\to H_{1}(W)
is a surjection.

(1)

Suppose
\mathcal{A}
has a single component (necessarily a non-closed arc),
and write

h(S\cup D,\mathfrak{s}):=\frac{\langle\,c_{1}(\mathfrak{s}),[S\cup D]\,\rangle-[S\cup D]\cdot[S\cup D]}{2}.

If
h(S\cup D,\mathfrak{s})+g(S_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}})-g(S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}})\geq 0
, then

F_{W,\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{s}}\simeq U^{g(S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}})}V^{g(S_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}})+h(S\cup D,\mathfrak{s})}\cdot F_{W,\mathfrak{s}}\otimes\id_{\mathbb{F}[U,V]}\mod H_{1}(Y),

with respect to the isomorphism from Equation (
5.1
).
If
h(S\cup D,\mathfrak{s})+g(S_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}})-g(S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}})\leq 0
, then

F_{W,\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{s}}\simeq U^{g(S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}})-h(S\cup D,\mathfrak{s})}V^{g(S_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}})}\cdot F_{W,\mathfrak{s}-\mathit{PD}[S]}\otimes\id_{\mathbb{F}[U,V]}\mod H_{1}(Y).

(2)

If
\mathcal{A}
has a closed component
\gamma
, then

F_{W,\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{s}}\simeq 0\mod H_{1}(Y).

Proof.

The proof is a modification of
[
57
]
*Proposition 9.7.
Consider first Claim (
1
), in the case when
h(S\cup D,\mathfrak{s})+g(S_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}})-g(S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}})\geq 0
. Note that the latter quantity is the
Alexander grading change of the map
F_{W,\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{s}}
. The reduction

F_{W,\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{s}}|_{V=1}
is computed explicitly in

[
58
]
*Theorem C, and depends only on
W
,
\mathfrak{s}
, and the
embedding of the subsurface
S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
in
W
. When
S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
is a connected
surface with a single boundary component, according to

[
57
]
*Lemma 9.6, the
V=1
reduction satisfies

(5.2)

F_{W,\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{s}}|_{V=1}\simeq F_{W,\mathfrak{s}}(\xi_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}\otimes-),

where
\xi_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}\in\Lambda^{*}(H_{1}(W)/\Tors)\otimes\mathbb{F}_{2}[\widehat{U}]
is an element
equal to
\widehat{U}^{g(S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}})}
modulo
H_{1}(W)
. Since
H_{1}(Y)

surjects onto
H_{1}(W)
, we can commute
\xi_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
with
F_{W,\mathfrak{s}}
to obtain the relation

F_{W,\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{s}}|_{V=1}\simeq\widehat{U}^{g(S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}})}\cdot F_{W,\mathfrak{s}}\mod H_{1}(Y).

Applying Lemma
5.1
, we conclude that

(5.3)

F_{W,\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{s}}\simeq V^{h(S\cup D,\mathfrak{s})+g(S_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}})-g(S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}})}\widehat{U}^{g(S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}})}\cdot F_{W,\mathfrak{s}}\otimes\id_{\mathbb{F}[U,V]}\mod H_{1}(Y).

Claim (
1
) in this case follows by rearranging equation (
5.3
)
using the fact that
\widehat{U}=UV
.

The argument fails when the Alexander grading
h(S\cup D,\mathfrak{s})+g(S_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}})-g(S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}})
is negative, since multiplication by

V^{h(S\cup D,\mathfrak{s})+g(S_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}})-g(S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}})}
is not a filtered map. Instead we
must consider the
U=1
reduction of
F_{W,\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{s}}
. According to

[
57
]
*Lemma 9.6, the
U=1
reduction satisfies

F_{W,\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{s}}|_{U=1}\simeq F_{W,\mathfrak{s}-\mathit{PD}[S]}(\xi_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}\otimes-),

for an element
\xi_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}\in\Lambda^{*}(H_{1}(W)/\Tors)\otimes\mathbb{F}_{2}[\widehat{U}]

equal to
\widehat{U}^{g(S_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}})}
modulo
H_{1}(W)
. Using this fact, the formula

F_{W,\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{s}}\simeq U^{g(S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}})-h(S\cup D,\mathfrak{s})}V^{g(S_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}})}\cdot(F_{W,\mathfrak{s}-\mathit{PD}[S]}\otimes\id_{\mathbb{F}[U,V]})\mod H_{1}(Y)

can be established by the same strategy as before.

Next, we consider Claim (
2
), where
\mathcal{A}
contains a closed component. As in
the proof of Claim (
1
), the key will be to consider the maps

F_{W,\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{s}}|_{V=1}
and
F_{W,\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{s}}|_{U=1}
.
Let
\Delta
denote the quantity

\Delta:=h(S\cup D,\mathfrak{s})+\frac{\chi(S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}})-\chi(S_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}})}{2},

which we note is the Alexander grading of the map
F_{W,\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{s}}
.

We first consider the case when
\Delta\geq 0
.
Let us write
C_{\bm{\mathbf{w}},0}
and
C_{\bm{\mathbf{z}},0}
for the components of
S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
and

S_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}
that intersect
\partial S
. We will reduce to the case when

\partial C_{\bm{\mathbf{w}},0}
contains a closed component disjoint from
\partial S
. If
C_{\bm{\mathbf{w}},0}=S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
,
then
\partial C_{\bm{\mathbf{w}},0}
trivially contains a closed component
disjoint from
\partial S
. If
C_{\bm{\mathbf{w}},0}
is not the only component of
S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
,
then, since
S
is connected, we can find a properly embedded path

\gamma_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}\colon I\to C_{\bm{\mathbf{z}},0}
, with both endpoints on
\mathcal{A}
, such that

\gamma_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(0)\in C_{\bm{\mathbf{w}},0}
, and
\gamma_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(1)
is a point in the
boundary of another component,
C_{\bm{\mathbf{w}},1}
of
S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
. There are four cases we consider:

(1)

C_{\bm{\mathbf{w}},1}
is planar, and
|\partial C_{\bm{\mathbf{w}},1}|=1
.

(2)

C_{\bm{\mathbf{w}},1}
is planar, and
|\partial C_{\bm{\mathbf{w}},1}|=2
.

(3)

C_{\bm{\mathbf{w}},1}
is planar, and
|\partial C_{\bm{\mathbf{w}},1}|>2
.

(4)

g(C_{\bm{\mathbf{w}},1})>0
.

In Case (
1
), the surface
C_{\bm{\mathbf{w}},1}
is topologically a disk, which is
necessarily disjoint from
\partial S
, since
|\mathcal{A}\cap\partial S|=2
. We claim that the
map
F_{W,\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{s}}\simeq 0
. Indeed, the cobordism map

F_{W,\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{s}}
can be factored through the composition of a
quasi-stabilization, followed by a quasi-destabilization, and such a
composition clearly vanishes.

We next consider Case (
2
), when
C_{\bm{\mathbf{w}},1}
is an annulus, which is
disjoint from
\partial S
. In this case, we also have
F_{W,\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{s}}\simeq 0
. To
see this, pick a properly embedded path
\gamma_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}\colon I\to C_{\bm{\mathbf{w}},1}

that connects the two boundary components of
C_{\bm{\mathbf{w}},1}
, and such that

\gamma_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}(0)=\gamma_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(1)
; see the top of Figure
5.1
.
We concatenate
\gamma_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}
and
\gamma_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
to get a path
\gamma
. A neighborhood of
\gamma
is the
domain of a bypass. Let
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
denote the other two dividing sets
in the bypass triple; see the bottom row of Figure
5.1
.
The bypass relation (relation (
3.12
) above and its interpretation in terms of decorated cobordisms from Figure
3.7
) implies that

(5.4)

F_{W,(S,\mathcal{A}),\mathfrak{s}}\simeq F_{W,(S,\mathcal{A}^{\prime}),\mathfrak{s}}+F_{W,(S,\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}),\mathfrak{s}}.

The key observation is that
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
are actually isotopic, so
equation (
5.4
) implies that
F_{W,(S,\mathcal{A}),\mathfrak{s}}\simeq 0
.
The isotopy between
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
is shown in Figure
5.1
.

Figure 5.1.
When
C_{\bm{\mathbf{w}},1}
is an annulus, the two other dividing sets in a bypass are isotopic.

We now consider Cases (
3
) and (
4
), when
C_{\bm{\mathbf{w}},1}
is
planar and
|\partial C_{\bm{\mathbf{w}},1}|>2
, or when
g(C_{\bm{\mathbf{w}},1})>0
, respectively. In
both cases, we let
\gamma_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}\colon I\to C_{\bm{\mathbf{w}},1}
be a properly
embedded curve which is non-separating and satisfies

\gamma_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(1)=\gamma_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}(0)
; see the top of Figure
5.2
.
If
g(C_{\bm{\mathbf{w}},1})>0
, we require that both
ends of
\gamma_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
are on the same component of
\partial C_{\bm{\mathbf{w}},1}
. We let

\gamma
denote the concatenation of
\gamma_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
and
\gamma_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}
. As in
Case (
2
), we consider the bypass triple obtained by taking a regular
neighborhood of the image of
\gamma
. Let
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
denote the
other two dividing sets in the bypass triple, shown on the bottom of Figure
5.2
.

Let
C_{\bm{\mathbf{w}},0}^{\prime}
and
C_{\bm{\mathbf{w}},0}^{\prime\prime}
denote the type-
\bm{\mathbf{w}}
subregions of

S\setminus\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
and
S\setminus\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
that intersect
\partial S
.
In Cases (
3
) and (
4
), it is easy to check that

\partial C_{\bm{\mathbf{w}},0}^{\prime}
and
\partial C_{\bm{\mathbf{w}},0}^{\prime\prime}
both contain a closed curve disjoint from
\partial S
,
so it is sufficient to show the main claim for each of
F_{W,(S,\mathcal{A}^{\prime}),\mathfrak{s}}
and

F_{W,(S,\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}),\mathfrak{s}}
separately. Case (
4
) is
illustrated in Figure
5.2
.

Figure 5.2.
The bypass relation in Case (
4
), when
g(C_{\bm{\mathbf{w}},1})>0
. On the
bottom row, the domain of the bypass is shown. The dotted lines outside of
the domain of the bypass indicate the configuration of the dividing set
outside the bypass region.

We now proceed to show that if
\mathcal{A}
is a dividing set on
S
such that

\partial C_{\bm{\mathbf{w}},0}
contains a closed component
\gamma
disjoint from
\partial S
, then

(5.5)

F_{W,\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{s}}|_{V=1}=A_{\gamma}\circ G

for some map
G
, where
A_{\gamma}
denotes the homology action of the curve

\gamma
. To establish equation (
5.5
),
we must recall some additional facts about the functor

F_{W,\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{s}}|_{V=1}
. According to
[
58
]
*Theorem C, the
chain homotopy type of the map
F_{W,\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{s}}|_{V=1}
depends only on

W
, the embedded surface
S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
(which is not properly embedded) and

\mathfrak{s}\in\Spin^{c}(W)
. To describe the reduction in more detail, we recall
that a
ribbon 1-skeleton
of
S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
is a choice of embedded graph

\Gamma_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}\subseteq S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
such that
\Gamma_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}\cap\partial S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}=\{w\}
,
and
S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
is a regular neighborhood of
\Gamma_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
in
S
; see

[
58
]
*Definition 14.5.

There is a simple way to construct the ribbon 1-skeleton of the subsurface

S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
. One starts with a collection of arcs
a\subseteq S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
such that

a\cap\partial S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}=\{w\}
, and such that each component of
S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
contains
exactly one arc. One then takes inward translates
C_{1},\dots,C_{n}
of the boundary components of
\partial S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
which do not contain a basepoint of
\bm{\mathbf{w}}
, which one connects to

a
by adjoining an embedded arc (disjoint from the other arcs). The complement of this graph in
S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}

consists of a collection of
|\partial S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}|
connected surfaces, each with a
single boundary component. The total genus of these surfaces is
g(S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}})
.
We then pick a geometric symplectic basis of
H_{1}
of the complement of this
graph (i.e., a collection of simple closed curves
A_{1},\dots,A_{g}
,

B_{1},\dots,B_{g}
that form a basis of
H_{1}
and satisfy
|A_{i}\cap B_{j}|=\delta_{ij}
). By connecting
a
with one of the curves
in each pair in the symplectic basis by an arc, we obtain a ribbon 1-skeleton of

S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
. An example is shown in Figure
5.3
.

Figure 5.3.
A ribbon 1-skeleton
\Gamma_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
for a genus 2 component of
S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}

with 2 boundary components.

The second author
[
53
]
constructed maps on
\mathit{CF}^{-}

induced by cobordisms with embedded ribbon graphs.
By
[
58
]
*Theorem C, the reduction

F_{W,\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{s}}|_{V=1}
is chain homotopic to the graph cobordism map

F_{W,\Gamma_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}},\mathfrak{s}}
for a ribbon 1-skeleton
\Gamma_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
of
S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
.
Note that
\Gamma_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
inherits a ribbon structure; i.e., a cyclic
ordering of the edges adjacent to each vertex, from
the orientation of
S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
.

By picking an appropriate ribbon 1-skeleton
\Gamma_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
of
\Sigma_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
(see Figure
5.4
), we
can decompose the graph cobordism
(W,\Gamma_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}})
such that it
is a sequence of graph cobordisms
(W_{3},\gamma_{3})\circ(W_{2},\Gamma_{2})\circ(W_{1},\Gamma_{1})
,
satisfying the following conditions:

(1)

W_{1}
is a 4-dimensional 1-handlebody, and the graph
\Gamma_{1}
intersects
\partial W_{1}
in a single point.

(2)

W_{2}
is a cylinder
I\times Y
, and
\Gamma_{2}
is a graph of the form

(I\times\{p\})\cup\gamma
, as shown in Figure
5.4
, where

\gamma
is a loop induced by one of the boundary components of

S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
which is disjoint from
\partial S
.

(3)

W_{3}
is a cobordism between two connected 3-manifolds,
and
\gamma_{3}
is a path connecting the two components.

This can be achieved as follows. We pick
\Gamma_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
by first taking an arc
a
in
\Sigma_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
such that
a\cap\partial\Sigma_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}=\{w\}
. We then join closed loops (as described above) for components of
\partial S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
not containing
w
, and also for a symplectic basis
A_{1},\dots,A_{g},B_{1},\dots,B_{g}
as above. We assume that of these loops,
\gamma
is joined the closest to
w
along the arc
a
. We pick an ordered handle decomposition for
W
into 0-, 1-, 2- and 3-handles. We let
W_{1}
be the the union of the 0- and 1-handles. We let
W_{2}
be a regular neighborhood of
\partial W_{1}
, and we let
W_{3}
be the 2-handles and 3-handles. By flowing using a gradient like Morse function for this handle decomposition, we may isotope all of the closed loops of
\Gamma_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
so that they lie below
W_{3}
. Therefore we may assume that
\Gamma_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}\cap W_{2}
consists of a subarc of
a
with
\gamma
spliced in, and that
W_{3}\cap\Gamma_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
consists only of a single arc, as claimed above.

Figure 5.4.
A decomposition of the graph cobordism
(W,\Gamma_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}})
. The
loop
\gamma
in
\Gamma_{2}
corresponds to a closed curve in
\partial C_{\bm{\mathbf{w}},0}

disjoint from
\partial S
.

The composition law for graph cobordism maps implies that

(5.6)

F_{W,\Gamma_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}},\mathfrak{s}}\simeq F_{W_{3},\gamma_{3},\mathfrak{s}|_{W_{3}}}\circ F_{W_{2},\Gamma_{2},\mathfrak{s}|_{W_{2}}}\circ F_{W_{1},\Gamma_{1},\mathfrak{s}|_{W_{1}}}.

Since
\gamma_{3}
is a path, the map
F_{W_{3},\gamma_{3},\mathfrak{s}|_{W_{3}}}

agrees with Ozsváth and Szabó’s cobordism map. By
[
55
]
*Proposition 4.6, we have

(5.7)

F_{W_{2},\Gamma_{2},\mathfrak{s}|_{W_{2}}}\simeq A_{\gamma}.

Since
H_{1}(Y)
surjects onto
H_{1}(W)
, we have

(5.8)

F_{W_{3},\gamma_{3},\mathfrak{s}|_{W_{3}}}\circ A_{\gamma}\simeq A_{\gamma}\circ F_{W_{3},\gamma_{3},\mathfrak{s}|_{W_{3}}}.

Combining equations (
5.6
),
(
5.7
), and (
5.8
), we
obtain the relation

(5.9)

F_{W,\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{s}}|_{V=1}\simeq F_{W,\Gamma_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}},\mathfrak{s}}\simeq A_{\gamma}\circ F_{W_{3},\gamma_{3},\mathfrak{s}|_{W_{3}}}\circ F_{W_{1},\Gamma_{1},\mathfrak{s}|_{W_{1}}}.

Since we assumed that the Alexander grading shift
\Delta
was nonnegative, by using Lemma
5.1
we obtain

\begin{split}F_{W,\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{s}}&\simeq V^{\Delta}\cdot(F_{W,\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{s}}|_{V=1})\otimes\id_{\mathbb{F}[U,V]}\\
&\simeq V^{\Delta}\cdot(A_{\gamma}\circ G)\otimes\id_{\mathbb{F}[U,V]}\\
&\simeq(A_{\gamma}\otimes\id_{\mathbb{F}[U,V]})\circ(V^{\Delta}\cdot G\otimes\id_{\mathbb{F}[U,V]})\\
&\simeq A_{\gamma}\circ(V^{\Delta}\cdot G\otimes\id_{\mathbb{F}[U,V]}),\end{split}

where
G\simeq F_{W_{3},\gamma_{3},\mathfrak{s}|_{W_{3}}}\circ F_{W_{1},\Gamma_{1},\mathfrak{s}|_{W_{1}}}
. In the last line, we are using the fact that
A_{\gamma}
preserves the Alexander grading, so
(A_{\gamma}|_{V=1})\otimes\id_{\mathbb{F}[U,V]}
coincides with the ordinary action of
A_{\gamma}
on
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(Y,\mathbb{U})
by Lemma
5.1
. This proves the claim.

The case when the Alexander grading change
\Delta
is negative is handled similarly,
using the
U=1
reductions instead.
∎

Next, we compute the effect of a stabilization, for a simple dividing set:

Lemma 5.4
.

Suppose that
(W,\mathcal{F})\colon(Y_{1},\mathbb{L}_{1})\to(Y_{2},\mathbb{L}_{2})
is a decorated
link cobordism with
b_{1}(W)=0
. Write
\mathcal{F}=(S,\mathcal{A})
, and suppose that
S
is connected.
Let
S^{\prime}
be a
(n,g)
-stabilization of
S
along
(B^{4},S_{0})
. Let
D_{1},\dots,D_{n}
denote
the components of
S\cap B^{4}
, and let
\widehat{D}\subseteq S
be a disk
that contains
D_{1},\dots,D_{n}
and intersects
\mathcal{A}
in a single arc. Consider the subsurface

S_{0}^{\prime}:=(\widehat{D}\setminus(D_{1}\cup\cdots\cup D_{n}))\cup S_{0}\subseteq S^{\prime}.

Let
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
be a dividing set on
S^{\prime}
that agrees with
\mathcal{A}
outside
\widehat{D}
,
and write
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}=(S^{\prime},\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
.

(1)

Suppose that
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
intersects
S_{0}^{\prime}
in a single arc that
divides
S_{0}^{\prime}
into two connected components.
Let
S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
and
S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}^{\prime}
denote the type-
\bm{\mathbf{w}}
regions,
and
S_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}
and
S_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{\prime}
the type-
\bm{\mathbf{z}}
regions
of
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
, respectively. Then

F_{W,\mathcal{F}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}}\simeq U^{g(S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}^{\prime})-g(S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}})}V^{g(S_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{\prime})-g(S_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}})}\cdot F_{W,\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{s}}.

(2)

If
\mathcal{A}\cap S_{0}^{\prime}
contains a closed component, then

F_{W,\mathcal{F}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}}\simeq 0.

Figure 5.5.
An example of a stabilization considered in Lemma
5.4
. The dividing set
\mathcal{A}
in the region
\widehat{D}\subseteq S
is shown on the left, and the dividing set
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
on the stabilization
S^{\prime}
is shown on the right.

Proof.

We first show Claim (
1
). Consider the punctured disk

\widehat{D}_{0}:=\widehat{D}\setminus(D_{1}\cup\cdots\cup D_{n}).

Let
N
denote the total space of the unit normal disk bundle of
\widehat{D}_{0}

in
W\setminus B^{4}
. Note that
N
is diffeomorphic to
\widehat{D}_{0}\times B^{2}
. Define

W_{0}:=B^{4}\cup N,

which, after rounding corners, we can view as a codimension 0 submanifold of

W
with smooth boundary. In fact,
W_{0}
is a 4-dimensional genus
n-1

handlebody
(\mathbb{S}^{1}\times B^{3})^{\natural(n-1)}
.
Let
Y
denote
\partial W_{0}
. We observe that
S_{0}^{\prime}
, as defined above, is equal to
W_{0}\cap S^{\prime}
. We can view

(W_{0},S_{0}^{\prime})
as a link cobordism from the empty link to the pair
(Y,K)

where
K=\partial\widehat{D}\times\{0\}
.

Let us write
C_{1},\dots,C_{n}
for the components of
\partial\widehat{D}_{0}\setminus\partial\widehat{D}
,
and
U_{1},\dots,U_{n}
for the components of the unlink

S_{0}\cap\partial B^{4}
. We can view

\partial N=(\widehat{D}_{0}\times\partial B^{2})\cup(\partial\widehat{D}\times B^{2})\cup\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}(C_{i}\times B^{2}).

Hence, we can write

Y=\left(\partial N\setminus\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}(C_{i}\times B^{2})\right)\cup\left(\partial B^{4}\setminus\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}N(U_{i})\right),

where the two manifolds are glued along their
n
torus boundary components.

We now claim that
K
is an unknot in
Y
. It is at this step that we use the
fact that
S\cap\partial B^{4}
is an unlink. To see that
K
is an unknot, we will
construct a Seifert disk
D_{K}
for
K
in
Y
. Let
r
denote a radial arc
from
0\in B^{2}
to a point
p\in\partial B^{2}
. Let
A
denote the annulus

A:=\partial\widehat{D}\times r\subseteq Y.

We then attach the punctured disk
\widehat{D}_{0}\times\{p\}
to the annulus
A
. The resulting surface has boundary

\partial(A\cup(\widehat{D}_{0}\times\{p\}))=K\cup\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}(C_{i}\times\{p\}).

Next, we note that the image of
C_{i}\times\{p\}
in
\partial N(U_{i})\subseteq\partial B^{4}

is the Seifert longitude, since the disks
D_{i}\subseteq S\cap B^{4}
can be
pushed into
\partial B^{4}
to give Seifert disks of
U_{i}
that intersect
\partial N(U_{i})

along
C_{i}\times\{p\}
. By capping
C_{i}\times\{p\}
with Seifert
disks of the
U_{i}
, we obtain the Seifert disk
D_{K}
of
K
in
Y
.

Let us write
\mathcal{F}_{0}
for the decorated surface
(\widehat{D},\mathcal{A}\cap\widehat{D})
,

\mathcal{F}_{0}^{\prime}
for the decorated surface
(S_{0}^{\prime},\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\cap S_{0}^{\prime})
, and
\mathfrak{s}_{0}
for

\mathfrak{s}|_{W_{0}}
. Since
H_{2}(W_{0})=0
, by applying Lemma
5.3

to both
F_{W_{0},\mathcal{F}_{0},\mathfrak{s}_{0}}
and
F_{W_{0},\mathcal{F}_{0}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}_{0}}
, we compute that

(5.10)

F_{W_{0},\mathcal{F}_{0}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}_{0}}\simeq U^{g(S_{0,\bm{\mathbf{w}}}^{\prime})}V^{g(S_{0,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{\prime})}\cdot F_{W_{0},\mathcal{F}_{0},\mathfrak{s}_{0}}\mod H_{1}(Y).

Write
W_{1}:=W\setminus\Int(W_{0})
,
\mathcal{F}_{1}:=\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\cap W_{1}
, and

\mathfrak{s}_{1}:=\mathfrak{s}|_{W_{1}}
. Since
W_{0}
