is a 4-dimensional handlebody, we conclude
that
b_{1}(W_{1})=0
. Noting that the map
\delta\colon H^{1}(Y)\to H^{2}(W)

is trivial, and using the
\Spin^{c}
composition law, we conclude from
equation (
5.10
) that

(5.11)

F_{W,\mathcal{F}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}}\simeq U^{g(S_{0,\bm{\mathbf{w}}}^{\prime})}V^{g(S_{0,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{\prime})}\cdot F_{W,\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{s}}.

Noting that
g(S^{\prime}_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}})-g(S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}})=g(S_{0,\bm{\mathbf{w}}}^{\prime})
and

g(S^{\prime}_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}})-g(S_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}})=g(S_{0,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{\prime})
, the proof of Claim (
1
) is complete.

We now consider Claim (
2
). In this case, Lemma
5.3
implies that

F_{W_{0},\mathcal{F}_{0}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}}\simeq\sum_{i=1}^{k}A_{\gamma_{i}}\circ J_{i}
for some
filtered, equivariant maps
J_{1},\dots,J_{k}
. The map
H_{1}(Y)\to H_{1}(W_{1})/\Tors

is trivial since
b_{1}(W)=0
and
W_{0}
is a 4-dimensional 1-handlebody.
Hence, using the composition law,

F_{W,\mathcal{F}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}}\simeq F_{W_{1},\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathfrak{s}_{1}}\circ F_{W_{0},\mathcal{F}_{0}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}_{0}}\simeq F_{W_{1},\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathfrak{s}_{1}}\circ\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}A_{\gamma_{i}}\circ J_{i}\right)\simeq 0,

concluding the proof of Claim (
2
).
∎

Lemma
5.4
computes the result of a stabilization of
a link cobordism, when the dividing set is nicely arranged on the
stabilization. However, to prove geometric bounds on the secondary versions of
V_{k}
,
we will need to consider more general dividing sets on stabilizations.

Definition 5.5
.

Suppose that
(B^{4},S)
is an undecorated knot cobordism from
\emptyset
to
an arbitrary knot
K
in
\mathbb{S}^{3}
. Let
\mathbb{K}
denote
K
decorated with two
basepoints, and let
\mathfrak{s}_{0}
be the unique
\Spin^{c}
structure on
B^{4}
.
We say that
S
satisfies the
decoration-independence condition (DI)
if the following holds:

(
DI
)

For any decoration

\mathcal{F}=(S,\mathcal{A})
whose dividing set intersects
K
in exactly two points,

(1)

the filtered, equivariant chain homotopy type of the map

F_{B^{4},\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{s}_{0}}\colon\mathbb{F}_{2}[U,V]\to\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(\mathbb{K})

depends only on
g(S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}})
and
g(S_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}})
when
|\mathcal{A}|=1
, and

(2)

F_{B^{4},\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{s}_{0}}\simeq 0
when
|\mathcal{A}|>1
.

Note that, if
S
is a stabilization of a slice disk
(B^{4},D)
, then, by
Lemma
5.4
, the link cobordism
(B^{4},S)
satisfies the decoration-independence condition (
DI
).

Definition 5.6
.

Let
S
and
\mathbb{K}
be as in Definition
5.5
.
Suppose
d\geq g(S)
is an integer. We say that
S
satisfies
the decoration-independence condition (
DI
)
above degree
d
if for any decoration
\mathcal{F}=(S,\mathcal{A})
compatible with
\mathbb{K}
,
and for any
i
,
j\in\mathbb{N}
satisfying
i+j+g(S)\geq d
,

(1)

the chain homotopy type of the map
U^{i}V^{j}\cdot F_{B^{4},\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{s}_{0}}

depends only on
i+g(S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}})
and
j+g(S_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}})
when
|\mathcal{A}|=1
, and

(2)

U^{i}V^{j}\cdot F_{B^{4},\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{s}}\simeq 0
when
|\mathcal{A}|>1
.

We define the invariant
\mathcal{I}(S)\in\mathbb{N}

to be the minimal
d\geq g(S)
such that
S
satisfies condition (
DI
) above degree
d
.

Remark 5.7
.

The quantity
\mathcal{I}(S)
is finite for every surface
S
. This can be seen as follows. Two
\mathbb{F}[U,V]
-equivariant chain maps
f
,
g\colon\mathbb{F}[U,V]\to\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(S^{3},\mathbb{K})
are
\mathbb{F}[U,V]
-equivariantly chain homotopic if and only if
[f(1)]=[g(1)]
, as elements of
\mathcal{H\!F\!L}^{-}(S^{3},\mathbb{K})
. However, the rank of
\mathcal{H\!F\!L}^{-}(S^{3},\mathbb{K})
in
(\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}},\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}
)-bigrading
(-2n,-2m)
is 1 whenever
n
,
m\geq 0
and
n+m
is sufficiently large.

Note that, to compute
\mathcal{I}(S)
, one would need to determine the cobordism maps
for infinitely many dividing sets on
S
, which is a formidable task.
However, to obtain a lower bound on
\mathcal{I}(S)
, it suffices to find
two dividing sets
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
on
S
, both consisting of a single arc,
and integers
i_{1}
,
i_{2}
,
j_{1}
, and
j_{2}
, such that

i_{1}+g(S_{1,\bm{\mathbf{w}}})=i_{2}+g(S_{2,\bm{\mathbf{w}}})\text{ and }j_{1}+g(S_{1,\bm{\mathbf{z}}})=j_{2}+g(S_{2,\bm{\mathbf{z}}})\text{, but}

U^{i_{1}}V^{j_{1}}\cdot F_{B^{4},(S,\mathcal{A}_{1}),\mathfrak{s}_{0}}\not\simeq U^{i_{2}}V^{j_{2}}\cdot F_{B^{4},(S,\mathcal{A}_{2}),\mathfrak{s}_{0}},

where
S_{k,\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
and
S_{k,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}
denote the type-
\bm{\mathbf{w}}
and type-
\bm{\mathbf{z}}

subsurfaces of
S
with respect to the decoration
\mathcal{A}_{k}
for
k\in\{1,2\}
.
In this case,
\mathcal{I}(S)>i_{1}+i_{2}+g(S)
.

Proposition 5.8
.

Suppose that
(B^{4},S)
satisfies the decoration-independence condition (
DI
) above degree
d
,
and let
S^{\prime}
be a stabilization of
S
.
Then
(B^{4},S^{\prime})
satisfies condition (
DI
) above degree
\max\{d,g(S^{\prime})\}
.

Our proof of Proposition
5.8
uses the following
combinatorial lemma about dividing sets on surfaces:

Lemma 5.9
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{A}_{1}
,
\mathcal{A}_{2}
, and
\mathcal{A}_{3}
are three dividing sets that fit
into a bypass triple on a surface
S
with
|\partial S|=1
, and
|\mathcal{A}_{i}\cap\partial S|=2
for
i\in\{1,2,3\}
. Then the number of
\mathcal{A}_{i}
that have no
closed loops is even.

Proof.

If
\mathcal{A}_{1}
,
\mathcal{A}_{2}
, and
\mathcal{A}_{3}
all have a closed loop, then the
statement is true since 0 is even, so instead assume that
\mathcal{A}_{1}
has
no closed loops. Note that this implies that
|\mathcal{A}_{1}|=1
, since
|\partial S\cap\mathcal{A}_{1}|=2
.
The dividing sets
\mathcal{A}_{i}
can be consistently oriented, by declaring their
orientation to be the boundary orientation of
S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
. Let
D
denote the
bypass region. The set
\mathcal{A}_{1}\cap D
consists of three arcs, which we label
as
a_{1}
,
a_{2}
, and
a_{3}
; see Figure
5.6
. The main claim can be
proven by considering separately six cases, corresponding to the possible
relative orderings of the arcs
a_{1}
,
a_{2}
, and
a_{3}
, as they appear on

\mathcal{A}_{1}
. Let us first consider the case when the arcs appear ordered

(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})
, read left-to-right. In this case,
\mathcal{A}_{1}
has no closed
loops by assumption, and by inspecting Figure
5.6
, we see that
exactly one of
\mathcal{A}_{2}
and
\mathcal{A}_{3}
also has no closed loops. The arguments
when the arcs appear along
\mathcal{A}_{1}
with ordering
(a_{1},a_{3},a_{2})
,

(a_{2},a_{1},a_{3})
,
(a_{2},a_{3},a_{1})
,
(a_{3},a_{1},a_{2})
, or
(a_{3},a_{2},a_{1})
are
easy modifications of the above argument.
∎

Figure 5.6.
The proof of Lemma
5.9
, when the
arcs
a_{1}
,
a_{2}
, and
a_{3}
appear on
\mathcal{A}_{1}
with order
(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})
,
read left-to-right. The bypass region is the disk shown. The dashed lines
outside the bypass regions represent the configuration of dividing arcs
outside the bypass region. In the case at hand,
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{3}
have
no closed components, while
\mathcal{A}_{2}
has two.

Proof of Proposition
5.8
.

Fix integers
i
,
j\geq 0
such that
i+j+g(S^{\prime})\geq d
. Analyzing the proof of
Lemma
5.4
, we can find a 4-dimensional 1-handlebody

W_{0}
whose boundary we denote
Y
, such that

(1)

S^{\prime}\cap Y=S\cap Y
is an unknot in
Y
;

(2)

S\cap W_{0}
is a disk, and
S^{\prime}\cap W_{0}
is a connected,
genus
g(S^{\prime})-g(S)
surface with only one boundary component.

Let
J
denote
S^{\prime}\cap Y
. Note that Lemma
5.4

immediately implies the statement for any dividing set
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\subseteq S^{\prime}

(connected or disconnected) that intersects
J
in exactly two points.

We now show the main claim by induction on
|\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\cap J|
.
We have established the base case,
|\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\cap J|=2
.
If
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is a dividing set on
S^{\prime}
with
|\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\cap J|\geq 4
, then, using the
bypass relation as shown in Figure
5.7
, we can write

(5.12)

F_{B^{4},(S^{\prime},\mathcal{A}^{\prime}),\mathfrak{s}_{0}}\simeq F_{B^{4},(S^{\prime},\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}),\mathfrak{s}_{0}}+F_{B^{4},(S^{\prime},\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime\prime}),\mathfrak{s}_{0}},

where
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
and
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
are dividing sets satisfying

|\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}\cap J|=|\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime\prime}\cap J|=|\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\cap J|-2.

Figure 5.7.
Reducing
|J\cap\mathcal{A}^{\prime}|
by 2, using the bypass relation. The
annulus shown is a neighborhood of
J
in the surface
S^{\prime}
. Using a small
isotopy, we may push the two bigons in each of the bottom two annuli out of
the neighborhood of
J
which is shown.

Let us write
S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}^{\prime\prime}
,
S_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{\prime\prime}
,
S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}^{\prime\prime\prime}
, and
S_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{\prime\prime\prime}
for the
type-
\bm{\mathbf{w}}
and type-
\bm{\mathbf{z}}
subregions of
S^{\prime}\setminus\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
and
S^{\prime}\setminus\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime\prime}
.
There are two cases to consider: when
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
has no closed components,
or when
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
has at least one closed component.

Let us consider the case when
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
has no closed components. In this case,
by Lemma
5.9
, we know that exactly one of

\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
and
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime\prime}
has no closed components, while the other has a closed
component. For definiteness, let us say that
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
has no closed
components. Note that, in this case,
g(S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}^{\prime})=g(S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}^{\prime\prime})
and

g(S_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{\prime})=g(S_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{\prime\prime})
.

By our inductive hypothesis, we know that
U^{i}V^{j}\cdot F_{B^{4},(S^{\prime},\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime\prime}),\mathfrak{s}_{0}}\simeq 0
. Combining this with
equation (
5.12
), we conclude that

U^{i}V^{j}\cdot F_{B^{4},(S^{\prime},\mathcal{A}^{\prime}),\mathfrak{s}_{0}}\simeq U^{i}V^{j}\cdot F_{B^{4},(S^{\prime},\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}),\mathfrak{s}_{0}}.

By the inductive hypothesis,
U^{i}V^{j}\cdot F_{B^{4},(S^{\prime},\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}),\mathfrak{s}_{0}}

depends only on the integers
i+g(S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}^{\prime\prime})

and
j+g(S_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{\prime\prime})
, and hence the same holds for
U^{i}V^{j}\cdot F_{B^{4},(S^{\prime},\mathcal{A}^{\prime}),\mathfrak{s}_{0}}
.

Next, we consider the case when
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
has a closed component. We
wish to show that

U^{i}V^{j}\cdot F_{B^{4},(S^{\prime},\mathcal{A}^{\prime}),\mathfrak{s}_{0}}\simeq 0.

By Lemma
5.9
, one of the following two cases holds:
Either
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
and
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime\prime}
both have a closed component, or neither
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
nor

\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime\prime}
has a closed component. If
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
and
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime\prime}
both have a closed
component, then
U^{i}V^{j}\cdot F_{B^{4},(S^{\prime},\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}),\mathfrak{s}_{0}}
and
U^{i}V^{j}\cdot F_{B^{4},(S^{\prime},\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime\prime}),\mathfrak{s}_{0}}
are both chain homotopic to zero, by induction. If neither
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}

and
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime\prime}
have a closed component, we note that

g(S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}^{\prime\prime})=g(S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}^{\prime\prime\prime})
and
g(S_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{\prime\prime})=g(S_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{\prime\prime\prime})
, so

U^{i}V^{j}\cdot F_{B^{4},(S^{\prime},\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}),\mathfrak{s}_{0}}\simeq U^{i}V^{j}\cdot F_{B^{4},(S^{\prime},\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime\prime}),\mathfrak{s}_{0}}

by induction. In both cases, the sum

U^{i}V^{j}\cdot F_{B^{4},(S^{\prime},\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}),\mathfrak{s}_{0}}+U^{i}V^{j}\cdot F_{B^{4},(S^{\prime},\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime\prime}),\mathfrak{s}_{0}}\simeq 0.

Hence, by equation (
5.12
),

U^{i}V^{j}\cdot F_{B^{4},(S^{\prime},\mathcal{A}^{\prime}),\mathfrak{s}_{0}}\simeq 0
, completing the proof.
∎

5.3.
Destabilizing genus bounds from
\mathcal{I}
and
\kappa_{0}

In this section, we show that the invariants
\kappa_{0}(S)
and
\mathcal{I}(S)
give lower bounds on the quantity
g_{\dest}(S)
,
introduced in Definition
2.17
. We begin with the invariant
\mathcal{I}(S)
from
Definition
5.6
.

Theorem 5.10
.

If
K
is slice a knot in
\mathbb{S}^{3}
and
S\in\Surf(K)
, then

\mathcal{I}(S)\leq g_{\dest}(S).

Proof.

Let
S_{1},\dots,S_{n}\in\Surf(K)
be a sequence of surfaces as in Definition
2.17

connecting
S_{1}=S
with a slice disk
S_{n}=D
, such that

g_{\dest}(S)=\max\{g(S_{1}),\dots,g(S_{n})\}
.

The result follows immediately from Proposition
5.8
,
with the following explanation.
The disk
S_{n}
trivially satisfies the decoration-independence condition (
DI
) above degree
0
.
By Proposition
5.8
, if
S_{k}
for
k\in\{2,\dots,n\}
satisfies condition (
DI
)
above degree
d
and
S_{k-1}
is a stabilization of
S_{k}
, then
S_{k-1}

satisfies condition (
DI
) above degree
\max\{d,g(S_{k-1})\}
. Using the
stabilization formula, Lemma
5.4
, the converse is
also true: If
S_{k}
satisfies condition (
DI
) above degree
d
and
S_{k-1}
is a
destabilization of
S_{k}
, then
S_{k-1}
also satisfies condition (
DI
) above degree
d
.
Hence, by induction, we see that
S=S_{1}
satisfies condition (
DI
)
above degree
d=\max\{g(S_{1}),\dots,g(S_{n})\}
, and hence
\mathcal{I}(S)\leq g_{\dest}(S)
.
∎

We note that the invariant
\mathcal{I}(S)
is not easy to determine,
since it involves computing the cobordism maps for infinitely
many decorations on
S
. The invariant
\kappa_{0}(S)

defined in Section
4.7
is easier to compute
because it involves calculating just a single cobordism map
on
\mathit{HFK}^{-}_{U=0}
, as opposed to infinitely many on
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}
.
We now prove that
\kappa_{0}(S)
also bounds
g_{\dest}(S)
:

Theorem 5.11
.

If
K
is a slice knot in
\mathbb{S}^{3}
and
S\in\Surf(K)
, then

\kappa_{0}(S)\leq g_{\dest}(S).

Proof.

Suppose that
g(S)>0
.
Recall that
\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{w}}}^{-}
is defined by
decorating
S
with a dividing set consisting of a single
arc such that
g(S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}})=g(S)
and
g(S_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}})=0
. Suppose
that
S_{1},\dots,S_{n}
is a stabilization sequence of surfaces
in
\Surf(K)
such that
S_{1}=S
and
S_{n}
is a slice disk.
Let

d:=\max\{g(S_{1}),\dots g(S_{n})\}.

By Theorem
5.10
, the surface
S
satisfies the decoration-independence condition (
DI
)
above degree
d
. There are two cases:

d=g(S)
or
d>g(S)
. If
d=g(S)
, then the stabilization formula implies that

\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{w}}}^{-}\simeq U^{g(S)}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S_{n}}^{-},

so
\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{w}}}^{-}
vanishes on
\mathit{HFK}^{-}_{U=0}
, implying that

\kappa_{0}(S)=g(S)=g_{\dest}(S).

We now consider the second case, where
d>g(S)
.
We note that

V^{d-g(S)}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{w}}}^{-}\simeq F_{B^{4},(S^{\prime},\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}^{\prime})},

where
(S^{\prime},\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}^{\prime})
is obtained from
(S,\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}})

by performing
d-g(S)
trivial 1-handle stabilizations along
S_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}
.
Since
(S^{\prime},\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}^{\prime})
satisfies condition (
DI
),
by definition the map
F_{B^{4},(S^{\prime},\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}^{\prime})}

depends only on the dividing set through the genera of the
type-
\bm{\mathbf{w}}
and type-
\bm{\mathbf{z}}
subregions. Hence, if
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\subseteq S^{\prime}

is any other dividing set on
S^{\prime}
consisting of a single
arc, such that the genera of the type-
\bm{\mathbf{w}}
and type-
\bm{\mathbf{z}}

subregions are the same as those of
(S^{\prime},\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}^{\prime})
, then

F_{B^{4},(S^{\prime},\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}^{\prime})}\simeq F_{B^{4},(S^{\prime},\mathcal{A}^{\prime})}.

We pick a dividing set
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\subseteq S^{\prime}
such that one
of the trivial stabilizations of
S^{\prime}
occurs in the
type-
\bm{\mathbf{w}}
subregion. See Figure
5.8
.

Figure 5.8.
The surfaces
(S,\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}})
,
(S^{\prime},\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}^{\prime})
, and
(S^{\prime},\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
from the proof of Theorem
5.11
.

Using the stabilization formula,
we conclude that there is a decorated surface
\mathcal{F}
such that

V^{d-g(S)}\cdot F_{B^{4},(S,\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}})}\simeq F_{B^{4},(S^{\prime},\mathcal{A}^{\prime})}\simeq U\cdot F_{B^{4},\mathcal{F}},

from which we conclude that
V^{d-g(S)}\cdot t^{-}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{w}}}\simeq 0

since the action of
U
is trivial on
\mathit{CFK}_{U=0}
.
∎

Remark 5.12
.

If
S
is a genus
g>0
stabilization of a surface,
then
\kappa_{0}(S)=g(S)
, since
\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{w}}}^{-}\simeq U^{g}\cdot G
for some map
G
, so
[t^{-}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{w}}}(1)]=0
in
\mathit{HFK}^{-}_{U=0}(\mathbb{K})
.
Also, we note that if
S
satisfies the decoration independence condition
(
DI
) at degree
d>g(S)
, then the map
V^{d-g(S)}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{w}}}^{-}
vanishes on
\mathit{HFK}^{-}_{U=0}
.
This follows by adapting the argument from the proof of Theorem
5.11
.
Hence

\kappa_{0}(S)\leq\max\{g(S)+1,\mathcal{I}(S)\}.

5.4.
Stabilization distance bounds from
\tau
and
V_{k}

Theorem 5.13
.

Let
K
be a knot in
\mathbb{S}^{3}
, and let
S
,
S^{\prime}\in\Surf(K)
. Then

\tau(S,S^{\prime})\leq\mu_{\st}(S,S^{\prime}).

Proof.

Let us write
m=\mu_{\st}(S,S^{\prime})
.
Suppose that
S_{1},\dots,S_{k}
is a stabilization sequence of surfaces in
B^{4}
connecting
S
and

S^{\prime}
, as in Definition
2.14
, such that

\max\{\,g(S_{1}),\dots,g(S_{k})\,\}=m.

Let
\mathbb{K}
denote
K
decorated with two basepoints. By Lemma
5.4
,
if
S_{i+1}
is obtained from
S_{i}
by a stabilization, then
the map
\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S_{i+1},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}
is filtered chain homotopic to
V^{g(S_{i+1})-g(S_{i})}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S_{i},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}
.
Similarly, if
S_{i+1}
is obtained from
S_{i}
by a destabilization, then
the map
\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S_{i+1},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}
is filtered chain homotopic to
V^{g(S_{i})-g(S_{i+1})}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S_{i+1},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}
.
It follows that all of the maps
V^{m-g(S_{i})}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S_{i},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}
coincide
for
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
. In particular,

(5.13)

V^{m-g(S)}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}\simeq V^{m-g(S^{\prime})}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}.

The map
\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S_{i},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}
on
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}
increases the Alexander grading by
g(S_{i})
,
so
V^{-g(S_{i})}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S_{i},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}
determines a well-defined map from
\mathbb{F}_{2}[\widehat{U}]
into
C(\mathbb{K},R_{0,-g(S_{i})})\subseteq\mathit{CFK}^{\infty}(\mathbb{K})
. Hence, from equation (
5.13
), we conclude that the induced elements
[V^{-g(S)}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)]
and
[V^{-g(S^{\prime})}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)]
coincide in
H_{*}(C(\mathbb{K},{\vphantom{I}}_{m}I))
. By Lemma
4.8
, this implies that
\tau(S,S^{\prime})\leq m
, completing the proof.
∎

A different algebraic perspective on the previous proof can be given using the formulation
of
\tau(S,S^{\prime})
in terms of
\mathit{HFK}^{-}_{U=0}(\mathbb{K})
described in Lemma
4.6
,
and the computation of the effect of stabilizations from Lemma
5.4
.

We now show that the local
h
-invariants give a lower bound on the stabilization
distance between two slice disks:

Theorem 5.14
.

If
D
and
D^{\prime}
are slice disks of
K
and
k\leq\mu_{\st}(D,D^{\prime})
, then

V_{k}(D,D^{\prime})\leq\left\lceil\frac{\mu_{\st}(D,D^{\prime})-k}{2}\right\rceil.

If
k\geq\mu_{\st}(D,D^{\prime})
, then
V_{k}(D,D^{\prime})=0
.

Proof of Theorem
5.14
.

Suppose first that
k\leq\mu_{\st}(D,D^{\prime})
,
and that
S_{1},\dots,S_{n}
is a sequence of embedded surfaces in

\Surf(K)
such that
S_{i+1}
is either obtained from
S_{i}

by a stabilization or destabilization. Further, we assume that
S_{1}=D
and

S_{n}=D^{\prime}
. Let
d
denote
\max\{g(S_{1}),\dots,g(S_{n})\}
.
Since
S_{n}
is a slice disk,
g_{\dest}(S_{i})\leq d
for
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
.
Furthermore,
\mathcal{I}(S_{i})\leq g_{\dest}(S_{i})
by Theorem
5.10
, hence

S_{i}
satisfies the decoration-independence condition (
DI
) above degree
d
.

Next, we fix an integer
k
such that
0\leq k\leq d
. By increasing
d
by 1,
if necessary, we may assume that
(d-k)/2
is an integer.
Let
\mathbb{K}
denote
K
decorated with two basepoints. We decorate
each surface
S_{i}
with a single dividing arc
\mathcal{A}_{i}
, and we pick
nonnegative integers
n_{i}
and
m_{i}
such that
g(S_{i})+n_{i}+m_{i}=d
and

g(S_{i,\bm{\mathbf{w}}})+n_{i}=\frac{d-k}{2}\text{ and }g(S_{i,\bm{\mathbf{z}}})+m_{i}=\frac{d+k}{2}.

We note that, since each
S_{i}
satisfies condition (
DI
) above degree
d
, it
follows that the map
U^{n_{i}}V^{m_{i}}\cdot F_{B^{4},(S_{i},\mathcal{A}_{i}),\mathfrak{s}_{0}}
depends on
the dividing set
\mathcal{A}_{i}
only up to the quantities
n_{i}+g(S_{i,\bm{\mathbf{w}}})
and

m_{i}+g(S_{i,\bm{\mathbf{z}}})
, and is independent of the choice of
\mathcal{A}_{i}
.
Using the stabilization formula, Lemma
5.4
, it thus
follows that all of the maps
U^{n_{i}}V^{m_{i}}\cdot F_{W,(S_{i},\mathcal{A}_{i}),\mathfrak{s}_{0}}

are chain homotopic. In particular,

(5.14)

U^{(d-k)/2}V^{(d+k)/2}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{D}^{\infty}\simeq U^{(d-k)/2}V^{(d+k)/2}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{D^{\prime}}^{\infty}.

Note that
U^{(d-k)/2}V^{(d+k)/2}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{D_{i}}^{\infty}(1)
is not an
element of
\mathit{CFK}^{\infty}(\mathbb{K})
, since it lives in Alexander grading
k
.
In fact,
U^{(d-k)/2}V^{(d+k)/2}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{D_{i}}^{\infty}(1)
is an element of
the subcomplex of
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(\mathbb{K})
of Alexander grading
k
. Multiplication by
V^{-k}
gives a chain
isomorphism between the subset of

\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(\mathbb{K})
in Alexander grading
k
and the
subcomplex of
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{K})
generated over
\mathbb{F}_{2}
by elements

U^{i}V^{j}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{x}}
with
A(\bm{\mathbf{x}})+(i-j)=0
,
i\geq 0
, and
j\geq-k
. The latter is

A_{k}^{-}(\mathbb{K})
, by definition. Hence, from equation (
5.14
), it follows that

\widehat{U}^{(d-k)/2}\cdot[\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{D}^{\infty}(1)]=\widehat{U}^{(d-k)/2}\cdot[\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{D^{\prime}}^{\infty}(1)]\in H_{*}(A_{k}^{-}(\mathbb{K})),

where
\widehat{U}=UV
. It follows that

V_{k}(D,D^{\prime})\leq\frac{d-k}{2},

completing the proof when
k\leq\mu_{\st}(D,D^{\prime})
.

The statement for
k\geq\mu_{\st}(D,D^{\prime})
follows from the statement for

k=\mu_{\st}(D,D^{\prime})
, together with the monotonicity result from Lemma
4.13
.
∎

6.
Regular homotopies and the double point distance

6.1.
The double point distance

If
K
is a knot in
\mathbb{S}^{3}
, we denote by
\Imm(K)
the set of
immersed

connected surfaces in
B^{4}
with boundary
K
. Furthermore, for
g\in\mathbb{N}
, we write
\Imm_{g}(K)
for
the subset of
\Imm(K)
consisting of genus
g
surfaces.
If
S
,
S^{\prime}\in\Imm(K)
, then a
regular homotopy
from
S
to
S^{\prime}
is a 1-parameter family

\{\,S_{t}:t\in I\,\}
in
\Imm(K)
that is continuous in the
C^{\infty}
-topology,
and such that
S_{0}=S
and
S_{1}=S^{\prime}
. If
S
and
S^{\prime}
are regularly homotopic,
then
g(S)=g(S^{\prime})
. For a
generic
regular homotopy, at all but finitely many
t
,
the surface
S_{t}
is embedded away from finitely many transverse double points.
At finitely many
t
, the immersion
S_{t}
has a single non-transverse double point,
where a pair of double points of opposite signs is created or canceled.
In particular, the algebraic number of double points is constant along a generic regular homotopy.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
K
be a knot in
\mathbb{S}^{3}
, and let
g\in\mathbb{N}
. Then any two surfaces
S
,
S^{\prime}\in\Surf_{g}(K)

are regularly homotopic relative to
K
.

Proof.

By extending the proof of Hirsch
[
14
, Theorem 8.2]
using the relative version
of his
h
-principle
[
14
, Theorem 5.9]
,
we obtain that the regular homotopy class of
S
relative to
K

is determined by the relative normal Euler class of
S
. Since
S
is embedded and
[S]=0

in
H_{2}(B^{4},\partial B^{4})
, there is a 3-manifold-with-boundary
M
embedded in
B^{4}
such
that
S\subseteq\partial M
and
\partial M\setminus S\subseteq\mathbb{S}^{3}
is a Seifert surface of
K
.
In particular,
M
induces a normal framing of
S
that restricts to the Seifert framing along
K
.
Hence, the normal Euler class of
S
relative to the Seifert framing vanishes.
Since the same holds for
S^{\prime}
, we obtain that
S
and
S^{\prime}
are regularly homotopic relative to
K
.
∎

The regular homotopy class of a generic immersed surface
S\in\Imm(K)
is determined by the algebraic
number of its double points. If
\{\,S_{t}:t\in I\,\}
is a regular homotopy
such that
S_{0}
is embedded, then the algebraic number of double points of
S_{t}
is zero
for every
t\in I
where
S_{t}
is generic.

Definition 6.2
.

Given an immersed surface
S\in\Imm(K)
, let
\Sing(S)

be the set of its double points (this might be infinite when
S
is not generic).
If
S
,
S^{\prime}\in\Surf_{g}(K)
, then we define

\widetilde{\mu}_{\Sing}(S,S^{\prime}):=\frac{1}{2}\min_{\{\,S_{t}:t\in I\,\}}\max\{\,|\Sing(S_{t})|:t\in I\,\},

where the minimum is taken over all generic regular homotopies
\{\,S_{t}:t\in I\,\}

such that
S_{0}=S
and
S_{1}=S^{\prime}
. Furthermore, we set

\mu_{\Sing}(S,S^{\prime})=\widetilde{\mu}_{\Sing}(S,S^{\prime})+g.

When
S
,
S^{\prime}\in\Surf(K)
and
g(S)\neq g(S^{\prime})
, we set
\mu_{\Sing}(S,S^{\prime})=\infty
.
We call
\mu_{\Sing}(S,S^{\prime})
the
double point distance
between
S
and
S^{\prime}
.

Since
\widetilde{\mu}_{\Sing}(S,S^{\prime})=0
if and only if
S
and
S^{\prime}
are isotopic, the
function
\widetilde{\mu}_{\Sing}
is an ultrametric on
\Surf_{g}(K)
for every
g\in\mathbb{N}
.
Furthermore,
\mu_{\Sing}
is a metric filtration whose normalization is
\widetilde{\mu}_{\Sing}
.
The goal of this section is to prove that, if
S
,
S^{\prime}\in\Surf_{g}(K)
, then

(6.1)

\tau(S,S^{\prime})\leq\mu_{\Sing}(S,S^{\prime}).

If
g>0
, we will also show that

(6.2)

\kappa(S,S^{\prime})\leq\mu_{\Sing}(S,S^{\prime}).

Equations (
6.1
) and (
6.2
)
are proven in Theorems
6.7
and
6.9
.
Finally, in Theorem
6.14
, we will show that
the local
h
-invariants also give lower bounds on
\mu_{\Sing}(S,S^{\prime})
.

6.2.
Movies of immersions and regular homotopies

Suppose that
S\in\Imm(K)
is the image of a proper immersion

f\colon\bar{S}\to B^{4}.

Let
B^{\prime}\subseteq\Int(B^{4})
be a ball disjoint from
S
.
After a suitable identification between
B^{4}\setminus\Int(B^{\prime})
and
I\times S^{3}
,
we can view
S
as an immersed surface in
I\times\mathbb{S}^{3}
,
satisfying
S\cap(\{0\}\times\mathbb{S}^{3})=\emptyset

and
S\cap(\{1\}\times\mathbb{S}^{3})=K
. We can visualize

S
by considering the movie
\{\,S^{s}:s\in I\,\}
,
where
S^{s}
is obtained by projecting
(\{s\}\times\mathbb{S}^{3})\cap S
into
\mathbb{S}^{3}
.
We orient
S^{s}
as the boundary of
([0,s]\times\mathbb{S}^{3})\cap S

using the outward-normal-first convention.

If
S
is generic, then
\pi_{I}\circ f
is a Morse function on
\bar{S}

and the double points are on regular level sets,
where
\pi_{I}\colon I\times\mathbb{S}^{3}\to I
is the projection onto the
I
-factor.
Hence
S^{s}
is an immersed link whenever
s
is a regular value. If
s
is a critical value of
index zero, then an unknotted component is born. If
s
has index one,
the link undergoes a saddle move, and if it has index two,
an unknotted component dies. Generically, passing a double point of
S
locally corresponds
to a crossing change of
S^{s}
; see Gompf–Stipsicz
[
10
, Figure 6.25]
.
We now explain why this is true, and how to read off the intersection sign.

Lemma 6.3
.

Generically, as we pass a positive (negative) double point
p
of
S
,
a negative (positive) crossing of
S^{s}
changes to a positive (negative) crossing; see Figure
6.1
.

Proof.

Suppose that
S
is the image of an immersion
f\colon\bar{S}\looparrowright B^{4}
.
The set of points
x\in\bar{S}
such that
S
is not
transverse to the sets
\{s\}\times\mathbb{S}^{3}
at
f(x)

is generically 0-dimensional, and hence disjoint from the
two preimages of
p
. Hence, generically, passing the double
point
p
corresponds to two strands of
S^{s}
passing through each other.

Write the double point
p\in S\subseteq I\times\mathbb{S}^{3}
as
p=(s_{0},p_{0})
, where
s_{0}\in I
and
p_{0}\in\mathbb{S}^{3}
. Suppose that, at
s=s_{0}
, a negative crossing of
S^{s}
turns into a positive crossing. Let
v_{+}
,
v_{-}\in T_{p_{0}}\mathbb{S}^{3}

denote oriented tangent vectors for the upper and lower strands of the crossing, respectively.
Let
\gamma\colon(s_{0}-\epsilon,s_{0}+\epsilon)\to\mathbb{S}^{3}
denote
the trajectory of a point on the upper strand, chosen to pass through
a point on the lower strand at
s_{0}
. By inspection of the crossing
change, the triple
(v_{+},v_{-},\gamma^{\prime}(s_{0}))
is a positive basis of

T_{p_{0}}\mathbb{S}^{3}
. Using the product orientation on
I\times\mathbb{S}^{3}
,
the 4-tuple
(\partial/\partial s,v_{+},v_{-},\gamma^{\prime}(s_{0}))
is an oriented basis
for
I\times\mathbb{S}^{3}
. It is easy to see that oriented bases
for the tangent spaces of the two sheets of
S
at
p
are

(\partial/\partial s+\gamma^{\prime}(s_{0}),v_{+})\quad\text{and}\quad(\partial/\partial s-\gamma^{\prime}(s_{0}),v_{-}),

respectively, which concatenate to form a positive basis of
I\times\mathbb{S}^{3}
.

A similar argument applies when a positive crossing turns into a negative one at
p
.
∎

Now suppose that
\{\,S_{t}:t\in[-1,1]\,\}
is a generic regular homotopy in
\Imm(K)
,
and that a pair of double points
p_{+}
and
p_{-}
appear as
t
passes
0\in[-1,1]
.
The immersed surface
S_{0}
has a non-transverse double point
p\in B^{4}
.
Write
p=(s_{0},p_{0})
, where
s_{0}\in I
and
p_{0}\in\mathbb{S}^{3}
.

A local model for a double point creation
can be visualized via a 2-parameter family

\{\,S_{t}^{s}:(s,t)\in[s_{0}-\epsilon,s_{0}+\epsilon]\times[-\epsilon,\epsilon]\,\}

of immersed links in
\mathbb{S}^{3}
that is constant outside a neighborhood
N(p_{0})
containing the crossing.
The families
\{\,S_{t}^{s_{0}-\epsilon}:t\in[-\epsilon,\epsilon]\,\}

and
\{\,S_{t}^{s_{0}+\epsilon}:t\in[-\epsilon,\epsilon]\,\}

are constant and have a positive crossing in
N(p_{0})
; we denote this link by
L_{+}
.
For
t<0
, the intersection
S_{t}^{s}\cap N(p_{0})
is a positive crossing
and the family of links
S_{t}^{s}
is embedded (and hence isotopic to
L_{+}
) for all
s\in[s_{0}-\epsilon,s_{0}+\epsilon]
.
For
t>0
, the positive crossing
L_{+}\cap N(p_{0})
changes
to a negative crossing, and then back to a positive crossing. Let
L_{-}
be the link
obtained by changing
L_{+}\cap N(p_{0})
to a negative crossing.
If we fix
t
, self-intersections in the 1-parameter family

\{\,S_{t}^{s}\colon s\in[s_{0}-\epsilon,s_{0}+\epsilon]\,\}
correspond to double points of the surface
the family traces out in
[s_{0}-\epsilon,s_{0}+\epsilon]\times\mathbb{S}^{3}
.
The movie
\{\,S_{t}^{s}:s\in[s_{0}-\epsilon,s_{0}+\epsilon]\,\}
for
t>0
is shown in the top of Figure
6.1
.
We prove the above in the following lemma.

Figure 6.1.
The top row shows a movie of a pair of double points after they have been born during
a regular homotopy of an immersed surface. The bottom row shows the standard model of a Whitney disk
used in the proof, which gives a canonical neighborhood of the pair of canceling double points.

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
\{\,S_{t}:t\in[-1,1]\,\}
be a generic regular homotopy of immersed surfaces such
that a pair of double points is born at time
0
at a point
p\in B^{4}
.
Furthermore, let
B^{\prime}\subseteq\Int(B^{4})
be a ball disjoint from
S_{t}
for every
t\in[-1,1]
.
Then there is an identification of
B^{4}\setminus\Int(B^{\prime})
with
I\times\mathbb{S}^{3}
and an
\epsilon>0

such that, if
p
corresponds to
(s_{0},p_{0})
, the 1-parameter family of immersed links

\{\,S_{t}^{s}:s\in[s_{0}-\epsilon,s_{0}+\epsilon]\,\}

is diffeomorphic to the constant 1-parameter family
L_{+}:=S_{-\epsilon}^{s_{0}-\epsilon}
for
t=-\epsilon
,
where
L_{+}
has a positive crossing in
N(p_{0})
,
and to the 1-parameter family shown on the top row of Figure
6.1
for
t=\epsilon
,
where the positive crossing of
L_{+}
in
N(p_{0})
changes to negative, and then back to positive.

Proof.

Choose an identification between
B^{4}\setminus\Int(B^{\prime})
and
I\times S^{3}
such that

s(p_{-})<s(p_{+})
, where
s
is the
I
-coordinate. We write
S_{t}
as the image of a 1-parameter
family of immersions
f_{t}\colon\bar{S}\looparrowright I\times\mathbb{S}^{3}
. Up to
isotopy, we can express any regular homotopy of a surface in a 4-manifold
as a composition of finger moves and Whitney moves; see Gabai
[
8
, Proposition 4.3]

and Freedman–Quinn
[
7
, Section 1.5]
. In particular,
p_{+}
and
p_{-}
admit a Whitney disk
B
.
A neighborhood of the Whitney disk can be put in the standard form of Milnor
[
31
, Lemma 6.7]
;
see the bottom row of Figure
6.1
.
This is given by an embedding
\varphi\colon U\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\to B^{4}
,
where
U
is a neighborhood of the disk
B_{0}
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
enclosed by arcs
c_{0}
and
c_{0}^{\prime}

that transversely intersect at points
a
and
b
. We have
\varphi(B_{0})=B
,

\varphi(a)=p_{+}
and
\varphi(b)=p_{-}
, and let us write
c=\varphi(c_{0})
and
c^{\prime}=\varphi(c_{0}^{\prime})
.
The preimages of the two branches of
S_{1}
meeting at
p_{+}
and
p_{-}
are
(U\cap c_{0})\times\mathbb{R}\times\{0\}

and
(U\cap c_{0}^{\prime})\times\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}
, respectively. The isotopy
S_{t}
at the finger move
is modeled on the isotopy of
c_{0}^{\prime}
shown in
[
31
, Figure 6.3]

that creates the intersection points
a
and
b
with
c_{0}
.
This isotopy is constant in the normal
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}
direction.

The immersed surface
S_{0}
has a non-transverse double point.
If
x
,
y\in\bar{S}
are the two preimages of the double point,
let
v
denote a generator of the 1-dimensional vector space
(f_{0})_{*}(T_{x}\bar{S})\cap(f_{0})_{*}(T_{y}\bar{S})
.
For
t>0
, the movie
S_{t}^{s}
has an extra pair of double points.
By the choice of the coordinate function
s
, we have
ds(v)\neq 0
and

s(p_{-})<s(p_{+})
. Both
p_{+}
and
p_{-}
correspond to a crossing change in the movie

\{\,S^{s}_{t}:s\in[s_{0}-\epsilon,s_{0}+\epsilon]\,\}
for
t>0
by
Lemma
6.3
. By arranging for the Whitney disk to be symmetric
about
s=s_{0}
in a small neighborhood of
s_{0}
, the movie for the second
double point is obtained by reversing the movie for the first double point.
When
t<0
, the curves
c_{0}
and
c_{0}^{\prime}
become disjoint,
and so the movie
\{\,S_{t}^{s}:s\in[s_{0}-\epsilon,s_{0}+\epsilon]\,\}
is just an isotopy of the link
L_{+}
,
completing the proof.
∎

6.3.
The desingularization of an immersed surface

Definition 6.5
.

Suppose
S\in\Imm(K)
is a generic immersed surface in
B^{4}
; i.e., an immersion
with only transverse double points. The
desingularization
of
(B^{4},S)

is the link cobordism
(B^{4}(S),\widehat{S})
obtained as follows:

(1)

The 4-manifold
B^{4}(S)
is constructed by blowing up the 4-manifold at each
negative
double point of
S
.
Topologically, this corresponds to connected summing with
\overline{\mathbb{CP}}^{2}
.

(2)

The surface
\widehat{S}
is constructed from the proper transform of
S
in
B^{4}(S)

by resolving each
positive
double point (increasing the genus of
S
by 1 at each point).

Definition
6.5
makes sense for any immersed oriented link cobordism as well.
For a movie presentation of the resolution of a positive double point, see Figure
6.2
,
taken from the book of Gompf and Stipsicz
[
10
, Figure 6.30]
.
For a movie of the blowup of a negative double point, see Figure
6.3
. The 4-dimensional
2-handle of
\overline{\mathbb{CP}}^{2}
is attached along a
(-1)
-framed unknot that links the negative crossing of
L_{+}
.

Figure 6.2.
Resolving a positive double point. The top row is the singular knot cobordism.
The bottom is our choice of resolution.

Figure 6.3.
Resolving a negative double point. The top is the singular knot cobordism.
The bottom is the resolution, obtained by blowing up the surface at the double point.

Let
(W,\mathcal{F})
with
\mathcal{F}=(S,\mathcal{A})
be an immersed, decorated link cobordism,
such that the double points are disjoint from
\mathcal{A}
. Furthermore, suppose that
the two branches meeting at a double point either both lie in
S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
, or
they both lie in
S_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}
. We write
(W(S),\widehat{\mathcal{F}})
for the decorated
link cobordism with double points resolved as described above.

Lemma 6.6
.

Suppose
(W_{0},\mathcal{F}_{0})
is a non-singular link cobordism, and
(W,\mathcal{F})
with
\mathcal{F}=(S,\mathcal{A})
is
obtained from
(W_{0},\mathcal{F}_{0})
by a double point birth, corresponding to a tangency
between either two branches of
S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
, or two branches of
S_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}
.
Let
(W(S),\widehat{\mathcal{F}})
denote the resolved link cobordism, as described above.
Then
W(S)=W_{0}\#\overline{\mathbb{CP}}^{2}
, and

\widehat{\mathcal{F}}
is obtained from
\mathcal{F}_{0}
by a 1-handle stabilization
along
S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
or
S_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}
, and disjoint from
\overline{\mathbb{CP}}^{2}
.

Let
\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}
be a
\Spin^{c}
structure on
\widehat{W}
such that

\langle\,c_{1}(\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}),E\,\rangle=\pm 1
, where
E
denotes the exceptional divisor
in
\widehat{W}
, and agrees with
\mathfrak{s}
on
W
. Then

F_{\widehat{W},\widehat{\mathcal{F}},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}}\simeq U\cdot F_{W,\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{s}}

if the double points both occur in
S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
, and

F_{\widehat{W},\widehat{\mathcal{F}},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}}\simeq V\cdot F_{W,\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{s}},

if the double points both occur in
S_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
6.4
, there is a movie presentation of
\mathcal{F}
as in the top of Figure
6.1
,
where a positive crossing changes to negative, and then back to positive.
Furthermore, the movie of the decorated surface
\mathcal{F}_{0}
only differs from that of
\mathcal{F}

by locally changing the above movie to one where the positive crossing stays positive.

We consider the composition of the resolutions of the positive and negative
double points shown in Figures
6.2
and
6.3
; see the top
row of Figure
6.4
. We can arrange
that the resolution of the negative double point occurs immediately before
the resolution of the positive double point. The resolution of the positive
double point is a pair of saddles, corresponding to attaching bands
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
.
The composition of the two resolutions can be rearranged such that we first attach the band
B_{1}
,
then attach a 4-dimensional 2-handle along the
-1
framed unknot
\mathcal{U}
, and
finally attach the band
B_{2}
; see the second row of Figure
6.4
.

Figure 6.4.
In the top row, we show the movie of the resolution of a canceling
pair of double points, which consists of a blowup followed by two band moves.
We then commute the blowup and the first band move, giving rise to the movie in
the second row. Finally, we slide the second band over the
(-1)
-framed 2-handle,
giving the third row. The two bands form a tube, and the blowup now happens away
from the surface.

We can slide the band
B_{2}
over
\mathcal{U}
, though it gains a full right-handed
twist when we do this; see the third row of Figure
6.4
.
The unknot
\mathcal{U}
is now totally unlinked from the knot
and bands, and
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
are simply dual bands,
corresponding to a 1-handle stabilization of the surface
\mathcal{F}_{0}

disjoint from the
-1
framed unknot giving the
\overline{\mathbb{CP}}^{2}
summand of
W(S)
.

Applying Lemma
5.4
for the effect of the 1-handle stabilization,
and using the standard blow-up formula for
-1
surgery on an unknot
contained in a ball disjoint from the link, we see that the composition is multiplication by either
U
or

V
, depending on whether the 1-handle is added to

\Sigma_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
or
\Sigma_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}
. Using the composition law, the proof is complete.
∎

6.4.
Tau, nu, and the double point distance

We now prove that
\tau
gives a lower bound on
\mu_{\Sing}
:

Theorem 6.7
.

If
S
,
S^{\prime}\in\Surf_{g}(K)
, then

\tau(S,S^{\prime})\leq\mu_{\Sing}(S,S^{\prime}).

Proof.

Suppose that
\{\,S_{t}:t\in I\,\}
is a generic regular homotopy between
embedded surfaces
S
,
S^{\prime}\in\Surf(K)
. The immersion
S_{t}
fails to
be self-transverse at times
s_{1},\dots,s_{n-1}\in(0,1)
.
Pick a point
t_{i}\in(s_{i-1},s_{i})
for every
i\in\{2,\dots,n-1\}
,
and let
S_{i}=S_{t_{i}}
. We write
S_{1}=S
and
S_{n}=S^{\prime}
.
Let
(B^{4}(S_{i}),\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{i})
denote the desingularization of
(B^{4},S_{i})
, as described in
Definition
6.5
. Let
\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{i}
denote any
maximal
\Spin^{c}
structure on
B^{4}(S_{i})
(i.e.,

c_{1}(\mathfrak{s})^{2}+b_{2}(B^{4}(S_{i}))=0
), such that
\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{i+1}
is obtained by
blowing up or blowing down
\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{i}
.

We decorate each
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{i}
such that the type-
\bm{\mathbf{w}}
region is a bigon along

K
, and the rest of
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{i}
is of type-
\bm{\mathbf{z}}
. In particular, all double
points occur in regions of type-
\bm{\mathbf{z}}
. If
S_{i}
is obtained from
S_{i-1}

via a double point birth, then Lemma
6.6
implies that

F_{B^{4}(S_{i+1}),\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{i+1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{i+1}}\simeq V\cdot F_{B^{4}(S_{i}),\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{i},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{i}}.

Similarly, if
S_{i+1}
is obtained from
S_{i}
via a double point cancellation, then

V\cdot F_{B^{4}(S_{i+1}),\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{i+1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{i+1}}\simeq F_{B^{4}(S_{i}),\widehat{\mathcal{F}}_{i},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{i}}.

It follows that

V^{n}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{\infty}\simeq V^{n}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{\infty},

where
n
is the maximal number of positive double points of any
S_{i}
. Since the algebraic count of double points
of each
S_{i}
is zero, we have
n=\frac{1}{2}\max\{\,|\Sing(S_{t})|:t\in I\,\}
.
It now follows from Lemma
4.6
that

\tau(S,S^{\prime})\leq\frac{1}{2}\max\{\,|\Sing(S_{t})|:t\in I\,\}+g.

Hence
\tau(S,S^{\prime})\leq\mu_{\Sing}(S,S^{\prime})
, as claimed.
∎

We now show that
\nu
, introduced in Section
4.4
,
gives a slightly better lower bound on the stabilization distance and the double point distance
than
\tau
.

Proposition 6.8
.

If
S
,
S^{\prime}\in\Surf(K)
, then

(6.3)

\nu(S,S^{\prime})\leq\min\{\mu_{\st}(S,S^{\prime}),\mu_{\Sing}(S,S^{\prime})\}.

Proof.

Let
\mathbb{K}
be
K
decorated with two basepoints, and write
g=g(S)
and
g^{\prime}=g(S^{\prime})
.
By Theorems
5.13
and
6.7
,
if
n
is either
\mu_{\st}(S,S^{\prime})
or
\mu_{\Sing}(S,S^{\prime})
, then

\tau(S,S^{\prime})\leq n
(recall that
\mu_{\Sing}(S,S^{\prime})=\infty
when
g\neq g^{\prime}
,
so the inequality obviously holds in this case).
Furthermore, their proofs imply that

(6.4)

V^{n-g}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{\infty}\simeq V^{n-g^{\prime}}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{\infty}.

If
\tau(S,S^{\prime})<n
, then equation (
6.3
) automatically holds,
since
\nu(S,S^{\prime})\leq\tau(S,S^{\prime})+1
,
so it is sufficient to consider the case when
\tau(S,S^{\prime})=n
.
It follows from equation (
6.4
) that

(6.5)

V^{-g}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}^{-}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(1)-V^{-g^{\prime}}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}^{-}_{S^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(1)=\partial x

for some
x\in C(\mathbb{K},i\geq 0,j\geq-n)
. It follows that the elements

\bm{\mathbf{t}}^{-}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(1)
and
\bm{\mathbf{t}}^{-}_{S^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(1)
agree in the homology
of any quotient of
C(\mathbb{K},i\geq 0,j\geq-n)
by a filtered subcomplex.
Since
C(\mathbb{K},{\vphantom{L}}_{n}L_{m})
is the quotient of
C(\mathbb{K},i\geq 0,j\geq-n)
by a
filtered subcomplex for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
, it follows that

[V^{-g}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)-V^{-g^{\prime}}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)]=0\in H_{*}(C(\mathbb{K},{\vphantom{L}}_{n}L_{m}))

for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Hence
\tau^{\prime}(S,S^{\prime})=-\infty
and
\nu(S,S^{\prime})=\tau(S,S^{\prime})=n
.
∎

6.5.
Kappa and the double point distance

In this section, we show that the kappa invariant also gives a lower bound on the double point distance.

Theorem 6.9
.

Let
K
be a knot in
\mathbb{S}^{3}
.
If
S
,
S^{\prime}\in\Surf_{g}(K)
and
g>0
, then

\kappa(S,S^{\prime})\leq\mu_{\Sing}(S,S^{\prime}).

The proof requires several steps.
Suppose that
S\in\Imm(K)
is a generic,
properly immersed surface in
B^{4}
(by generic, we mean
S
has a discrete collection
of transverse double points, disjoint from
\partial S
).
The surface
S
is the image of an immersion
f\colon\bar{S}\looparrowright B^{4}
, and
write
\widehat{S}\subseteq B^{4}(S)
for the desingularization of
S
.
Let

\bm{\mathbf{p}}^{+}\subseteq\bar{S}

denote the preimages of the positive double points of
S
.
Note that each positive double point of
S
contributes two
points to
\bm{\mathbf{p}}^{+}
. Let
P
be a subset of the positive double points of
S
.

Definition 6.10
.

Suppose that
S\in\Imm(K)
is a generic immersed surface.
Let
T\subseteq\bar{S}
denote an embedded tree such that the following hold:

(
T1
)

T\cap\partial\bar{S}
consists of a single point.

(
T2
)

Each point of
f^{-1}(P)
is a leaf of
T
,
and
T
is disjoint from
\bm{\mathbf{p}}^{+}\setminus f^{-1}(P)
.

Given such a tree
T
, we define an induced decoration
\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}(T)
on
\bar{S}
, as follows.
The underlying dividing set of
\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}(T)
is
\partial N(T)\setminus\partial\bar{S}
. We declare
N(T)

to be the type-
\bm{\mathbf{z}}
subregion, and the complement of
N(T)
to be the type-
\bm{\mathbf{w}}
subregion.
We note that the decoration
\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}(T)
on
\bar{S}
induces a decoration on the desingularized surface

\widehat{S}\subseteq B^{4}(S)
, for which we also write
\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}(T)
. There is an analogous decoration
\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(T)
, obtained by reversing the roles of
\bm{\mathbf{w}}
and
\bm{\mathbf{z}}
.

Note that
g(\widehat{S}_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}})=g(\bar{S})+|\bm{\mathbf{p}}^{+}|/2-|P|
and
g(\widehat{S}_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}})=|P|
.
We now prove the following, somewhat surprising fact:

Proposition 6.11
.

Suppose that
S\in\Imm(K)
is a generic immersed surface in
B^{4}
with boundary
K
,
and that
P
is a subset of the positive double points of
S
. Let
T\subseteq\bar{S}
be a tree satisfying
conditions (
T1
) and (
T2
). If
\mathfrak{s}\in\Spin^{c}(B^{4}(S))
, the chain homotopy type of the map

F_{B^{4}(S),(\widehat{S},\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}(T)),\mathfrak{s}}\colon\mathcal{R}^{\infty}\to\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{K})

is independent of the choice of tree
T
.

To prove Proposition
6.11
, we need a set of moves that can be used
to connect two trees satisfying conditions (
T1
) and (
T2
).
We introduce the following
tree-moves
:

(
TM1
)

T
is replaced by another tree
T^{\prime}
satisfying (
T1
) and (
T2
) such that
\partial N(T)
and
\partial N(T^{\prime})
are isotopic through dividing sets which are fixed on
\partial\bar{S}
and never intersect
\bm{\mathbf{p}}^{+}
.

(
TM2
)

Suppose that
e
is an edge of
T
which contains a point
p
of
\bm{\mathbf{p}}^{+}
, and that
e^{\prime}
is an embedded path in
\bar{S}
such that
e^{\prime}\cap T=\partial e^{\prime}
, and such that
e\cap e^{\prime}
consists of a single point
t
. The tree
T
is replaced with the tree
T^{\prime}
formed by adding
e^{\prime}
, and removing a segment of
e
that is not between
p
and
t
; see the top row of Figure
6.5
.

Lemma 6.12
.

If
\bar{S}
is a surface,
\bm{\mathbf{p}}^{+}\subseteq\bar{S}
is a collection of points,
and
f^{-1}(P)\subseteq\bm{\mathbf{p}}^{+}
is a chosen subset, then any two trees satisfying (
T1
)
and (
T2
) can be connected by moves (
TM1
) and (
TM2
).

Proof.

We pick a subset
A\subseteq\bar{S}
consisting of
g(\bar{S})
compressing
curves on
\bar{S}
such that we are left with a disk after surgering

\bar{S}
on
A
. If
T
is a tree satisfying (
T1
) and
(
T2
), we give
T
the partial order determined by setting
T\cap\partial S
to be the maximal point. If
t\in T
, define

L(t)=\{x\in T:x\leq t\}.

As a first step, we show that any tree
T
satisfying conditions
(
T1
) and (
T2
) can be connected by moves
(
TM1
) and (
TM2
) to a tree which is disjoint
from
A
. To establish this, we show that if
|A\cap T|>0
, we can always
reduce
|A\cap T|
by 1, using moves (
TM1
) and (
TM2
).
To do this, we pick any point
t\in A\cap T
such

L(t)\cap A=\emptyset
. There are two cases: either
L(t)\cap\bm{\mathbf{p}}^{+}=\emptyset
or
L(t)\cap\bm{\mathbf{p}}^{+}\neq\emptyset
.

If
L(t)\cap\bm{\mathbf{p}}^{+}=\emptyset
, then we can just isotope
L(t)
(an
instance of move (
TM1
)) so that it no longer intersects
A
,
thus reducing
|A\cap T|
.

Next, we consider the case that
L(t)\cap\bm{\mathbf{p}}^{+}\neq\emptyset
. In this
case, we pick a point
t^{\prime}\in L(t)
such that
L(t^{\prime})
is a subset of a single
edge of
T
, and
L(t^{\prime})
contains a point
p\in\bm{\mathbf{p}}^{+}
. We let
e^{\prime}
be any
embedded path in
S\setminus A
such that
e^{\prime}\cap T=\partial e^{\prime}
, and
\partial e^{\prime}

consists of
t^{\prime}
and another point of
T
which is not contained in
L(t)
.
Let
e
denote a subinterval of the edge of
T
containing
t^{\prime}
, such that

t^{\prime}
is the smaller endpoint of
e
. We can then
use move (
TM2
) to replace
e
with
e^{\prime}
. This reduces

L(t)\cap\bm{\mathbf{p}}^{+}
by 1, and does not increase
|A\cap T|
. Repeating this
procedure, we may reduce to the case that
L(t)\cap\bm{\mathbf{p}}^{+}=\emptyset
.
Arguing as before, an isotopy of
L(t)
can then be used to reduce
|A\cap T|
by 1.

Hence, if
T
and
T^{\prime}
are two trees satisfying conditions (
T1
)
and (
T2
), by applying moves (
TM1
) and
(
TM2
), we may assume that
T
and
T^{\prime}
are both disjoint from

A
. We may compress
\bar{S}
along
A
to get a disk
D
, containing
T
and

T^{\prime}
, as well as a collection of
2g(\bar{S})
points
\bm{\mathbf{p}}
corresponding to the
curves in
A
. We note that
T
and
T^{\prime}
are disjoint from
\bm{\mathbf{p}}
.
Furthermore, isotoping an edge of
T
or
T^{\prime}
across a point in
\bm{\mathbf{p}}
may
be achieved by move (
TM2
). In this manner, we can reduce the
claim to the case that
S
is a disk, and it is straightforward to see that
in this situation that
T
and
T^{\prime}
can be related by applying move (
TM1
).
∎

Proof of Proposition
6.11
.

By Lemma
6.12
, it is sufficient to show invariance
of
F_{B^{4}(S),(\widehat{S},\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}(T)),\mathfrak{s}}

under moves (
TM1
) and (
TM2
).
First note that, up to isotopy, the decoration
\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}(T)

depends only on a regular neighborhood of
T\subseteq\bar{S}
,
so move (
TM1
) does not change the cobordism map

F_{B^{4}(S),(\widehat{S},\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}(T)),\mathfrak{s}}
.

We now address move (
TM2
).
Suppose that
e
is an edge of
T
which has exactly
one endpoint at a point
p\in\bm{\mathbf{p}}^{+}
and another
at a vertex
v\in T\setminus\bm{\mathbf{p}}^{+}
. Suppose that

e^{\prime}
is an embedded path on
\bar{S}
such that
e^{\prime}\cap T=\partial e^{\prime}
and
e\cap e^{\prime}=\{t\}
.
Let
T^{\prime}
denote the tree obtained by removing a segment of
e
not between
t
and
p
,
and inserting
e^{\prime}
. There is a bypass relation

(6.6)

F_{B^{4}(S),(\widehat{S},\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}(T)),\mathfrak{s}}+F_{B^{4}(S),(\widehat{S},\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}(T^{\prime})),\mathfrak{s}}+F_{B^{4}(S),(\widehat{S},\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}),\mathfrak{s}}\simeq 0

for a third decoration
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\widehat{S}
, which is shown in Figure
6.5
.

Figure 6.5.
The graphs
T
and
T^{\prime}
on the top row are related by move (
TM2
).
The point
p
is in
\bm{\mathbf{p}}^{+}
. On the bottom row, the associated
decorations
\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}(T)
and
\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}(T^{\prime})
on the desingularized surface
\widehat{S}
are shown,
as well as a third decoration
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
, which fits
into a bypass triple with
\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}(T)
and
\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}(T^{\prime})
.
The dashed circles denote where a tube is added on the desingularized surface.

We now claim that

(6.7)

F_{B^{4}(S),(\widehat{S},\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}),\mathfrak{s}}\simeq 0

for any
\mathfrak{s}\in\Spin^{c}(B^{4}(S))
, which will complete the proof when
combined with equation (
6.6
).
The key observation is that the dividing set
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
has a closed loop,
which is homologically essential in the tube that
is added to form the desingularized surface
\widehat{S}
. Let

B
be a 4-ball in
B^{4}(S)
containing the point
f(p)
.
We note that
\widehat{S}
intersects
\partial B
in a negative Hopf link
H
.
Let
A_{0}
denote the annulus that is inserted into
B

to form the desingularized surface
\widehat{S}
. By isotoping

\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
in
\widehat{S}
, we may assume that
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}

intersects
A_{0}
in the dividing set
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\prime\prime}
shown in Figure
6.6
.

Figure 6.6.
On the left is the annulus
A_{0}\subseteq\widehat{S}
,
which is added to desingularize the immersed surface
S
at the
negative double point
f(p)
. The decoration

\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}_{0}
on
A_{0}
is shown. On the right is
the dividing set corresponding to the map
\Phi_{w}\circ\Psi_{z}
,
on a cylindrical link cobordism.

Let us write
\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}
for
(\widehat{S},\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime})
and
\mathcal{F}_{0}^{\prime\prime}=(A_{0},\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\prime\prime})
.
Using the composition law, it is sufficient to show that

F_{B,\mathcal{F}_{0}^{\prime\prime},\mathfrak{s}_{0}}\simeq 0,

where
\mathfrak{s}_{0}
is the unique torsion
\Spin^{c}
structure on
B
.

Let
\mathbb{H}^{+}
denote the positive Hopf link, decorated with basepoints

w_{1}
and
z_{1}
on one component, and
w_{2}
and
z_{2}

on the other. By isotoping the dividing set on
\mathcal{F}_{0}^{\prime\prime}
,
we may factor the map
F_{B,\mathcal{F}_{0}^{\prime\prime},\mathfrak{s}_{0}}
through

\Phi_{w_{1}}\Psi_{z_{1}}\colon\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(\mathbb{H}^{+})\to\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(\mathbb{H}^{+}).

Figure 6.7.
The positive Hopf link (left), and a genus zero Heegaard diagram (right).

Figure 6.8.
The chain complex
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(\mathbb{H}^{+})
, as well as
the maps
\Phi_{w_{1}}
and
\Psi_{z_{1}}
.

A diagram for
\mathbb{H}^{+}
is shown in Figure
6.7
.
The differential can be computed by simply counting bigons.
It is not hard to see that the complex
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(\mathbb{H}^{+})

is chain homotopy equivalent to the complex shown in
Figure
6.8
. The chain homotopy type
of the maps
\Phi_{w_{1}}
and
\Psi_{z_{1}}
are also
shown in Figure
6.8
.
Examining the maps
\Phi_{w_{1}}
and
\Psi_{z_{1}}
shown
in Figure
6.8
, we see that
\Phi_{w_{1}}\Psi_{z_{1}}
vanishes, completing the proof.
∎

Definition 6.13
.

Given a generic immersed surface
S\in\Imm(K)
that is the image of an immersion
f\colon\bar{S}\looparrowright B^{4}
,
as well as a subset
P
of the positive double points of
S
,
pick a tree
T\subseteq\bar{S}
satisfying conditions (
T1
) and (
T2
).
We form the decoration
\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}(T)
of the
desingularization
(B^{4}(S),\widehat{S})
as in Definition
6.10
.
For
\mathfrak{s}\in\Spin^{c}(B^{4}(S))
, we define the map

\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,P,\bm{\mathbf{w}},\mathfrak{s}}^{-}\colon\mathcal{R}^{-}\to\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(\mathbb{K})

to be the decorated link cobordism map
F_{B^{4}(S),(\widehat{S},\mathcal{A}_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}(T)),\mathfrak{s}}
,
which is independent of the choice of
T
by Proposition
6.11
.
Analogously, we can define the map

\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,P,\bm{\mathbf{z}},\mathfrak{s}}^{-}\colon\mathcal{R}^{-}\to\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(\mathbb{K}).

We can now prove that
\kappa(S,S^{\prime})
is a lower bound for
\mu_{\Sing}(S,S^{\prime})
:

Proof of Theorem
6.9
.

Suppose that
S_{1},\dots,S_{n}
is a sequence of
immersed surfaces, each of which is obtained from the
previous via creating or canceling a pair of double points,
up to diffeomorphism fixing
\partial B^{4}
pointwise, and
S_{1}=S
and
S_{n}=S^{\prime}
.
Furthermore, let
P_{k}
be the set of all positive double points of
S_{k}
for
k\in\{1,\dots,n\}
.

Using Lemma
6.6
, we conclude that, if

m:=\frac{1}{2}\max\left\{|\Sing(S_{1})|,\dots,|\Sing(S_{n})|\right\},

and
\mathfrak{s}_{1},\dots,\mathfrak{s}_{n}
is a stabilization sequence of
\Spin^{c}

structures on
B^{4}(S_{1}),\dots,B^{4}(S_{n})
that are all maximal,
then the filtered chain homotopy types of the maps

V^{m-\tfrac{1}{2}|\Sing(S_{i})|}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S_{k},P_{k},\bm{\mathbf{w}},\mathfrak{s}}^{-}

coincide for
k\in\{1,\dots,n\}
. In particular, the maps on
\mathit{CFK}^{-}_{U=0}
coincide, so

V^{m}\cdot[t_{S,\bm{\mathbf{w}}}^{-}(1)]=V^{m}\cdot[t_{S^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{w}}}^{-}(1)]\text{ in }\mathit{HFK}^{-}_{U=0}(\mathbb{K}),

and hence
\kappa(S,S^{\prime})\leq m+g
.
∎

6.6.
The local
h
-invariants and the double point distance

In this section, we show the following:

Theorem 6.14
.

Suppose that
S
,
S^{\prime}\in\Surf_{g}(K)
and
g\leq k\leq\mu_{\Sing}(S,S^{\prime})
. Then

V_{k}(S,S^{\prime})\leq\left\lceil\frac{\mu_{\Sing}(S,S^{\prime})-k}{2}\right\rceil.

If
k\geq\mu_{\Sing}(S,S^{\prime})
, then
V_{k}(S,S^{\prime})=0
.

Definition 6.15
.

Suppose that
S\in\Imm(K)
is a generic immersed surface. We denote by
P^{+}
the set of positive
double points of
S
. Furthermore, let
d
be an integer satisfying
d\geq g(S)+|P^{+}|
. We say that
S

satisfies the
singular, decoration-independence condition (SDI) above degree
d
if the following holds:

(
SDI
)

For all
maximal
\mathfrak{s}\in\Spin^{c}(B^{4}(S))
, for every
i
,
j\in\mathbb{N}
satisfying

g(S)+|P^{+}|+i+j\geq d,

and for all
P\subseteq P^{+}
, the chain homotopy type of the map

U^{i}V^{j}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}^{-}_{S,P,\bm{\mathbf{z}},\mathfrak{s}}\colon\mathcal{R}^{-}\to\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(\mathbb{K})

depends only on
\mathfrak{s}
and the quantities

i+|P|\quad\text{and}\quad j+|P^{+}|-|P|.

Lemma 6.16
.

Let
S\in\Imm(K)
be a generic immersion with positive double points
P^{+}
, and
suppose that
S^{\prime}
is obtained from
S
by the birth of a pair of double points.
Write
p^{+}
and
p^{-}
for the new positive and negative double points of
S^{\prime}
, respectively.
If
S
satisfies the singular decoration-independence condition (
SDI
) above degree
d
, then
S^{\prime}
satisfies condition (
SDI
) above degree

\max\{d,g(S^{\prime})+|P^{+}\cup\{p^{+}\}|\}.

Similarly, if
S^{\prime}
satisfies (
SDI
) above degree
d
, then so does
S
.

Proof.

Consider first the case that
S
satisfies condition (
SDI
) above degree
d
.
Let
\mathfrak{s}\in\Spin^{c}(B^{4}(S))
and
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}\in\Spin^{c}(B^{4}(S^{\prime}))
be such that
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}
agrees with
\mathfrak{s}
on

B^{4}(S)\setminus N(p^{-})
and
\langle\,c_{1}(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}),E\,\rangle=\pm 1
,
where
E
is the exceptional divisor that appears after blowing up
B^{4}(S)
at
p^{-}
.

Let
P\subseteq P^{+}
and
P^{\prime}\subseteq P^{+}\cup\{p^{+}\}
.
By Lemma
6.6
, we have that

(6.8)

U^{i}V^{j}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}^{-}_{S^{\prime},P^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}},\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}}\simeq\begin{cases}U^{i+1}V^{j}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}^{-}_{S,P^{\prime}\setminus\{p^{+}\},\bm{\mathbf{z}},\mathfrak{s}}&\text{ if }p^{+}\in P^{\prime}\\
U^{i}V^{j+1}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}^{-}_{S,P^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}},\mathfrak{s}}&\text{ if }p^{+}\not\in P^{\prime}.\end{cases}

Since
S
satisfies condition (
SDI
) above degree
d
, the
expression on the right side of equation (
6.8
)
depends only on the quantities
i+|P^{\prime}|
and
j+1+|P^{+}|-|P^{\prime}|
, as long as

i+j+1+|P^{+}|+g(S)\geq d.

Hence
S^{\prime}
satisfies condition (
SDI
) above degree
d
if
d>g(S)+|P^{+}|
,
or above degree
d+1
if
d=g(S)+|P^{+}|
.

Next, we suppose that
S^{\prime}
satisfies condition (
SDI
)
above degree

d\geq g(S^{\prime})+|P^{+}\cup\{p^{+}\}|=g(S^{\prime})+|P^{+}|+1.

The stabilization formula from equation (
6.8
)
shows that
S
satisfies condition (
SDI
) above degree
d
, as well.
∎

We can now prove that
V_{k}(S,S^{\prime})
gives a lower bound on
\mu_{\Sing}(S,S^{\prime})
:

Proof of Theorem
6.14
.

Suppose first that
g\leq k\leq\mu_{\Sing}(S,S^{\prime})
.
Pick a sequence of generic immersed surfaces
S_{1},\dots,S_{n}\in\Imm(K)
such that
consecutive surfaces differ by the birth or death of a pair of double points.
Furthermore, assume that
S_{1}=S
and
S_{n}=S^{\prime}
. Note that, trivially,
S
and
S^{\prime}
satisfy the singular decoration-independence condition (
SDI
) above degree
g:=g(S)=g(S^{\prime})
.
We pick a stabilization sequence of maximal
\Spin^{c}
structures
\mathfrak{s}_{1},\dots,\mathfrak{s}_{n}

on
B^{4}(S_{1}),\dots,B^{4}(S_{n})
, respectively.

Write
m=\tfrac{1}{2}\max\{|\Sing(S_{1})|,\dots,|\Sing(S_{n})|\}
. By
Lemma
6.16
, each of the immersed surfaces

S_{i}
satisfies condition (
SDI
) above degree
g+m
.
By adding one additional birth-death pair of double points, if necessary,
we may assume that
\frac{m+g-k}{2}
and
\frac{m-g+k}{2}
are both integers.
Note also that both expressions are nonnegative, since
g\leq k\leq m+g
by assumption.
Let
P_{l}^{+}
be the set of positive double points of
S_{l}
for
l\in\{1,\dots,n\}
.
Since

\frac{m+g-k}{2}+\frac{m-g+k}{2}=m

and
|P_{l}^{+}|\leq m
for all
l
, we can pick subsets
P_{l}\subseteq P_{l}^{+}
such that

|P_{l}|\leq\frac{m+g-k}{2}\qquad\text{and}\qquad|P_{l}^{+}|-|P_{l}|\leq\frac{m-g+k}{2}.

We then pick sequences of nonnegative integers
i_{l}
and
j_{l}
such that

i_{l}+|P_{l}|=\frac{m+g-k}{2}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad j_{l}+|P_{l}^{+}|-|P_{l}|=\frac{m-g+k}{2}

for all
1\leq l\leq n
.

Using our computation of the effect of a double point birth from
Lemma
6.6
, as well as
Lemma
6.16
to change the decorations as
needed, we conclude that the maps

U^{i_{l}}V^{j_{l}}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}^{-}_{S_{l},P_{l},\bm{\mathbf{z}},\mathfrak{s}_{l}}\colon\mathcal{R}^{-}\to\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{-}(\mathbb{K})

are all filtered chain homotopic to one another. In particular,

(6.9)

U^{\frac{g+m-k}{2}}V^{\frac{m-g+k}{2}}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}\simeq U^{\frac{g+m-k}{2}}V^{\frac{m-g+k}{2}}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}.

The maps in equation (
6.9
) increase the Alexander grading by
k
.
Multiplying equation (
6.9
) by
V^{-k}
and rearranging, we conclude that

\widehat{U}^{\frac{g+m-k}{2}}\cdot[V^{-g}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)]=\widehat{U}^{\frac{g+m-k}{2}}\cdot[V^{-g}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{S^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}(1)]\text{ in }H_{*}(A_{k}^{-}(\mathbb{K})),

completing the proof for
k\leq\mu_{\Sing}(S,S^{\prime})
.

To verify that
V_{k}(S,S^{\prime})=0
when
k\geq\mu_{\Sing}(S,S^{\prime})
,
we note that the above result implies that
V_{\mu_{\Sing}(S,S^{\prime})}(S,S^{\prime})=0
.
The monotonicity result from Lemma
4.13
then implies the claim for
k>\mu_{\Sing}(S,S^{\prime})
.
∎

7.
Upsilon and an infinite family of topological metric filtrations

Let
K
be a knot in
\mathbb{S}^{3}
, and let
S
,
S^{\prime}\in\Surf(K)
.
The invariant
\Upsilon_{(S,S^{\prime})}
gives a family of algebraically defined functions

\Upsilon_{(S,S^{\prime})}(t)\colon\Surf(K)\times\Surf(K)\to\mathbb{R}^{\geq 0}

parametrized by
t\in[0,2]
. In this section, we describe
a topologically defined family
M_{(S,S^{\prime})}(t)

of functions that are bounded below by
\Upsilon_{(S,S^{\prime})}(t)
.

7.1.
The topological
M
-metric on
\Surf(K)

The topological
M
-metric will be defined using the following
generalized stabilization operation:

Definition 7.1
.

Suppose that
(W,S)
bounds
(\mathbb{S}^{3},K)
, and that
B^{4}\subseteq\Int(W)
is an
embedded 4-ball such that
\partial B^{4}\cap S
is an
n
-component unlink
U_{n}
. Further,
suppose that
B^{4}\cap S
consists of disks
D_{1},\dots,D_{n}
that can be
smoothly isotoped into
\partial B^{4}
relative to
U_{n}
. We say that
(W^{\prime},S^{\prime})
is a
generalized
stabilization
of
(W,S)
if it is formed by removing
(B^{4},S\cap B^{4})
from

(W,S)
, and gluing in a link cobordism
(X_{0},S_{0})
such that the following hold:

(1)

(X_{0},S_{0})
is a cobordism from
\emptyset
to
(\partial B^{4},U_{n})
,

(2)

b_{2}^{+}(X_{0})=b_{1}(X_{0})=0
,

(3)

S_{0}
is connected and oriented.

We remark that, although Definition
7.1

clearly generalizes the stabilization operation from
Section
2.8
, it may still seem somewhat
unmotivated. We note that, after a double point creation, the desingularization of an
immersed surface changes by a generalized stabilization:

Example 7.2
.

Suppose that
S
is an immersed surface in
B^{4}
which bounds
K
, and
S^{\prime}
is
obtained from
S
by a creation of a pair of canceling double points. If

(W,\widehat{S})
and
(W^{\prime},\widehat{S}^{\prime})
denote their desingularizations, as defined
in Definition
6.5
, then
(W^{\prime},\widehat{S}^{\prime})
can be
obtained from
(W,\widehat{S})
by a generalized stabilization. Indeed, since a
double point creation can be achieved by a finger move supported in a
neighborhood of a path
\lambda
connecting two points on
S
, we can take

B^{4}
to be a neighborhood of
\lambda
. Clearly,
\partial B^{4}
intersects
S

along two disks, and
S\cap\partial B^{4}
is a 2-component unlink. Since
S^{\prime}

differs from
S
only inside
B^{4}
, the desingularization
(W,\widehat{S})
can
be obtained by cutting out
B^{4}
and gluing in
(\overline{\mathbb{CP}}^{2}\setminus B^{4},S_{0})
for an annulus
S_{0}
in
\overline{\mathbb{CP}}^{2}\setminus B^{4}
.

If
W
is a compact, oriented 4-manifold with boundary
\mathbb{S}^{3}
, we let

\Char(W)
denote the set of
characteristic vectors
of the
intersection form
Q_{W}
; i.e., the set of elements
C\in H^{2}(W)\cong H^{2}(W,\partial W)
such that

\langle\,x\cup x,[W,\partial W]\,\rangle\equiv\langle\,C\cup x,[W,\partial W]\,\rangle\mod 2

for every
x\in H^{2}(W,\partial W)
.
It is well known that
\Char(W)=\{\,c_{1}(\mathfrak{s}):\mathfrak{s}\in\Spin^{c}(W)\,\}
.

Suppose
(W,S)
is a link cobordism from
\emptyset
to
(\mathbb{S}^{3},K)
, with

b_{1}(W)=b_{2}^{+}(W)=0
. For
C\in\Char(W)
, let

H_{t}(W,[S],C):=\frac{C^{2}+b_{2}(W)-2t\langle\,C,[S]\,\rangle+2t[S]\cdot[S]}{4},

where
[S]\in H_{2}(W)\cong H_{2}(W,\partial W)
. Furthermore, for
\mathfrak{s}\in\Spin^{c}(W)
, we write

H_{t}(W,[S],\mathfrak{s}):=H_{t}(W,[S],c_{1}(\mathfrak{s})).

If
t\in[0,2]
, we define the
M
-degree
of
(W,S)
to be the function

M_{(W,S)}(t):=\min_{C\in\Char(W)}-H_{t}(W,[S],C)+t\cdot g(S).

Definition 7.3
.

Suppose that
K
is a knot in
\mathbb{S}^{3}
, and
S
,
S^{\prime}\in\Surf(K)
.
We say that
\vec{\mathcal{S}}=\{\mathcal{S}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{S}_{n}\}
is a

generalized stabilization sequence
from
S
to
S^{\prime}
if the
following hold:

(1)

Each
\mathcal{S}_{i}=(W_{i},S_{i})
is a link cobordism bounding
(\mathbb{S}^{3},K)
,
such that
S_{i}
is connected and
b_{1}(W_{i})=b_{2}^{+}(W_{i})=0
.

(2)

\mathcal{S}_{1}=(B^{4},S)
and
\mathcal{S}_{n}=(B^{4},S^{\prime})
.

(3)

Up to diffeomorphism fixing
\mathbb{S}^{3}
pointwise,

\mathcal{S}_{i+1}
is obtained from
\mathcal{S}_{i}
via a generalized
stabilization or destabilization.

We write
P_{\st}(S,S^{\prime})
for the set of stabilization sequences
connecting
S
and
S^{\prime}
.

Definition 7.4
.

Let
K
be a knot in
\mathbb{S}^{3}
and suppose
S
,
S^{\prime}\in\Surf(K)
.
Given a stabilization sequence

\vec{\mathcal{S}}=\{\mathcal{S}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{S}_{n}\}
from
S
to
S^{\prime}
,
we define the
M
-degree
of the sequence
\vec{\mathcal{S}}
to be the function

M_{\vec{\mathcal{S}}}(t):=\max_{1\leq i\leq n}M_{(W_{i},S_{i})}(t).

Furthermore, the
M
-distance
of
S
and
S^{\prime}
is the function

M_{(S,S^{\prime})}\colon[0,2]\to\mathbb{R}^{\geq 0}
defined by

M_{(S,S^{\prime})}(t):=\min_{\vec{\mathcal{S}}\in P_{\st}(S,S^{\prime})}M_{\vec{\mathcal{S}}}(t).

For each
t\in[0,2]
, the quantity
M_{(S,S^{\prime})}(t)
is a metric filtration on
\Surf(K)
.

7.2.
The
M
-metric and
\Upsilon

In this section, we prove the following:

Theorem 7.5
.

If
K
is a knot in
\mathbb{S}^{3}
and
S
,
S^{\prime}\in\Surf(K)
, then

\Upsilon_{(S,S^{\prime})}(t)\leq M_{(S,S^{\prime})}(t).

The proof of Theorem
7.5
is similar to the proof of
Theorem
5.13
, our bound on
\tau
.
It is convenient to introduce the following notation.
Suppose
(W,\mathcal{F})\colon(Y_{1},\mathbb{L}_{1})\to(Y_{2},\mathbb{L}_{2})
is a decorated link cobordism,
and
\mathfrak{s}\in\Spin^{c}(W)
. Write
\mathcal{F}=(S,\mathcal{A})
.
If
\mathfrak{s}|_{Y_{i}}
is torsion and
\mathbb{L}_{i}=(L_{i},\bm{\mathbf{w}}_{i},\bm{\mathbf{z}}_{i})

is null-homologous for
i\in\{1,2\}
, we define the quantities

G_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}(W,\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{s}):=\frac{c_{1}(\mathfrak{s})^{2}-2\chi(W)-3\sigma(W)}{4}+\chi(S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}})-\frac{1}{2}(|\bm{\mathbf{w}}_{1}|+|\bm{\mathbf{w}}_{2}|)

and

G_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(W,\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{s}):=\frac{c_{1}(\mathfrak{s}-\mathit{PD}[S])^{2}-2\chi(W)-3\sigma(W)}{4}+\chi(S_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}})-\frac{1}{2}(|\bm{\mathbf{z}}_{1}|+|\bm{\mathbf{z}}_{2}|).

For
t\in[0,2]
, we define

G_{t}(W,\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{s}):=\left(1-\frac{t}{2}\right)\cdot G_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}(W,\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{s})+\frac{t}{2}\cdot G_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(W,\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{s}).

In the case when
(W,\mathcal{F})
is a cobordism from
\emptyset
to
(\mathbb{S}^{3},\mathbb{K})

with
b_{2}^{+}(W)=b_{1}(W)=0
, and the dividing set
\mathcal{A}
consists of a single arc
that divides
S
into two components, we have

G_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}(W,\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{s})=\frac{c_{1}(\mathfrak{s})^{2}+b_{2}(W)}{4}-2g(S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}})\text{ and }G_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(W,\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{s})=\frac{c_{1}(\mathfrak{s}-\mathit{PD}[S])^{2}+b_{2}(W)}{4}-2g(S_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}).

In this situation, we also have

(7.1)

G_{t}(W,\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{s})=H_{t}(W,[S],\mathfrak{s})-(2-t)\cdot g(S_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}})-t\cdot g(S_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}).

We now compute the effect of a generalized stabilization;
cf. Lemma
5.4
:

Lemma 7.6
.

Suppose that
(W,\mathcal{F})\colon(Y_{1},\mathbb{L}_{1})\to(Y_{2},\mathbb{L}_{2})
is a decorated link
cobordism with
b_{1}(W)=0
. Write
\mathcal{F}=(S,\mathcal{A})
. Suppose that
(W^{\prime},S^{\prime})
is a
generalized stabilization of
(W,S)
, obtained by cutting out
B^{4}\subseteq W

and gluing in a link cobordism
(X_{0},S_{0})
with
b_{1}(X_{0})=b_{2}^{+}(X_{0})=0
,
as in Definition
7.1
. Let
D_{1},\dots,D_{n}
denote
the components of
S\cap B^{4}
, and suppose that
\widehat{D}\subseteq S
is a disk
that contains each of
D_{1},\dots,D_{n}
, and intersects
\mathcal{A}
in a single arc. Define

S_{0}^{\prime}:=(\widehat{D}\setminus(D_{1}\cup\cdots\cup D_{n}))\cup S_{0},

and suppose that
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is a dividing set on
S^{\prime}
that intersects
S_{0}^{\prime}
in
a single arc, and agrees with
\mathcal{A}
outside
\widehat{D}
. Write

\mathcal{F}^{\prime}=(S^{\prime},\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
. If
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}\in\Spin^{c}(W^{\prime})
agrees with
\mathfrak{s}\in\Spin^{c}(W)

on
W\setminus B^{4}
, then

F_{W^{\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}}\simeq U^{-d_{1}/2}V^{-d_{2}/2}\cdot F_{W,\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{s}},

where
d_{1}:=G_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}(W^{\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})-G_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}(W,\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{s})
and

d_{2}:=G_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(W^{\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})-G_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(W,\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{s})
.

Proof.

Let
\widehat{D}_{0}
denote the punctured disk
\widehat{D}\setminus B^{4}
.
We write
N(\widehat{D}_{0})
for the total space of the unit normal disk bundle of

\widehat{D}_{0}
in
W\setminus B^{4}
, and let

W_{0}:=N(\widehat{D}_{0})\cup B^{4}\text{ and }W_{0}^{\prime}:=N(\widehat{D}_{0})\cup X_{0}.

Note that
W_{0}
and
W_{0}^{\prime}
are topologically obtained from
B^{4}
and
X_{0}
, respectively,
by attaching a collection of 4-dimensional 1-handles. Write
Y:=\partial W_{0}=\partial W_{0}^{\prime}
.
We can view
(W_{0},\widehat{D})
and
(W_{0}^{\prime},S_{0}^{\prime})
as undecorated link cobordisms
from the empty set to the knot

K:=\partial\widehat{D}\times\{0\}=S^{\prime}\cap Y\subseteq N(\widehat{D}_{0})

in
Y
. As in Lemma
5.4
, the knot
K
is an unknot in
Y
,
since we can explicitly construct a Seifert disk
D_{K}
.
Let us write
\mathcal{F}_{0}=(\widehat{D},\mathcal{A})
and
\mathcal{F}_{0}^{\prime}=(S^{\prime}_{0},\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
.

Suppose
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{s}\#\mathfrak{t}
, for
\mathfrak{t}\in\Spin^{c}(X_{0}).

Consider the quantity

h(S_{0}^{\prime}\cup D_{K},\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}):=\frac{\langle\,c_{1}(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}),[S_{0}^{\prime}\cup D_{K}]\,\rangle-[S_{0}^{\prime}\cup D_{K}]\cdot[S_{0}^{\prime}\cup D_{K}]}{2}.

If
h(S_{0}^{\prime}\cup D_{K},\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})+g(S_{0,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{\prime})-g(S_{0,\bm{\mathbf{w}}}^{\prime})\geq 0
,
then, according to Lemma
5.3
,

(7.2)

F_{W_{0}^{\prime},\mathcal{F}_{0}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}|_{W_{0}^{\prime}}}\simeq U^{g(S_{0,\bm{\mathbf{w}}}^{\prime})}V^{g(S_{0,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{\prime})+h(S_{0}^{\prime}\cup D_{K},\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})}\cdot(F_{W_{0}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}|_{W_{0}^{\prime}}}\otimes\mathbb{F}[U,V])\mod H_{1}(Y),

while

(7.3)

F_{W_{0},\mathcal{F}_{0},\mathfrak{s}|_{W_{0}}}\simeq(F_{W_{0},\mathfrak{s}|_{W_{0}}}\otimes\mathbb{F}[U,V])\mod H_{1}(Y).

Up to diffeomorphism, we can write
W_{0}
and
W_{0}^{\prime}
as the compositions

W_{0}\cong W_{1}\circ B^{4}\text{ and }W_{0}^{\prime}\cong W_{1}\circ X_{0},

where
W_{1}\cong(I\times\mathbb{S}^{3})\cup N(\widehat{D}_{0})
.
We note
W_{1}
is a 1-handle cobordism.

The map

F_{X_{0},\mathfrak{t}}\colon\mathit{HF}^{-}(\mathbb{S}^{3})\to\mathit{HF}^{-}(\mathbb{S}^{3})

is an injection since
b_{1}(X_{0})=b_{2}^{+}(X_{0})=0
, by the proof of
[
35
]
*Theorem 9.1.
The map
F_{X_{0},\mathfrak{t}}
has grading

d:=\frac{c_{1}(\mathfrak{t})^{2}+b_{2}(X_{0})}{4},

and hence must be chain homotopic to multiplication by
\widehat{U}^{-d/2}
. Hence

(7.4)

F_{W_{0}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}|_{W_{0}^{\prime}}}\simeq F_{W_{1},\mathfrak{s}|_{W_{1}}}\circ F_{X_{0},\mathfrak{t}}\simeq\widehat{U}^{-d/2}\cdot F_{W_{1},\mathfrak{s}|_{W_{1}}}\circ F_{B^{4},\mathfrak{s}|_{B^{4}}}\simeq\widehat{U}^{-d/2}\cdot F_{W_{0},\mathfrak{s}|_{W_{0}}}.

Write
W_{2}:=W\setminus W_{1}
. The inclusion
H_{1}(Y)\to H_{1}(W_{2})/\Tors
is trivial,
since
Y
is the boundary of a 4-dimensional 1-handlebody in
W
.
Similarly, the coboundary maps
H^{1}(Y)\to H^{2}(W)
and
H^{1}(Y)\to H^{2}(W^{\prime})
are both trivial.
Hence, combining equations (
7.2
), (
7.3
), and (
7.4
),
and using the
\Spin^{c}
composition law, we conclude that

F_{W^{\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}}\simeq U^{-d/2+g(S_{0,\bm{\mathbf{w}}}^{\prime})}V^{-d/2+h(S_{0}^{\prime}\cup D_{K},\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})+g(S_{0,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{\prime})}\cdot F_{W,\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{s}}.

It is easy to see that

-\frac{d}{2}+g(S_{0,\bm{\mathbf{w}}}^{\prime})=-\frac{1}{2}\left(G_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}(W^{\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})-G_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}(W,\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{s})\right)

and

-\frac{d}{2}+h(S_{0}^{\prime}\cup D_{K},\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})+g(S_{0,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{\prime})=-\frac{1}{2}\left(G_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(W^{\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})-G_{\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(W,\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{s})\right),

which completes the proof in the case when
h(S_{0}^{\prime}\cup D_{K},\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})+g(S_{0,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{\prime})-g(S_{0,\bm{\mathbf{w}}}^{\prime})\geq 0
.

The proof when
h(S_{0}^{\prime}\cup D_{K},\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})+g(S_{0,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{\prime})-g(S_{0,\bm{\mathbf{w}}}^{\prime})\leq 0
is an easy modification,
using the corresponding subcase of Lemma
5.3
.
∎

Lemma
7.6
also immediately computes
the effect of a generalized stabilization on the
t
-modified
versions of the link cobordism maps:

Corollary 7.7
.

If
(W^{\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime})

is a generalized stabilization of
(W,\mathcal{F})
and
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}\in\Spin^{c}(W^{\prime})

restricts to
\mathfrak{s}
on
W\setminus B^{4}
, then

\mathit{tF}_{W^{\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}}\simeq v^{-G_{t}(W^{\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})+G_{t}(W,\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{s})}\cdot\mathit{tF}_{W,\mathcal{F},\mathfrak{s}}.

We can now prove Theorem
7.5
:

Proof of Theorem
7.5
.

Fix
t\in[0,2]
. Suppose
\vec{\mathcal{S}}=\{\mathcal{S}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{S}_{n}\}
is a
stabilization sequence connecting
(B^{4},S)
and
(B^{4},S^{\prime})
, and write

\mathcal{S}_{i}=(W_{i},S_{i})
. Decorate each
S_{i}
with a dividing set
\mathcal{A}_{i}
consisting
of a single arc, such that the type-
\bm{\mathbf{w}}
subregion has genus 0, and the
type-
\bm{\mathbf{z}}
subregion has genus
g(S_{i})
. We can assume the dividing arc is
very near to the knot
K
, and the the type-
\bm{\mathbf{w}}

subregion is unaffected by any of the stabilizations.
Write
\mathcal{F}_{i}=(S_{i},\mathcal{A}_{i})
.

Let us call a sequence
\vec{\mathfrak{s}}=\{\mathfrak{s}_{1},\dots,\mathfrak{s}_{n}\}
of
\Spin^{c}
structures on

W_{1},\dots,W_{n}
, respectively, a
stabilization sequence
if,
whenever
(W_{i+1},S_{i+1})
is obtained by stabilizing

(W_{i},S_{i})
with the negative definite link cobordism
(X_{0},S_{0})
, the

\Spin^{c}
structure
\mathfrak{s}_{i+1}
can be written as
\mathfrak{s}_{i}\#\mathfrak{t}_{i}
for

\mathfrak{t}_{i}\in\Spin^{c}(X_{0})
. We require an analogous condition whenever
W_{i+1}
is
a generalized destabilization of
W_{i}
. We define

M_{\vec{\mathcal{S}},\vec{\mathfrak{s}}}(t):=\max_{1\leq i\leq n}-G_{t}(W_{i},\mathcal{F}_{i},\mathfrak{s}_{i})=\max_{1\leq i\leq n}-H_{t}(W_{i},[S_{i}],\mathfrak{s}_{i})+t\cdot g(S_{i}),

where the second equality follows from equation (
7.1
) and the fact that
g(S_{i,\bm{\mathbf{w}}})=0

and
g(S_{i,\bm{\mathbf{z}}})=g(S_{i})
.

By Corollary
7.7
,
if
\vec{\mathfrak{s}}=\{\mathfrak{s}_{1},\dots,\mathfrak{s}_{n}\}
is a
stabilization sequence of
\Spin^{c}
structures on
W_{1},\dots,W_{n}
,
then all of the elements

v^{M_{\vec{\mathcal{S}},\vec{\mathfrak{s}}}(t)+G_{t}(W_{i},\mathcal{F}_{i},\mathfrak{s}_{i})}\cdot[\mathit{tF}_{W_{i},\mathcal{F}_{i},\mathfrak{s}_{i}}(1)]

coincide in
\mathit{tHFK}^{-}(\mathbb{K})
. In particular,

(7.5)

v^{M_{\vec{\mathcal{S}},\vec{\mathfrak{s}}}(t)-t\cdot g(S)}\cdot[\mathit{tF}_{S,\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(1)]=v^{M_{\vec{\mathcal{S}},\vec{\mathfrak{s}}}(t)-t\cdot g(S^{\prime})}\cdot[\mathit{tF}_{S^{\prime},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}(1)],

as
G_{t}(W_{1},\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathfrak{s}_{1})=-t\cdot g(S)
and
G_{t}(W_{n},\mathcal{F}_{n},\mathfrak{s}_{n})=-t\cdot g(S^{\prime})
.

Suppose that
(W_{i\pm 1},S_{i\pm 1})
is obtained by stabilizing
(W_{i},S_{i})
using
the negative definite link cobordism
(X_{0},S_{0})
, and

\mathfrak{s}_{i}\in\Spin^{c}(W_{i})
and
\mathfrak{t}_{i}\in\Spin^{c}(X_{0})
. Then

H_{t}(W_{i}\#X_{0},[S_{i}]+[S_{0}],\mathfrak{s}_{i}\#\mathfrak{t}_{i})=H_{t}(W_{i},[S_{i}],\mathfrak{s}_{i})+H_{t}(X_{0},[S_{0}],\mathfrak{t}_{i}).

Hence, the
\Spin^{c}
structure
\mathfrak{s}_{i}\#\mathfrak{t}_{i}
minimizes

-H_{t}(W_{i\pm 1},[S_{i\pm 1}],\mathfrak{s}_{i}\#\mathfrak{t}_{i})
for a fixed
t
if and
only if
\mathfrak{s}_{i}
minimizes
-H_{t}(W_{i},[S_{i}],\mathfrak{s}_{i})
and
\mathfrak{t}_{i}
minimizes
-H_{t}(X_{0},[S_{0}],\mathfrak{t}_{i})
.
It follows that we can always construct a stabilization sequence of
\Spin^{c}
structures

\vec{\mathfrak{s}}=\{\mathfrak{s}_{1},\dots,\mathfrak{s}_{n}\}
such that

-H_{t}(W_{i},[S_{i}],\mathfrak{s}_{i})=\min_{\mathfrak{s}\in\Spin^{c}(W_{i})}-H_{t}(W_{i},[S_{i}],\mathfrak{s})=M_{(W_{i},S_{i})}(t)-t\cdot g(S_{i}).

Then

(7.6)

M_{\vec{\mathcal{S}},\vec{\mathfrak{s}}}(t)=\max_{1\leq i\leq n}-H_{t}(W_{i},[S_{i}],\mathfrak{s}_{i})+t\cdot g(S_{i})=\max_{1\leq i\leq n}M_{(W_{i},S_{i})}(t)=M_{\vec{\mathcal{S}}}(t).

Combining equations (
7.5
)
and (
7.6
), we conclude that

(7.7)
