*Section 5.

Proof.

This follows from the trace formula in Theorem
9.1
, using the same argument as the proof of

[
21
, Theorem 5.1]
.
∎

We now prove the following:

Proposition 10.1
.

Let
D_{K,\id}
and
D_{K,r}
be the canonical and the 1-roll-spun slice disks of
-K\#K
, respectively.
Then

\tau(D_{K,\id},D_{K,r})\leq 1.

Proof.

Let
w
and
z
be basepoints on
K
, and write
\mathbb{K}=(K,w,z)
and
-\mathbb{K}\#\mathbb{K}=(-K\#K,w,z)
.
By Lemma
4.6
, we can calculate

\tau(D_{K,\id},D_{K,r})
using
\mathit{HFK}^{-}_{V=0}(-\mathbb{K}\#\mathbb{K})
. By Theorem
1.2
,

(10.1)

E\circ t_{D_{K,\id}}^{-}\simeq\cotr\in\Hom\left(\mathbb{F}_{2}[U],\mathit{CFK}^{-}_{V=0}(\mathbb{K})^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{2}[U]}\mathit{CFK}^{-}_{V=0}(\mathbb{K})\right).

Furthermore,
r_{*}\simeq\id+\Phi_{w}\circ\Psi_{z}
by
[
54
]
*Theorem B, so

(10.2)

E\circ t_{D_{K,r}}^{-}\simeq(\id\otimes(\id+\Phi_{w}\circ\Psi_{z}))\circ\cotr.

Hence, if we can show that
U\cdot\Phi_{w}\circ\Psi_{z}
is
U
-equivariantly chain homotopic
to zero, then

U\cdot t_{D_{K,\id}}^{-}\simeq U\cdot t_{D_{K,r}}^{-},

so
\tau(D_{K,\id},D_{K,r})\leq 1
. We note that
\Phi_{w}
has a simple algebraic
interpretation on
\mathit{CFK}^{-}_{V=0}(\mathbb{K})
. It is the map obtained by writing the
differential as a matrix with entries in
\mathbb{F}_{2}[U]
, and then differentiating
each entry; cf. equation (
3.1
).
According to
[
12
]
*Proposition 6.3, since

\mathit{CFK}^{-}_{V=0}(\mathbb{K})
is a finitely generated, free,
\mathbb{Z}
-graded chain complex over
\mathbb{F}_{2}[U]
,
the map
U\cdot\Phi_{w}
is
U
-equivariantly chain homotopic to
zero, and hence so is
U\cdot\Phi_{w}\circ\Psi_{z}
. The claim follows.
∎

Question 10.2
.

In light of Proposition
10.1
, it is natural to ask whether

\mu_{\st}(D_{K,\id},D_{K,r})\leq 1
for any roll-spun slice disks
D_{K,\id}
and
D_{K,r}
;
cf. Conjecture
2.22
.
This would give a topological proof of Proposition
10.1
by Theorem
5.13
.

10.2.
Computational examples

In this section, we compute the invariants
\tau
,
V_{k}
,
and
\Upsilon
for several pairs of deform-spun slice disks. We begin by considering the
complex
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{\infty}(4_{1})
for the figure-eight knot
4_{1}
, which
is shown in Figure
10.1
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{\infty}(4_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{4}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}},A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-1,-1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}

Figure 10.1.
The complex
\mathcal{C\!F\!L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{3},4_{1})
.

Lemma 10.3
.

Let
K
be the figure-eight knot,
and let
D_{K,\id}
and
D_{K,r}
denote the canonical and the 1-roll-spun slice disk
slice disks of
-4_{1}\#4_{1}
. Then

\tau(D_{K,\id},D_{K,r})=1,\text{ }V_{0}(D_{K,\id},D_{K,r})=1\text{, and }V_{1}(D_{K,\id},D_{K,r})=0.

Proof.

The Alexander filtered chain complex
\widehat{\mathit{CFK}}{}^{\mathit{fil}}(4_{1})
, obtained by setting
U=0
and
V=1
,
has the form

\widehat{\mathit{CFK}}{}^{\mathit{fil}}(4_{1})=\big((\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0})_{1}\longrightarrow(\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{1})_{0}\qquad(\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{2})_{0}\qquad(\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{3})_{0}\longrightarrow(\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{4})_{-1}\big).

The notation
(\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{i})_{j}
means the intersection points
\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{i}
,
which has Alexander grading
j
. Using equations (
3.1
) and (
3.2
),

(\Phi_{w}\circ\Psi_{z})(\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{3})=\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{1},

and
\Phi_{w}\circ\Psi_{z}
vanishes on all other generators. The complex

\widehat{\mathit{CFK}}{}^{\mathit{fil}}(-4_{1})
is obtained by dualizing
\widehat{\mathit{CFK}}{}^{\mathit{fil}}(4_{1})
(note that, although
4_{1}
is
amphichiral, to compute the trace formula, it is better to ignore this fact). Hence

\widehat{\mathit{CFK}}{}^{\mathit{fil}}(-4_{1})=\big((\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0}^{\vee})_{-1}\longleftarrow(\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{1}^{\vee})_{0}\qquad(\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{2}^{\vee})_{0}\qquad(\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{3}^{\vee})_{0}\longleftarrow(\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{4}^{\vee})_{1}\big).

Using equations (
10.1
) and (
10.2
),

E(\widehat{t}_{D_{K,r}}(1)-\widehat{t}_{D_{K,\id}}(1))=(\id\otimes(\Phi_{w}\circ\Psi_{z}))\circ\cotr(1)=\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{3}^{\vee}\otimes(\Phi_{w}\circ\Psi_{z})(\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{3})=\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{3}^{\vee}\otimes\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{1}.

We now observe that
\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{3}^{\vee}\otimes\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{1}
is nonzero in the homology
of the 0-filtration level

\mathcal{G}_{0}\left(\widehat{\mathit{CFK}}{}^{\mathit{fil}}(-4_{1})\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}\widehat{\mathit{CFK}}{}^{\mathit{fil}}(4_{1})\right),

where
\mathcal{G}_{i}
denotes Alexander filtration level
i
.
Indeed, the only elements mapped to
\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{3}^{\vee}\otimes\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{1}

by the differential are
\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{4}^{\vee}\otimes\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{1}

and
\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{3}^{\vee}\otimes\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0}
. However, neither of these are in
\mathcal{G}_{0}
;
instead, they are in
\mathcal{G}_{1}
. Hence
\tau(D_{K,\id},D_{K,r})=1
.

We now consider the invariants
V_{0}
and
V_{1}
. The complex
A_{0}^{-}(-4_{1}\#4_{1})

has 25 generators over
\mathbb{F}_{2}[\widehat{U}]
. The generators are the
monomials
U^{n}V^{m}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{i}^{\vee}\otimes\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{j}
, where
n
,
m\geq 0
, and

(10.3)

A(\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{i}^{\vee})+A(\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{j})+m-n=0.

Similarly,
A_{1}^{-}(-4_{1}\#4_{1})
is generated by the monomials

U^{n}V^{m}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{i}^{\vee}\otimes\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{j}
, where
n\geq 0
,
m\geq-1
,
and satisfy equation (
10.3
).

As above, we can identify
t^{-}_{D_{K,r}}(1)-t^{-}_{D_{K,\id}}(1)

with
U^{0}V^{0}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{3}^{\vee}\otimes\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{1}
. It is straightforward to see that

U^{0}V^{0}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{3}^{\vee}\otimes\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{1}
is not a boundary in
A_{0}^{-}(-4_{1}\#4_{1})
,
so
V_{0}\geq 1
. However,

\begin{split}\partial(U^{1}V^{0}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{4}^{\vee}\otimes\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{1})&=U^{1}V^{1}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{3}^{\vee}\otimes\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{1}:=\widehat{U}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{3}^{\vee}\otimes\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{1}\text{ and }\\
\partial(U^{0}V^{-1}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{4}^{\vee}\otimes\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{1})&=\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{3}^{\vee}\otimes\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{1},\end{split}

implying that
V_{0}\leq 1
and
V_{1}=0
.
∎

Lemma 10.4
.

Let
K
denote the figure-eight knot, and let
D_{K,\id}
and
D_{K,r}
be
as in Lemma
10.3
. Then

\Upsilon_{(D_{K,\id},D_{K,r})}(t)
takes the form shown in
Figure
10.2
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to Lemma
10.3
. Therein, we computed
t_{D_{K,r}}^{-}(1)-t_{D_{K},\id}^{-}(1)
to be
\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{3}^{\vee}\otimes\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{1}\in\mathit{CFK}^{\infty}(4_{1})^{\vee}\otimes\mathit{CFK}^{\infty}(4_{1})
. The two elements
y_{1}=V^{-1}\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{4}^{\vee}\otimes\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{1}
and
y_{2}=U^{-1}\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{3}^{\vee}\otimes\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{0}
lie in homogeneous
(\gr_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}},A)
-grading
(-1,0)
. It is straightforward to check that for
t\in[0,1]
,
y_{1}
satisfies
\partial y_{1}=\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{3}^{\vee}\otimes\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{1}
and
y_{1}\in\mathcal{G}_{t}^{t}(\bar{4}_{1}\#4_{1})
. Furthermore, if
s<t
, then there are no elements
z\in\mathcal{G}_{s}^{t}(\bar{4}_{1}\#4_{1})
such that
\partial z=\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{3}^{\vee}\otimes\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{1}
. Similarly, if
t\in[1,2]
, then
\partial y_{2}=\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{3}^{\vee}\otimes\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{1}
and
y_{2}\in\mathcal{G}_{2-t}^{t}(\bar{4}_{1}\#4_{1})
, and there are no elements
z\in\mathcal{G}_{s}^{t}(\bar{4}_{1}\#4_{1})
such that
\partial z=\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{3}^{\vee}\otimes\bm{\mathbf{x}}_{1}
for
s<2-t
.
∎

Figure 10.2.
\Upsilon_{D_{4_{1},\id},D_{4_{1},r}}(t)
.

.

.

\omega\in\{\mu_{\st},\mu_{\Sing}\}

10.3.
Slice disks with large stabilization distance

We now prove Theorem
1.3
of the introduction.

Theorem 10.7
.

Given
n\geq 0
, there is a knot
K_{n}
and a pair of slice disks
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
for
K_{n}
such that
\tau(D_{1},D_{2})\geq n
.

If
K
is a knot in
S^{3}
, consider the
V
-torsion order
of
\mathit{HFK}^{-}_{U=0}(K)
, for which we write
\Tor_{V}(K)
. This is the minimal
n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
such that

V^{n}\cdot\Tor(\mathit{HFK}^{-}_{U=0}(K))=\{0\}.

See
[
1
]
and
[
18
]
for examples of applications of the torsion order in knot Floer homology.

Let
K
be a knot in
S^{3}
, and consider the slice knot
J=K_{1}\#K_{2}\#\bar{K}_{3}\#\bar{K}_{4}
, where each
K_{i}
denotes a copy of
K
. We define two slice disks for
J
, which are boundary connected sums of slice disks for pairs of summands, as follows. Let
D_{1}
be the spun slice disk obtained by viewing
J
as
(K_{1}\#\bar{K}_{3})\#(K_{2}\#\bar{K}_{4})
and taking the boundary connected sum of the Artin spun slice disks for
K_{1}\#\bar{K}_{3}
and
K_{2}\#\bar{K}_{4}
, and let
D_{2}
be the spun slice disks obtained by viewing
J
as
(K_{1}\#\bar{K}_{4})\#(K_{2}\#\bar{K}_{3})
, and taking a similar boundary connected sum.

Lemma 10.8
.

If
K
is a knot and
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
are the slice disks for
J=K\#K\#\bar{K}\#\bar{K}
described above, then

\tau(D_{1},D_{2})=\Tor_{V}(K).

Proof.

Firstly, note that the connected sum formula and the duality formula for mirroring knots implies that
\Tor_{V}(L)=\Tor_{V}(-L)
, and also
\Tor_{V}(L\#M)=\max(\Tor_{V}(L),\Tor_{V}(M))
for any knots
L
and
M
. In particular
\Tor_{V}(J)=\Tor_{V}(K)
.

We claim firstly that

(10.4)

\tau(D_{1},D_{2})\leq\Tor_{V}(K).

This follows from algebraic considerations. Indeed,
[t_{D_{1}}^{-}(1)]=[t_{D_{2}}^{-}(1)]+\sigma
, where
\sigma\in\mathit{HFK}^{-}_{U=0}(J)
is
V
-torsion. Hence, if
n=\Tor_{V}(K)
, then
V^{n}\cdot\sigma=0
, so

V^{n}\cdot[t_{D_{1}}^{-}]=V^{n}\cdot[t_{D_{2}}].

This establishes equation (
10.4
).

To establish the reverse inequality of equation (
10.4
), we argue as follows. Consider the connected sum decomposition of
J
as
(K_{1}\#\bar{K}_{3})\#(K_{2}\#\bar{K}_{4})
. The corresponding 2-sphere gives a pair-of-pants cobordism from
(S^{3},J)
to
(S^{3},K\#\bar{K})\sqcup(S^{3},K\#\bar{K})
. Denote the cobordism map by
F
. By composing
t_{D_{1}}^{-}
and
t_{D_{2}}^{-}
with
F
, we may view the induced elements as chain maps

T_{1},T_{2}\in\Hom_{\mathbb{F}[V]}(\mathit{CFK}^{-}_{U=0}(K\#\bar{K}),\mathit{CFK}^{-}_{U=0}(K\#\bar{K})).

Since
F
is a homotopy equivalence,

V^{n}\cdot[t^{-}_{D_{1}}]=V^{n}\cdot[t^{-}_{D_{1}}]

if and only if
V^{n}\cdot[F(t^{-}_{D_{1}})]=V^{n}\cdot[F(t^{-}_{D_{1}})]
, which in turn occurs if and only if
V^{n}\cdot T_{1}\simeq V^{n}\cdot T_{2}
, where
\simeq
denotes
\mathbb{F}[V]
-equivariant chain homotopy.

The maps
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
may be identified with concordance maps for concordances from
K\#\bar{K}
to
K\#\bar{K}
. The map
T_{1}
is identified with the identity map
\id
. On the other hand, the map
T_{2}
is the concordance map for a concordance which factors through the unknot. In particular,
T_{2}
must annihilate all torsion, as
\Tor_{V}(\mathrm{Unknot})=0
. In particular, if
V^{n}\cdot\id\simeq V^{n}\cdot T_{2}
, then
n
must be larger than the torsion order of
K\#\bar{K}
.
∎

We now prove Theorem
10.7
:

Proof of Theorem
10.7
.

It suffices to construct knots where
\Tor_{V}(K)\geq n
. This is straightforward. For example,
\Tor_{V}(T_{p,q})=\min(p,q)-1
. (This fact is well known, but a proof may be found in
[
18
]
*Lemma 5.3).
∎

11.
The cobordism distance

In this section, we consider the following notion of distance between two surfaces:

Definition 11.1
.

Suppose that
g\in\mathbb{N}
, and
S
,
S^{\prime}
are two slice surfaces of a knot
K\subseteq S^{3}
. We say that
S
and
S^{\prime}
are
strictly
g
-cobordant
if there is a smoothly embedded, orientable 3-manifold
Y\subseteq I\times B^{4}
, such that the following are satisfied:

(1)

\partial Y=(I\times K)\cup-(\{0\}\times S)\cup(\{1\}\times S^{\prime})
.

(2)

Projection of
Y
onto
I
is Morse.

(3)

The sum of the genera of the components of each regular level set of
Y
at most
g
.

We write
\mu_{\Cob}(S,S^{\prime})
for the minimal
g
such that
S
and
S^{\prime}
are strictly
g
-cobordant. We call this quantity the
cobordism distance
of
S
and
S^{\prime}
. In the case of a 0-cobordism,
this coincides with the notion of a
0
-cobordism
introduced by Melvin
[
29
]
for 2-knots. He defined a
g
-cobordism
to be one where each component of every level set has genus at most
g
.

The main result of this section is that the invariant
\tau(D,D^{\prime})
gives a lower bound on
\mu_{\Cob}(D,D^{\prime})
for slice disks
D
and
D^{\prime}
.

Theorem 11.2
.

Suppose
D
and
D^{\prime}
are two slice disks of a knot
K
in
S^{3}
. Then

\tau(D,D^{\prime})\leq\mu_{\Cob}(D,D^{\prime}).

The main additional subtlety in the proof of Theorem
11.2
is that the level sets of a strict
g
-cobordism
Y
need not be connected, whereas the link cobordism maps vanish when there is a closed component. Hence, some care is required in the proof.

11.1.
Tubing disconnected surfaces

In this section, we describe a way of meaningfully assigning cobordism maps to disconnected surfaces by tubing the components together.

Definition 11.3
.

Suppose that
W
is a compact 4-manifold with boundary
Y
, and
S
is a properly embedded, orientable surface in
W
. Suppose further that
\partial S
is equal to a knot
K\subseteq Y
. A
tubing
of
S
is a properly embedded surface
\widehat{S}\subseteq W
with
\partial\widehat{S}=K
, obtained by attaching tubes to
S
which are the boundaries of 3-dimensional 1-handles in
W
. Furthermore, we assume
g(\widehat{S})=g(S)
and
\widehat{S}
is connected.

We now prove a local relation for the graph cobordism maps (cf.
[
59
]
*Lemma 6.2):

Lemma 11.4
.

The graph cobordism maps satisfy the relation shown on the bottom of Figure
11.1
.

Proof.

We begin with the bypass relation for the knot Floer cobordism maps, which is shown on the top of Figure
11.1
. We may take the underlying link cobordism to be
I\times K
, where
K
is an unknot. The bypass relation for the link cobordism maps is proven in
[
56
]
*Lemma 1.4. We then obtain the graph relation by considering the
V=1
reductions of the graph cobordism maps, following
[
58
]
*Theorem C.
∎

Figure 11.1.
The bypass relation, as well as an induced relation obtained by setting
V=1
, in terms of graph cobordisms.

Proposition 11.5
.

Suppose that
S
is a properly embedded, oriented surface in
B^{4}
with boundary equal to a knot
K
. Suppose that
\widehat{S}_{1}
and
\widehat{S}_{2}
are two tubings of
S
. Then

\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{\widehat{S}_{1},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}\simeq\bm{\mathbf{t}}_{\widehat{S}_{2},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}^{-}.

Proof.

Any tubing of
S
is isotopic to a tubing where each tube has one foot on the component of
S
containing
K
, and one foot on a closed component of
S
. Since tubes are boundaries of 3-dimensional 1-handles, we may assume that, after an isotopy, any two such tubings have disjoint tubes. In particular, it suffices to change tubes one at a time.

We assume that
T
and
T^{\prime}
are tubes which have their feet on the same components of
S
. We assume the feet of the tubes are very close, and we pick an open neighborhood of the two tubes which is diffeomorphic to
S^{1}\times B^{3}
. We can factor the two cobordism maps through
(S^{1}\times S^{2},O_{2})
, where
O_{2}
is a two-component unlink in
S^{1}\times S^{2}
.

We will prove the tube relation shown in Figure
11.2
. This tube relation may be proven by considering the
V=1
reduction of the link cobordism maps, and then applying the graph relation shown in Figure
11.1
. Since the link in
S^{1}\times S^{2}
is an unlink with 2 basepoints per component, the link cobordism maps are determined by the graph cobordism maps, and so it is sufficient to prove the analogous formula for the graph cobordism maps. We do this in Figure
11.3
, using the local relation from Lemma
11.4
,
which is shown in Figure
11.1
.

We now claim that the cobordism map for the right-most surface in Figure
11.2
factors through the
H_{1}
-action. This is proven as follows. The
V=1
reduction factors through the
H_{1}
-action by
[
55
]
*Proposition 4.6. Since the Alexander grading change of the link cobordism map is zero, Lemma
5.1
implies that the link cobordism itself factors through the
H_{1}
-action. In particular, once we compose this cobordism map for
S^{1}\times B^{3}
with the remainder of the cobordism map for the surface in
B^{4}
, we obtain the 0-map since
b_{1}(B^{4})=0
.

∎

Figure 11.2.
A relation involving tubes.

Figure 11.3.
A relation involving graphs.

11.2.
Proof of Theorem
11.2

Proof of Theorem
11.2
.

Suppose that
Y
is a strict
g
-cobordism between slice disks
D
and
D^{\prime}
of
K\subseteq S^{3}
. The projection from
Y
onto the factor
I
of
I\times B^{4}
is a Morse function, by assumption. We may understand this Morse function as determining a sequence of 3-dimensional handles attached to
\{0\}\times D
, which build the 3-manifold
Y
. These handles may be of any index in
\{0,1,2,3\}
. A 0-handle or 3-handle corresponds to adding or deleting an unknotted 2-sphere that is unlinked from the rest of the surface. Attaching a 1-handle corresponds to a 1-handle stabilization, while a 2-handle corresponds to 1-handle destabilization. Let us write
S_{1},\dots,S_{n}
for a sequence of surfaces induced by a strict
g
-cobordism. By definition

k=\max_{1\leq i\leq n}g(S_{i}),

where
g(S_{i})
is the sum of the genera of the components of
S_{i}
.

The surfaces
S_{1},\dots,S_{n}
will in general not be connected. Let
\widehat{S}_{i}
be any tubing of
S_{i}
for
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
. Note that, by definition,

g(\widehat{S}_{i})=g(S_{i})
. We decorate each
\widehat{S}_{i}
with a dividing set such that
(\widehat{S}_{i})_{\bm{\mathbf{w}}}
is a bigon. We write
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{i}
for this decorated surface.
Proposition
11.5
implies that the map
\bm{\mathbf{t}}^{-}_{\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{i},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}
is independent of the choice of tubing, and hence depends only on
S_{i}
.

If
S_{i}
is obtained from
S_{i-1}
by a 0-handle, then we can pick tubes so that
\widehat{S}_{i}
is isotopic to
\widehat{S}_{i-1}
. If
S_{i}
is obtained by a 3-handle, then, after changing tubes if necessary, the same is true. In particular,
\bm{\mathbf{t}}^{-}_{\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{i},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}=\bm{\mathbf{t}}^{-}_{\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{i-1},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}
if
S_{i}
is obtained from
S_{i-1}
by attaching a 0-handle or a 3-handle.

If
S_{i}
is obtained by attaching a 1-handle to
S_{i-1}
, then either
g(S_{i})=g(S_{i-1})
or
g(S_{i})=g(S_{i-1})+1
. In the first case, the 1-handle connects two different components of
S_{i-1}
, and consequently, after changing tubes if necessary,
\widehat{S}_{i}
and
\widehat{S}_{i-1}
are isotopic. In the second case,
\widehat{S}_{i}
is obtained by stabilizing
\widehat{S}_{i-1}
. We have the same conclusions for a 2-handle attachment, with the roles of
S_{i-1}
and
S_{i}
reversed. Consequently, using the formula in Lemma
5.4
for stabilization, the maps
V^{k}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{t}}^{-}_{\widehat{S}_{i},\bm{\mathbf{z}}}
coincide for all
i
. So
\tau(D,D^{\prime})\leq k
by Lemma
4.6
, which completes the proof.
∎
